This book is an amended, updated and expanded edition of the previously published The Mardzong 
Manuscripts: Preservation, Interpretation and Dating of an Archaeological Find in Mustang, Nepal that was 
published in 2018 by Eikon Publishing, Poland, in a limited edition.
The Project was funded by The National Science Centre, Poland, in the framework of the program “SONATA 
BIS 4”. Grant 2014/14/E/HS3/00770 entitled “The manuscripts from the Mardzong Caves in Mustang, Nepal: 
Interpretation, paper analysis and dating.” Principal Investigator: dr hab. Agnieszka Helman-Ważny.
Cover illustration: The second stage of cleaning and preliminary sorting of the folios before wrapping them 
in bundles. Photograph by Charles Ramble.

Preface
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
This book is about the Mardzong manuscripts, a collection found in the 
Caves of Upper Mustang near Lo Monthang in Nepal. These manuscripts 
are an extraordinary archaeological find, like the more archaic caches of Silk 
Road manuscripts from Central Asia, but they are still unknown. They are an 
information-dense repository of artefacts, history, and ideas spanning half 
a millennium. The collection was discovered in 2008 by a group of climbers 
led by writer and Himalaya expert Broughton Coburn and mountaineer Peter 
Athans in a cave complex called Mardzong, southeast of Lo Monthang, in 
Nepal’s Mustang District (Figs 0.1 and 0.2).1
1	 The expedition is documented in the National Geographic film “Secrets of Shangri-La” and 
information about the discovery is published in the Britannica Book of the Year 2010.
Figure 0.1	
 
The location of both 1) the 
Mardzong cave complex at 
the elevation 3877 m above 
sea level—the site where 
the manuscript collection 
was discovered—and 2) the 
Choede Monastery Antique 
Museum in Lo Monthang, 
where the Mardzong 
manuscripts collection is 
preserved now, on the map 
of Mustang (Lo) in Nepal
Map by Dorota Maya 
Helman, courtesy 
of the Mardzong 
Manuscripts Project

--- Page 10 of 332 ---
x
Preface
The find site resembles a scene from Star Wars, isolated as it is among the many 
caves hewn from a cliff facing the Upper Kali Gandaki River. The site is now 
entirely abandoned, the wind alone howling through the valley. It is located 
a half-hour horse-ride from Lo Monthang, the capital of the former kingdom 
of Mustang (Lo). Such an unusual number of sandstone sky-caves are a typi-
cal feature of the Mustang landscape. Most caves are now deserted, but in the 
past they were commonly used for housing, shrines and monasteries.2 Even 
today some of the other Mustang caves have been converted into modern 
dwellings and monasteries. The cave complex inside the Mardzong hill is thus 
likely to have been a settlement that was abandoned (Fig. 0.3). Our collection 
of manuscripts was found in one of the most beautiful so-called “cathedral” 
caves next to one decorated with paintings, where loose folios were scattered 
all around the open space of the cave. The place is now considered by local 
people, however, to be haunted, which is somehow reflected in the mood of its 
surroundings. The people of Mustang believe that it was the scene of a black 
magic battle between two powerful lamas. This may be one of the reasons for 
suspecting that texts hidden there were treated as if they might be a source of 
recondite knowledge.
That many miniatures have been cut out of book leaves, however, indicates 
that the collection had already been discovered before 2008. Local people 
would probably have long known that these caves hid a collection of old manu-
scripts, remarkable wall paintings and archaeological artefacts. Bearing all this 
2	 For the archaeology of caves, the settlement process and the formation of states in Mustang 
see Pohle and Haffner 2001.
Figure 0.2	 The Mardzong hill and its surroundings—the site of manuscripts found in 2008. Elevation 
3493 m above sea level
Photograph by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2016)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 11 of 332 ---
xi
Preface
in mind, the circumstances in which the cave complex was abandoned, and 
when this might have happened are unknown. The circumstances of its re-
discovery by Westerners are also unclear. We learn from the film Lost Caves of 
Mustang by Liesl Clark and Peter Athans that they were informed about “sky-
caves” by a local goatherd, and they were the first team since Mustang was 
opened to foreigners in 1991 to be granted an official 30-day permit to explore 
the “sky caves” of Upper Mustang. From that exploration they report many ar-
tefacts, such as paintings and manuscripts in other caves too. All wall paintings 
were executed in a similar style and dominated by the colours white, red and 
black (black used as contour). Unfortunately, they also reported many signs of 
looting in all these seemingly inaccessible places.
Whether the cave complex was a rock-hewn monastery, a shelter or a 
“burial site” for a number of libraries, apparently from settlements around 
Lo Monthang that were abandoned remains a mystery. The discovery team 
suggests that the Mustang caves may be a part of the “hidden valleys” of the 
Himalayas (which often contained so-called hidden treasure texts), thought to 
represent the Buddhist spiritual paradise known as Shambhala. These hidden 
valleys are believed to have been created at times of struggle, when Buddhism 
was threatened. Nagru Geshe Gelek Jinpa believes that manuscripts found in 
the Mardzong caves could originate from the ancient monastery of Bonkhor, 
Figure 0.3	 The Mardzong cave complex
Photograph by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2017)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 12 of 332 ---
xii
Preface
the ruins of which are located near Lo Monthang (Fig. 0.4).3 This idea is sup-
ported by a dedication folio found in the collection, which states explicitly that 
some of the manuscripts (including the Khams chen and the Klu ’bum) were 
produced there (see chapter 10).
The custom of abandoning old and damaged texts in stupas, shrines and 
possibly caves is known all across Tibet. There are many caves in this part of the 
Himalayas with scriptures and objects of religious cults, since people believe 
that having a damaged figure or book in their home gives rise to bad karma. 
Under normal circumstances Tibetans refrain from destroying religious texts, 
placing them on the floor or otherwise treating them with disrespect. If some-
thing is broken, people thus prefer to place it in another location rather than in 
their homes. It is therefore possible that the kings or nobility of Mustang aban-
doned the Bon and Buddhist sacred texts in the Mardzong caves out of respect, 
as an alternative to destroying them. This is one of our main hypotheses for 
the reason why this collection was deposited in the cave, if those books were 
seriously damaged before being stored in one of the Mardzong caves. The con-
dition of the manuscripts, however, suggest rather that they were not severely 
3	 He is a monk from the Triten Norbutse monastery who was also present when the manu-
scripts were taken out of cave. For an account of his trip see Nagru Geshe Gelek Jinpa 2013, 39.
Figure 0.4	 The ruins of Bonkhor located near Lo Monthang
Photograph courtesy of Charles Ramble

--- Page 13 of 332 ---
xiii
Preface
worn out. Most of the signs of serious damage, such as bird droppings and 
the sandy dust covering the folios, gaps in frontispieces caused by miniatures 
being cut out, possibly by looters, may date from more recent times, during the 
period when the books were stored in the crumbling cave (Figs 0.5 and 0.6). 
The missing miniatures from illuminated book leaves may, however, not neces-
sarily be an effect of looting by people who wanted to sell them on the art mar-
ket. Fidel Devkota reports that during his research in Dhe village, east of the 
Kali Gandaki, many villagers vividly recalled their childhoods, when they used 
to play with the manuscripts in a local cave called Ludakphu and in doing so 
destroyed them, especially folios with colourful lettering and images. Devkota 
reports that decades later villagers showed some of the folios from Ludakphu 
to gYung drung rgyal mtshan (the reincarnation of the Tibetan lama bsTan pa 
rgyal mtshan, now resident in Lubrak) who immediately identified them as 
belonging to a text related to Yungdrung Bon (gYung drung Bon), and took with 
him a small piece that is still kept safely in his protective amulet.4 As a result of 
such practices, we see the remaining mutilated pages. We cannot exclude the 
possibility that the same thing may have happened to the Mardzong collec-
tion. In some folios of the Mardzong manuscripts we also find careful repairs 
of paper tears (Figs 0.7 and 0.8).
4	 Devkota forthcoming.
Figures 0.5 and 0.6	
Visible damage on the Mardzong books leaves: folio fragment covered by bird 
droppings on the left, and an empty space left by a miniature cut out by looters 
on the right

--- Page 14 of 332 ---
xiv
Preface
These small signs of care, the sewn tears in the paper, cavities filled with pieces 
of paper from elsewhere, suggest rather that those books were painstakingly 
cared for, rather than being a possible source of bad karma. It is indeed a les-
son in impermanence to think about the cache of loose paper folios stored for 
centuries in the crumbling cave, slowly but inevitably disintegrating in spite of 
Mustang’s dry climate. Besides the activity of birds, looters, and the crumbling 
cave, many folios were found at the site, blown out of the cave by the wind.5 
All this may also suggest that the Mardzong collection was not stored in the 
remote cave as a respectful alternative to abandoning the books centuries ago, 
when they must still have been in good condition. At least that might not be 
the only reason. It is also possible that there were other reasons, for example 
that during the oppression of Bon people hid texts that might otherwise have 
been destroyed by Buddhists who wanted to subjugate them since most of the 
preserved material belongs to the Bon tradition. This does not, however, ex-
plain the presence of fragments of Buddhist works among the Mardzong vol-
umes. At this point we therefore have to accept that we do not know the reason 
why these particular books found themselves inside the Mardzong hill.
The discovery of the Mardzong cave complex was well publicised and docu-
mented by various film crews, but the subsequent fate of the Mardzong collec-
tion became unclear. Very little was done in terms of scholarly identification 
of these books after their discovery. Unfortunately, no professional preserva-
tion measures were undertaken to protect them. The preliminary identifica-
tion of the content of some volumes was undertaken by Charles Ramble, a 
member of the team that removed these manuscripts from the Mardzong cave 
in 2009. At that time, he estimated the collection at 25 000 manuscript folios 
5	 See chapter 6.
Figures 0.7 and 0.8	
The repairs of paper tears in the Mardzong folios
Photographs by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2017)

xv
Preface
originating from around 100 texts. Angela M. H. Schuster in the Britannica 
Book of the Year 2010, while reporting the archaeological discovery of the man-
uscript cache, more cautiously estimated their number at “more than 8 000 
folios belonging to some 30 religious tracts.”6 In reality, the result of our project 
count shows that there are 5 095 folios preserved in Choede monastery. This 
shows that the folios were not counted when discovered, or at the time they 
were moved out of the cave. Nobody therefore really knew what the collection 
contained or what its volume was. Both the pressure of time and the volume of 
material were responsible for such a situation.
As we see in the National Geographic film “Secrets of Shangri-La,” the texts 
were gathered into bundles by the climbers and lowered to the ground by 
rope. At the foot of the Mardzong hill a couple of monks gave them a prelimi-
nary dusting by the simple expedient of beating stacks of folios against rocks 
(Fig. 0.9). It was a rough way of cleaning the old manuscripts when it would 
have been very helpful to have shown a basic grasp of conservation measures. 
The monks then passed the bundles of folios on to a group inside a tent who 
removed some of the remaining feathers and dust with brushes, and sorted 
6	 Schuster 2010, 204.
Figure 0.9	 Monks giving the manuscripts folios a preliminary dusting by beating stacks of 
folios against rocks
Photograph by Charles Ramble (2008)

xvi
Preface
the material for the next step (Fig. 0.10). The superficially cleaned folios were 
then wrapped, as is customarily the case with Tibetan books, in new blue cot-
ton cloth purchased by the discovery team from a shop in Lo Monthang. A 
rough arrangement of folios into volumes was then attempted, and all the 
unidentified miscellaneous material was gathered into a single pile and simi-
larly wrapped up.7 The entire collection was finally packed in some twenty-five 
large bundles, and then transported up the hill to the Choede (Glo bo chos 
sde) monastery. As far as possible, the folios of all unidentified texts (exclud-
ing canonical volumes, judged after a cursory examination of the contents) 
were photographed by three people with cameras of varying quality. Due to 
the pressure of time the folios were not organised into orderly volumes, but a 
basic photographic record was kept of a large part of the material that was not 
immediately identifiable.
The photographs taken with the three cameras were later pooled and or-
ganised by Kemi Gurung, a member of our project team. He sorted the images 
into folders corresponding to different works. In all cases the texts were in-
complete, and not all folios were photographed. Charles Ramble reported that 
7	 Ramble, personal communication, May 17, 2017.

xvii
Preface
a list of all the illuminations found was made and given to Mohan Singh Lama 
of the Department of Archaeology in Kathmandu.8 The documentation of the 
manuscript folios done at that time, albeit incomplete, was crucial, especially 
in light of what has subsequently happened with the collection.
According to the official resolution of the local authorities, the collection 
was the property of the people of Lo Monthang and was now under the cura-
torship of Choede Monastery, where it was deposited for safe keeping. Bundles 
of manuscript folios are now kept in a small “antique museum” within the 
complex of Choede monastery, which itself is a sort of room filled with his-
torical objects as advertised on its notice by the Student Welfare Committee 
(Monastic School) (Figs 0.11, 0.12 and 0.13). The room is small and poorly lit, 
with a basic shelf construction added in 2017. A few books, masks, interiors 
of prayer wheels, wooden blocks and figures are exhibited in a rather random 
order. At the front doors we read that the museum contains “a collection of 
sacred Things from Mustang & Tibet.” The whole Mardzong collection, in bun-
dles wrapped in blue cotton, is stored on the windowsill against the wall on the 
window side, on the left when one enters the room. It was, however, impossible 
to obtain any information about these books, or about other objects, at the 
place where they are stored.
At the beginning the official status of the collection was uncertain. The site 
on which the hill and the Mardzong cave stand is under the jurisdiction of 
several different social groups in Lo Monthang, and access to the material had 
been complicated by discussions between the corresponding committees from 
the moment of discovery in 2008. A conflict which began when foreigners 
8	 Ramble, personal communication, May 17, 2017.

xviii
Preface
wanted to move the manuscript folios out of the cave may have been some-
how related to the subsequent fate of the collection, but there is no certainty 
about this. Despite the fact that the local youth group, headed by a young man, 
the owner of an art and antique gallery in Lo Monthang, vehemently objected 
to moving the collection out of the cave complex in 2008, in the end the vari-
ous committees of the town agreed that the best course of action would be to 
remove all the material from the caves, and to store it in the main monastery in 
the nearby town of Lo Monthang, Lo Choede, a proposal to which the manage-
ment of the Choede consented. From the point of view of preservation it was 
the most reasonable course of action.
Our project team re-visited the collection a couple of times between 2015 
and 2018. The earthquake in 2015 made our work enormously challenging at 
the beginning. Luckily, Lo Mothang was far enough from the epicentre of the 
earthquake, and buildings there suffered only minor damage. The general 
chaos and the time needed to restore the roads and the connection between 
Kathmandu and Upper Mustang, however, made the situation difficult in the 
region as a whole, since people first of all had to secure their living conditions. 
For this reason it was a bad time for scholarly research and we postponed our 
inventory work until 2016. During this fieldwork we realized that a part of the 
Figure 0.13	
Interior of the Choede monastery Antique Museum with the Mardzong 
volumes stored on the left

xix
Preface
collection that was supposed to be in Choede monastery was missing. We do 
not know what happened to those volumes, and for this reason some of the 
data collated here so far provide no real answers. I trust, however, that those 
facts, together with thorough documentation of everything that is left, may 
help to ascertain what happened and possibly even to locate the missing part. 
When we gather smaller puzzles together, they sometimes reveal a picture 
even if they are not yet all in place. We still sincerely hope that the missing 
part of the collection has simply been misplaced, and will soon be found lying 
forgotten somewhere in the monastery.

Preface to the 2nd Edition
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble
This is the second edition of a book devoted to the Mardzong manuscripts 
that were found in a cave complex in Upper Mustang near the town of Lo 
Monthang. Since the publication of the first edition, this important find has 
begun to attract increasing attention from both local and international com-
munities. There is also an ongoing discussion within the community in Lo 
Monthang about the prospect of making the Mardzong Cave complex a heri-
tage site; Choede monastery, where the manuscripts are being kept, is currently 
undergoing reconstruction and will soon have improved conditions in which 
to store the collection. The first edition of this book, entitled The Mardzong 
Manuscripts: Preservation, Interpretation and Dating of an Archaeological Find 
in Mustang, Nepal was published in 2018 by Eikon Publishing in Poland in a 
limited edition that is now out of print. The present volume is an amended and 
expanded edition, that has given us the opportunity to correct a number of er-
rors in the first edition and to add findings arising from our continuing research 
on the material. The preface to the first edition, by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, 
describes both the circumstances of the discovery of the Mardzong manu-
scripts and the present status of the collection in Lo Monthang. It is followed 
by glimpses from the project goals and progress during our already five years 
(from 2015) of conducting research on the Mardzong collection. This second 
edition, edited by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble, present-
ing additional results achieved by 2020. It contains eleven chapters written 
by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, Charles Ramble, Nyima Drandul Gurung, Naljor 
Tsering, Sarah Skumanov, Emilie Arnaud-Nguyen, Bazhen Zeren, a bibliogra-
phy and also an index, which the first edition did not have.
The first chapter Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and its heritage, by Agnieszka 
Helman-Ważny, presents the cultural and historical background to Mustang, 
its natural remoteness as a part of Northwest Nepal, and character of histori-
cal and geographical relations to its surrounding states and kingdoms. It intro-
duces the Lo kingdom, its rulers, and cultural links with Tibet, mentioning its 
significant role as a refugial territory for Bon followers.
The second chapter, A note on interdisciplinary methods, by Agnieszka 
Helman-Ważny, discusses the cross-disciplinary approach adopted to the 
Mardzong manuscripts as cultural heritage objects. It outlines the challenge 
of combining various methodologies applied to documents, dating and au-
thentication, as well as the preservation of important collections of ancient 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 21 of 332 ---
xxi
Preface to the 2nd Edition
manuscripts in Nepal. The study includes codicological, historical, philological 
and scientific analyses. Photography, video recording, categorizing, and collat-
ing particular folios according to distinctive attributes, such as: age and prov-
enance, utilized script and language, type of paper, its features, title, volume, 
page number, format is merged to serve a goal of making the collection known 
and accessible.
The third chapter, The Mardzong texts in relation to the Bon canon by Charles 
Ramble, Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, Nyima Drandul Gurung and Bazhen Zeren 
offers a basic account of the content of the Mardzong collection and the 
place of a selection of works within the Bon canon. It contains translated ex-
cerpts from selected colophons and manuscripts fragments, which included 
the names of scribes, authors, historical and religious figures, or patrons and 
funders of particular manuscripts. These shed a light on possible dating of the 
particular manuscripts, crucial for assessing the cultural and material value of 
the Mardzong collection.
The fourth chapter, by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, discusses the codicologi-
cal findings characterizing the particular texts produced in different times. 
This comparative study includes the conventions for page numbering, script 
and handwriting, illuminations and decorations, format and layout, as well as 
types of paper, ink, and paints. It is described in the context of techniques and 
technologies used in manuscript production.
The fifth chapter, by Emilie Arnaud-Nguyen and Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, 
describes paper fragments found in other archaeological sites in Mustang that 
have been dated by means of C14 analysis. This gives a context to the further 
study of materials and technologies used during the production of the manu-
scripts in the collection. The chapter provides information about the produc-
tion methods that were used and what can be learned from these findings.
The sixth chapter, by Charles Ramble, presents the missing texts, and in-
cludes the preliminary photographic documentation that was carried out at 
the moment of moving the collection from caves to Choede monastery. These 
hastily-executed photographs, taken in conditions that were far from ideal, are 
now the only existing record of the great majority of these items, since the 
bundle in which they were wrapped before being entrusted to Choede mon-
astery has been misplaced. The chapter includes a detailed list of the missing 
folios, together with their place within known collections of Bon and Buddhist 
texts, some of them very rare and a few apparently unique.
The seventh chapter, by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble, 
traces the provenance of the collection based on scientific and linguistic re-
search. It gives a record of possible scenarios of the reasons for hiding the col-
lection in one of the Mardzong caves, and approaches to dating.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 22 of 332 ---
xxii
Preface to the 2nd Edition
The eighth chapter, by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, focuses on Tibetan book-
making techniques, many of which are represented in the Mardzong volumes.
The ninth chapter, by Sarah Skumanov, discusses the preservation of the 
Mardzong collection in the wider perspective of contemporary Nepalese law 
and institutions responsible for archaeological heritage. It considers the pos-
sible scenarios designed for preventative conservation in such a challenging 
environment as Mustang.
The tenth chapter, by Charles Ramble, contains the translation of excerpts 
from a selection of manuscripts. Examples are carefully chosen to provide his-
torical information in addition to the text of canonical works, such as informa-
tion about patrons’ families, lineages of dynasties ruling at that time, masters 
and their religious schools, and other information which together provide a 
cultural background for the texts.
The eleventh and final chapter, by Charles Ramble and Naljor Tsering, is a 
new addition to the volume. It offers a discussion and annotated translation of 
Mardzong A, a fragmentary manuscript that may be the only known Tibetan 
manual for the preparation, administration and treatment of poisons.
To summarise: this second, expanded and improved, edition has given us 
the opportunity to correct a number of minor errors that appeared in the first 
edition and to incorporate the results of research on the Mardzong collection 
that we have carried out since its publication; the additional material includes 
extended treatments of some of the Bonpo canonical texts contained in the 
collection, notably two versions of the Klu ’bum (the “Hundred thousand 
nāgas”), and the gZer mig, the middle-length life of the Bonpos’ founder fig-
ure Shenrab Miwo (gShen rab mi bo). It also includes the transliteration and 
commentary of a treatise on toxicology purporting to be a Buddhist tantra, an 
incomplete work that appears to be unique.

Acknowledgements
This research was facilitated by the project entitled Manuscripts from 
the Mardzong Caves in Mustang, Nepal: Interpretation, Paper Analysis 
and Dating. The project, which ran from 2015–2018, was funded by The 
National Science Centre, Poland in the framework of the program “SONATA 
BIS 4”, Grant 2014/14/E/HS3/00770, and hosted by the Department of Books 
and Media History at the Faculty of Journalism, Information and Book Studies 
at the University of Warsaw, Poland.1 The photographs were taken by a team 
consisting of Kemi Gurung, Rajmund Radziewicz, Sarah Skumanov and 
Agnieszka Helman-Wazny during fieldwork in Mustang within the Mardzong 
Manuscripts Project 2015–18, and a team consisting of Kris Eriksson, Renan 
Ozturk, Nyima Drandul and Charles Ramble in 2009.
We would like to thank Sarah Skumanov, Nyima Drandul Gurung, Kami 
Gurung, Rinchen Loden Lama, Zdzisław Dobrowolski and Rajmund Radziewicz, 
who were members of the project team, for their commitment, enthusiasm, 
and hard work. We would like to thank Thomas Schrom for sharing his many 
years of experience of preserving the Nepali Cultural Heritage as UNESCO 
representative in Kathmandu, painting conservator Luigi Fieni for sharing his 
knowledge about conservation of cultural heritage in Mustang, and archaeolo-
gist Mark Aldenderfer, who provided us with further information about the 
archaeological sites of Mustang and provided the samples of manuscripts he 
discovered in Mustang. Lucie Vignon kindly volunteered her time while work-
ing with us on the Mardzong manuscripts during our fieldwork in 2017. Bob 
France read passages of the book and offered useful advice on English-language 
matters. Finally, we would like to thank the abbot and monks of Choede mon-
astery and the people of Lo Monthang. We are grateful to all those who helped 
and facilitated our research and also to those who made it more difficult, since 
this gave us an opportunity to learn.

Chapter 1
Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and its Heritage
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
1	
Accounts of History and Religion
The area of Mustang district is located in the northern highlands of west-
central Nepal on the edge of the Tibetan plateau. It was originally known as 
the Kingdom of Lo, a name used for over fourteen hundred years by its inhabit-
ants (Lopa). “Mustang” is derived from the original Tibetan name of the capital 
of Lo, traditionally spelt in Tibetan as sMon thang, along with other variants 
in literary sources.1 As Dhungel explains, the Tibetan sources provide at least 
five different spellings of the word whose most obvious meaning may suggest 
“South”, as Mustang is located on the southern frontier of the Tibetan-speaking 
world. Some may prefer Dhungel’s more poetic meaning: “the plain land of 
aspiration, blessing or good desire.”2
Both historical and archaeological records suggest that Mustang was in the 
past absolutely crucial, a vibrant, dynamic, culturally rich, and religiously di-
verse area and a part of ancient Tibetan culture. Even though politically part of 
Nepal today, Mustang is still linked by its history, culture and religious tradition 
to Tibet. Although Tibet now lies within the political boundary of China, it was 
once an empire and the inspiring source of culture for the Himalayan people 
in what is now northern Nepal. Tibet’s written history dates from the seventh 
century CE with the ruler Srong btsan sgam po (d. 649/650), who was able to 
bring Tibet to the international scene and consolidate its scattered tribes into 
a nation, and to establish a strong empire ruled by a single supreme author-
ity. The southern territory of Tibet during his successors’ reigns included the 
southern and western settlements in the Himalayas, including upper parts of 
what are now Kashmir, Nepal, Sikkim, Bhutan and Assam. The region is also 
mentioned in the chronicles of Ladakh as existing in the seventh century.
In the ninth century, because of the internal conflicts between the Bon and 
Buddhist aristocrats, the early Tibetan empire disintegrated, causing the emer-
gence of numerous principalities within the ancient territory of Tibet. The 
Lo (Mustang) region then became one of the southern-most frontier districts 
1	 The current pronunciation of the name among the inhabitants is Lo Monthang.
2	 Dhungel 2002, 185–186.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 36 of 332 ---
2
Chapter 1
of Ngari (mNga’ ris), located in West Tibet. Ngari was the collective name of 
the three political districts of Western Tibet known as Mar yul (the Ladakh 
region), Gu ge Pu rang (Mansarovara and Kailash regions), and Mang yul (the 
Gungthang region). Mustang was once an independent kingdom in its own 
right. Several monuments, such as Ghami’s Mani wall (305 m long), Khorok 
Chorten, Ghami, Dakmar and Gar Gonpa of Lo Gekar in Upper Mustang, 
are believed to be connected with the legendary history of Padmasambhava. 
Likewise, the famous Indian teacher Atiśa visited Mustang on his way to Tibet 
in the tenth century. At the turn of eleventh century the region was visited by 
Milarepa. This was a time of the most dynamic development of religion and 
the building of monasteries and centres of education, as well as the growth of 
art, religious and political life. It was also a time when a number of Buddhist 
schools were established in Mustang.
Amepal, a nobleman from the neighbouring Tibetan principality of 
Gungthang, completed the conquest his father began and unified the feudal 
estates along the Kali Gandaki River in the fourteenth century. He also moved 
his base from Geling to Lo Monthang, and succeeded in founding the Kingdom 
of Lo in 1380. The city of Lo Monthang was founded by Amepal’s son Agön 
Zangpo in the mid-fifteenth century.3 During the reign of Amepal and his suc-
cessors Lo became a prosperous state, strong both economically and political-
ly. During the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, it also become a religious and 
scholarly centre in the Tibetan world. Monks from Lo travelled to Tibet to en-
hance their knowledge under renowned masters in local monasteries. Lo also 
became a destination for pilgrims. Arts flourished, especially in the fifteenth 
century, thanks to the rulers’ patronage of the Sakya school of Buddhism in 
south-western Tibet. As mentioned above, the history of Mustang is interwo-
ven with the history of southern Latö (La stod) and Gungthang (Southwestern 
Tibet) where the production of art, books, and printing technology flourished. 
A rich variety of artefacts and historic buildings from that time are there-
fore preserved in Mustang. An artistic aesthetic was introduced from India 
by Newar and Tibetan artists through the consistent and consequent use of 
Indian iconography and iconometry.
Buddhism was introduced to Lo by Indian and Tibetan masters through the 
translation of Sanskrit texts. In the eleventh and twelfth centuries Buddhism 
flourished in northern Mustang, whilst Bon spread throughout the south. In 
the fifteenth century we witness a renaissance of Buddhism under the Sakya 
master Kun dga’ bzang po (1382–1456).4 The major monasteries of Mustang 
3	 Ramble 2008, 26.
4	 Ehrhard 2001, 235.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 37 of 332 ---
3
Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and Its Heritage
belong to the Sakya and Ngor schools of Tibetan Buddhism, along with nu-
merous temples, palaces, and ruins that bear witness to its rich past. Since the 
founding of the kingdom of Mustang in the fourteenth century, we see that the 
kings had been supporters of the Sakya school of Tibetan Buddhism, and some 
members of the family had been important hierarchs in the Buddhist Church. 
The family itself had originally been followers of the old Bon religion of Tibet.
Bon, a combination of several elements of Indian Buddhism and a range 
of endemic traditions, is considered by Tibetans to be the indigenous religion 
that preceded the arrival of Buddhism in the seventh century CE. Today’s reli-
gion is the result of an extraordinarily rich convergence of religious traditions 
from different places, notably India, Central Asia, China and Tibet itself. For 
followers of Bon it is axiomatic that all the teachings contained in the scrip-
tural corpus represent the teachings of the quasi-mythical founder of the reli-
gion, Tonpa (the Teacher) Shenrab Miwo. They believe Tonpa Shenrab to have 
lived between 18,000 and 8,000 years ago, and to have been born in the sacred 
land of Olmo Lungring, in Zhangzhung, which is vaguely linked with Western 
Tibet. Part of Zhangzhung corresponds to Tazig, itself a term for an undefined 
part of Central Asia. The teachings of Bon are said to have been transmitted in 
the language of Zhangzhung, a historical but now extinct western Himalayan 
language, and later translated into Tibetan. Bonpos maintain that there was a 
Zhangzhung script, and, while this script is sometimes used for short passages 
in Bonpo works, no conclusive evidence has yet come to light to support the 
belief that such a script may have antedated the development of the Tibetan 
alphabet in the seventh century. The teachings attributed to Shenrab are or-
ganised according to several traditional models, the best known of which is 
the “nine-vehicle” framework, commonly referred to as the Nine Ways of Bon. 
Whereas the “higher” vehicles exhibit a clear kinship with the Indian Buddhist 
Tantric systems, the “lower” vehicles are a set of rituals and recitations that ap-
pear to have indigenous roots.
Mustang at that time dominated the salt trade along the Kali Gandaki River 
and became a wealthy and powerful kingdom. The situation become more 
complicated in the seventeenth century, when Mustang came under the suzer-
ainty of the Kingdom of Jumla, to which it was forced to pay taxes. The Kingdom 
of Lo, together with Jumla, became part of Nepal following the Gorkha con-
quest in the eighteenth century (1789). At that time Prithvi Narayan Shah, the 
young king of Gorkha, embarked on a series of conquests that would bring a 
vast swathe of the Himalayas under a single ruler. In 1855 Lo supported Nepal 
against the Tibetans. The King of Nepal thus allowed the King of Mustang to 
keep the title of ‘Raja of Mustang’, although he had little political power. After 
the unification, the dukes of Baragaon retained judicial and administrative 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 38 of 332 ---
4
Chapter 1
powers under the authority of the Gorkhas until the mid-19th century, when 
the government of Nepal, by then in the hands of the dynastic rule of the Rana 
Prime Ministers, auctioned off these rights, together with the power to collect 
taxes, to a non-local contractor. Much later, the fact that the area had been for 
some time a loose tributary to the Gorkha Kings of Nepal led China to respect 
Mustang’s boundaries in 1959, and Mustang thereby avoided Chinese occupa-
tion and the disasters of the Cultural Revolution. Soon afterwards, renegade 
Khampa freedom fighters battling the Chinese used Mustang as a base of op-
erations, and the area was closed to Westerners until 1992.
Within the nation of Nepal, the kings of Mustang had continued to uphold a 
degree of autonomy within their capital city Lo Monthang, while Mustang itself 
became one of 75 districts of Nepal. Mustang was allowed to keep its hereditary 
rulers as long as Nepal also remained a kingdom. In 2006 the new Communist 
government of Nepal declared an end to the monarchy that had founded and 
ruled the nation for over 200 years, and at the same time abolished the royal 
status of three other minor kingdoms that had been absorbed into the nation 
during the unification. One of these was Mustang. Nepal changed from a king-
dom to become a republic in 2008, and the district of Mustang still requires a 
special, restricted-area permit to enter. This limits number of visitors, but does 
not protect culture and environment of the region from sustainable changes.
2	
Climate, Landform, People and Trade along the Kali Gandaki River
Mustang is also notable for both its climate and geographical appearance, as 
the monsoon rains barely reach the area, on the outskirts of the Tibetan pla-
teau. This extremely arid region has to rely almost exclusively upon glacier 
streams for its water supply. The Kali Gandaki River flows through Mustang 
from north to south, with its small tributaries creating amazing ravines, passes, 
and rocky hills along the way (Fig. 1.1). In Nepal the Kali Gandaki River is no-
table for its deep gorge, cutting right through the middle of the highest parts of 
the Himalayas between the Annapurna and the Dhaulagiri massifs. It has cre-
ated the greatest canyon on earth, deeper in fact that the Grand Canyon carved 
by the Colorado River in Arizona, United States. Still, greenery is only found in 
the settled areas near the river banks.
The Kali Gandaki is a sacred river throughout the Hindu world. Many pil-
grims make their way along it to the sacred shrine of Muktinath, which lies 
behind Mt Annapurna. The status of Muktinath derives from the fact that a 
little temple there shelters three miraculous objects: a burning stone, a flaming 
stream, and blazing earth. Knowledge about the natural gas leaking through 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 39 of 332 ---
5
Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and Its Heritage
the soil to create this natural phenomenon has not diminished the fame of this 
sacred shrine. Ammonites, known as saligram, highly treasured in the area, are 
found in the river bed especially north of Tukuche.
The canyon of the Kali Gandaki River is one of the few passages linking 
the world of the High Tibetan plateau (south-western Tibet) with the lower 
tropical lands of India via Nepal. When walking along the passage one moves 
onward through jungle thicket to pine trees, from hot stuffy narrow valleys to 
wind-swept pastures on rounded peaks. This geographical position offers ex-
cellent potential for controlling trade and collecting tolls from traders along 
the route. Much is known about the way in which trade in the region operated 
thanks mainly to the pioneering work of Christoph von Fürer-Haimendorf 
and James F. Fisher.5 Fürer-Haimendorf was the first anthropologist to spend 
time among the Thakalis and to study Northwest borderland areas of Nepal 
and Tibet, along with centuries-old interconnections and trade paths in the 
region. Fisher focused on the village of Tarangpur in the Dolpo region and 
its economy and trade. The area of Mustang, especially the 30 km long Thak 
5	 Fürer-Haimendorf 1975; Fisher 1987.
Figure 1.1	 Kali Gandaki river valley near Chusang
Photograph by Sarah Skumanov (2018)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 40 of 332 ---
6
Chapter 1
Khola valley in southern Mustang was the main focus of Michael Vinding’s re-
search on the ethnography and life of the Thakali people.6
The Kali Gandaki Valley of Nepal was therefore one of the main commercial 
highways between the Gangetic Plains and the Tibetan Plateau for centuries. 
The main commodities of exchange were grain from the large markets in the 
Indian subcontinent and salt from the dry saline lakes of Tibet north-west of 
Lhasa, such as Dhabe Tshakha, Marme Tshakha, and Jang Tsakha. The route, 
however, also carried a hugely diverse range of goods.7 From upland down the 
valley traders also brought wool, blankets, carpets, butter, cheese, weapons, 
live sheep and goats. In the other direction grain, such as rice and wheat, oil, 
chili, paper, metal utensils and cotton products were brought. The goods were 
carried by animals in caravans or by porters. The pack-animals used in higher 
regions were yaks, dzos and mules; in lowlands donkeys and horses.8
As a result of trade many Lopas migrate south during the cold period and 
engage in trade, for example, in textiles and herbs. This seasonal migration 
sometimes seems to turn into a semi-permanent migration because the Lopas 
find it easier to make their living in the lowlands of Nepal and India. That in 
turn indicates that more income-generating programmes and activities in 
Mustang need to be developed in order to persuade people to stay in their 
homeland.9 Since the Chinese took over Tibet, traditional trading patterns 
have changed. Many Mustangi have been forced to exchange labour for cash 
in order to subsist. The old system of barter could no longer work. Although 
heavily regimented by Chinese control of exchange rates and trading partners, 
it was still possible to make a living some years ago, but less so now. People in 
Mustang are good at business, and used to trade across the border, especially 
on the black market. Today, however, the borders are closed, and the Korala 
border point only opens for trade twice a year during festivals, where limited 
amounts of goods may be exchanged via representatives on both the Nepali 
and Chinese sides (Fig. 1.2).
The road-building project has been underway for the last 3 years, and a fur-
ther 2 years are required to complete the road from Jomsom to Lo Monthang, 
and beyond that to Tibet (China).10 The paved roads to Muktinath are already 
complete. A huge customs point is now being built on the Chinese side, while

nothing has yet been begun on the Nepali side. There is also a completed wide 
gravel road from Choser (above Lo Monthang) to the border.
Everyday life in Mustang, however, still revolves around local trade, con-
trolled tourism and animal husbandry. A partial road connecting Lo Monthang 
with Tibet has been built, and yaks, ponies and, more recently, jeeps and mo-
torbikes are used to transport goods and people between villages. The situa-
tion today, however, is changing due to the rapid development of the region 
and the construction of new roads and connections (Fig. 1.3). A connection by 
air between Pokhara and Jomsom has been in operation since 1976. Jomsom 
airport is on the Kali Gandaki River world’s deepest gorge at an elevation of 
2682 m above the mean sea level (Fig. 1.4). The river gorge reaches a depth of 
ca. 5000 m between the Annapurna and Dhaulagiri massifs. The air route to 
Jomsom Airport enters the Kali Gandaki River gorge at Ghasa and follows the 
path from Lete, Tukuche and Marpha, to Jomsom.11 The gorge extends further 
north along Kagbeni, Chuksang, Chele, Lo Monthang and finally flowing onto 
the Tibetan Plateau.

Regmi 2014, 111–112.

Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and Its Heritage
Survival in such a harsh environment demanded an interdependent society 
with common values and stable population. Exposure to outside influences, 
however, combined with a shift of power from the local to the national level, 
has begun to erode the traditional way of life. The system of shared values, 
which has bound the community together for nearly a thousand years, has 
begun to engage increasingly with the modern world. This has caused a sense 
of insecurity and has triggered numerous changes which are now irreversible.
3	
Cultural Heritage of Mustang
It takes between 20 and 23 days to walk from Kathmandu to Upper Mustang. 
In the age of aeroplanes and helicopters, however, this may be less meaning-
ful than in the past. In spite of this, Mustang is still often described as “the 
forbidden kingdom” or “the last bastion of Tibetan Culture.” Isolated so long, 
both geographically and politically Nepal was closed to foreigners until the 
1950s and Tibet was also forbidden territory. When the first Western explor-
ers became interested in Upper Mustang, they could only gain a peek into the 
kingdom due to the regional restrictions imposed by both the Government of 
Nepal and the people of Mustang themselves, the latter in an effort to protect 
their ancient way of life. The first official tourist groups gained access to Upper 
Mustang only in 1992, and there are continued restrictions today: the Nepali 
government in Kathmandu offers a special 10-day permit and it is mandatory 
for explorers to travel in groups of two or more with a guide, and pay 50 USD 
per day just for permission to enter the restricted area. Tourism has brought 
with it both advantages and disadvantages on the preservation of the tangible 
and intangible cultural heritage.
Despite all the difficulties of exploring Mustang there are many accounts 
on that region written by researchers, travellers and writers. Their accounts 
are a valuable source of information about Mustang, its culture, people and 
heritage. In 1952 Giussepe Tucci,12 the legendary Tibetologist, was probably 
the first 20th-century western scholar to travel through the region of western 
Nepal. His account preserves an invaluable record of the art, culture, social and 
religious practices of the inhabitants of Mustang prior to the inevitable chang-
es that have occurred with the intrusion of the modern world and regional 
politics into this ancient land.13 His main work related to Mustang is Journey 
12		
Tucci 2006.
13		
For a bibliography of Tucci works see Nalesini 2018.

Chapter 1
to Mustang written in 1952. Soon after Tucci, Michel Peissel,14 a French-born, 
English-raised explorer, wrote his account for National Geographic in 1964. 
His book, originally published in 1967, conveyed a dramatic standpoint on 
a land little seen by outsiders even as the Cold War threatened its borders. 
Michel Peissel was the first person who tried to record the history of Mustang 
from Mollas, though it was David P. Jackson who translated and interpreted 
The Mollas of Mustang. David Snellgrove focused on Tibetan Buddhism. 
Ramesh K. Dhungel’s The Kingdom of Lo (Mustang) a Historical Study, is a po-
litical history of Lo which narrates the history from the beginning to the pres-
ent with sources found from both Nepali and Tibetan historical documents.
Mustang has also been in the spotlight for archaeologists for the last couple 
of decades because of its ancient history, location along the silk roads, its sta-
tus as the “last bastion” of Tibetan culture outside China and the fact that it is 
accessible to western scholars for research, and because it is still rich in both 
elements of indigenous biodiversity and artefacts of cultural heritage.
Some of the most mesmerising sights studied by archaeologists in the re-
gion are the hundreds of isolated cliff dwellings. In recent decades teams of 
mountaineers and archaeologists have discovered more of these cliff-face sky 
caves, many of which were used by Buddhists as meditation chambers, while 
others seem to have been used as storage units. An interesting example is the 
Phudzeling site, possibly a primary settlement of modern Kagbeni, about 2 km 
to the east of Kagbeni on the Dzong River explored by archaeologists in the 
1990s. The ruins of houses, abandoned fields and several cave systems were 
discovered and studied by Angela Simons.15 Organic samples found in the cave 
systems have revealed that the cave settlements in the Muktinath valley may 
go back to 4000 BCE.16 In 1995 a team of archaeologists from the Institute of 
Prehistory of the University of Cologne, Germany, in cooperation with the 
Department of Archaeology in Kathmandu, Nepal found a unique prehistoric 
burial site in the artificial cave systems of Muktinath Valley, 3600 m above sea 
level. One of the caves (Mebrak) had been used as a community burial cham-
ber from ca. 400 BC to 50 AD, and contained approximately 30 naturally mum-
mified bodies deposited in bed-like wooden coffins exhibiting ornamental 
carving and elaborate painting.17 More recently the cave complex in Samdzong 
Village (4200 m above sea-level), north-west of Lo Monthang, revealed shaft 
tombs and a mortuary tradition dating back as far as ca. 400–650 CE. A large

The material culture of this region, as in the case of Tibet, is interwoven 
with the religion. Objects from Buddhism and Bon dominated the visual envi-
ronment, from sacred architecture, such as monasteries, temples and stupas, 
resplendent with interior artefacts, such as books, scroll paintings, murals, and

statues, to elements of sacred landscape, such as stones with carved mantras, 
painted rocks, and prayer flags. So-called Tibetan (Buddhist or Bon) ‘art’ was, 
in this historical context, a functional, religious instrument, intended to be 
spiritually effective. The materiality of these objects for Tibetans, however, is 
secondary and often neglected when it comes to the preservation of cultural 
heritage. What is left is in rather poor condition despite the favourable climate.
UNESCO’s expanding World Heritage Sites promote a universal concept of 
heritage, while new individual and collective interests are rooted in local ways 
of thinking, rather supporting vintage and re-use of local concepts.20 In vari-
ous parts of the world similar claims are used to legitimise rights for under-
privileged or indigenous groups. The people of Mustang are an example of one 
such community. The conservation of cultural heritage improved significantly 
when Nepal joined UNESCO in 1953. While most of the funds and support have 
been directed to the Kathmandu valley, however, inhabitants of Mustang feel 
rather neglected. Although local communities are proud of their heritage, 
they lack both the resources and knowledge to safeguard their heritage. The

Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and Its Heritage
situation became even more evident after the devastating impact of the 2015 
earthquake on Mustang’s unique cultural heritage. The continuing, albeit still 
rare, conservation projects funded by Western foundations and conducted by 
professional conservators are unable to address all needs.
Only a few artefacts have received any attention from Western conservators, 
and are undergoing restoration. Since 1999 important restoration work has 
been carried out in two main temples in Lo Monthang, the capital of Lo. For 
the time being, however, conservation projects are inaccessible to the profes-
sional community, since the documentation of both the original artefacts and 
the treatment conducted is unavailable to the scholarly world until the work 
is completed. The quality of the present conservation treatment may not thus 
be assessed or evaluated by professionals for generations to come depending 
on when the project is officially declared completed. The important point was 
made by Christian Luczanits in his contribution to the Buddhist Art Forum in 
2012. Each intervention also entails the obscuration of particular aspects of 
the artwork relevant to research into art history, and at times such evidence 
may be made inaccessible or destroyed entirely. To him, solving the architec-
tural problems of the monument is by far the greatest priority, and work on the 
interior decoration of a monument needs to be carefully evaluated and imple-
mented, and we may not deal with Himalayan monuments and art differently 
from the way we would our own heritage.21 At the same time, due to a clash 
between Western conservation ethics and the expectation of local society, it is 
difficult to develop a policy for the protection of cultural heritage.
It is thus unsurprising that the preservation work in Mustang has provoked 
some controversy in recent years. Since the territory of Upper Mustang was 
opened to foreigners in 1992, people have slowly become aware that their cul-
tural heritage is under threat.22 Lo Monthang, the capital of the former king-
dom of Mustang in present-day Nepal, has been on the tentative UNESCO 
World Heritage List since 2008. Since that time an effort to protect the heritage 
of the Mustang has encouraged both foreigners and local people to undertake 
research and documentation of artefacts of cultural heritage. Some preserva-
tion initiatives have been taken, such as the creation of a protection zone in 
Lo Monthang, whereby no new buildings may be constructed inside the me-
dieval walls. The traditional settlements are compact and dense in style with 
flat roofed buildings to suit the region’s climate. The buildings are made up 
of rammed earth and wood making good use of local tradition and the local 
materials. The settlements are accompanied by forts known as Dzong, as well

as Chortens, Mani walls, Gonpas, Caves, and Lhakhangs, all of which are el-
ements of living heritage in the Himalayan region with the practice of both 
Buddhism and Bon adapted to Tibetan cultural and sacred geography (Fig. 1.7).
The oldest monastery, Gar Gonpa of Lo Gekar, belonging to the Nyingma 
tradition, is believed to have been built in the 8th century, shortly before Samye 
Monastery in Tibet. Both Nyingma, Luri Gonpa and Gonpa Gang were respec-
tively founded in the 13th and the 17th century.23 Choede monastery, where the 
Mardzong collection is currently stored, was built by Tashi Goyen in the fif-
teenth century. The monastery continues to accommodate practising monks. 
Valuable sculptures, books, thangkas and masks belonging to the Jhyampa and 
Thubchen monasteries are kept there. The famous Tiji festival is conducted 
every year by the monks there. By 1427 Amepal had built the fort (Tib. rdzong) 
at mKha ’spyod and the castle (Tib. mkhar) at Lo Monthang.24 However, accord-
ing to the Byams pa gtsug lag khang gi dkar chag the palace was built by Agön 
Zangpo, Amepal’s successor, and a wall was later built around Lo Monthang 
during his reign in the second half of the fifteenth century.25

Introduction to Mustang (Lo) and Its Heritage
Although some of Mustang’s murals and caves have been studied and those 
studies published, portable works such as sculptures, scroll paintings, stupas 
and especially books have attracted less attention and have remained large-
ly unknown to scholars and the general public alike. These objects attest to 
the region’s rich past and its relevance as an important centre of cultural and 
artistic activity and religious practices. Moreover, books were sacred objects, 
which, when not read, were treated as objects of cult and rituals, hidden from 
strangers’ gaze and forbidden from being touched or opened by those unpre-
pared. During recent decades the looting of art and cultural objects in many 
monasteries has been an important problem especially concerning the por-
table cultural heritage of Mustang.
The looting and subsequent restitution of cultural property in Europe were 
a part of political and public life for several decades after the Second World 
War. Alongside the many public accounts, a diverse and wide-ranging aca-
demic literature has developed, providing insights from legal, historical and art 
historical perspectives.26 Research into provenance is also rapidly establishing 
itself as a field in its own right. In Mustang, however, the situation persists and 
local people seem not to know how to deal with the problem. Many monaster-
ies are incapable of developing sufficient security measures. The fact that there 
is no clear record of art objects in the possession of monasteries in Mustang 
has thus become more valid, since when objects are plundered original owners 
are unable to prove ownership and re-claim them even if the stolen artefacts 
surface somewhere in the West.
In response to this problem some efforts to pursue the documentation of 
monastic collections in Mustang have been organised in the region, such as 
Christian Luczanits’ SOAS-based and AHRC-funded research project, “Tibetan 
Buddhist Monastery Collections Today,” which directly collaborates with mon-
asteries in Ladakh and Mustang to document, assess and support the manage-
ment of their collections.27 Since 2012 the SOAS-based project documented the 
collections of the monasteries of Garphug, Namgyal (manuscripts and their 
covers), Lo Gekhar, and Jarkhot (Dzar Choede). Each participating institution 
received the full photographic documentation and an inventory of their collec-
tion. Another documentary project connected with Mustang written heritage 
is Helmut Tauscher’s project based at the University of Vienna and focusing

on ‘local’ Kanjurs, documenting the collections of Kanjur in Mustang, Dolpo, 
mNga’ ris, and Ladakh.28
Tibetan monasteries are known for being repositories of a variety of ancient 
objects filling their interiors. Some of these are no longer used, while others 
continue to serve their original purpose as parts of rituals and the religious 
life of monasteries. Originally many of these collections were an integral part 
of the fixtures and fittings of the monastery, and thus accessible to visitors. 
Monasteries traditionally had inventory lists of such objects in their posses-
sion, created by curators who periodically checked inventories and added new 
acquisitions. Such lists are, however, now often lost.
Today many monasteries have added museum spaces to their premises to 
cater to tourists who pass through Mustang in great numbers these days. These 
spaces are an expression of a modern transformation, but are at the same time 
in conflict with the traditional monastic function of these objects. The concept 
of the museum in Mustang is thus extremely complex, since it is difficult to 
reconcile the needs of tourists and the needs of local residents. The problem 
also emerges when we consider the function of old books. As objets d’art as 
well as curiosities for tourists they have a value to the academic community 
and the art market in the West, and for local people they are sacred objects 
bound with the presence of the divine: the embodiment of Buddha’s words. 
They also originally functioned as collections, rather than individual objects. 
Since Mustang has changed in recent years and these changes are irreversible, 
new ways of thinking about cultural heritage needs to be implemented.

Chapter 2
A Note on Interdisciplinary Methods
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
The heterogeneous character of the knowledge embodied in manuscripts en-
genders multiple methodological problems for manuscript studies.1 The ab-
sence of obvious or generally accepted methods, terminology, and standards 
makes manuscript studies an experimental field lacking clear-cut, readily ap-
plicable guidelines for its application. To reveal the multi-layered meaning of 
a single object, as well as its textual content we also need to look at its materi-
ality, economic dimension, and artistic value. The necessary interdisciplinary 
research includes aspects of paper science, archaeometry, history, codicology, 
anthropology, and geography in order to classify, locate, and, where possible, 
date books that are currently anonymous. A combination of data sources de-
rived from books, ethnographic information collected in the field, mapping 
of plant sources, and fibre analysis combine to provide an unprecedented po-
tential to illuminate the history of the production of studied manuscripts and 
greatly increase the interpretative strength of the results.
The research methodology applied to the Mardzong manuscripts combines 
a traditional textual study of manuscripts with a new perspective, involving the 
examination of the codicological and material features underlying their tex-
tual content. This strong, multidisciplinary approach offers new ways to view 
books as artefacts. Tibetan codicology is an emerging field of study to which our 
Mardzong Project belongs. Broadly speaking, the study we are undertaking for 
the Mardzong manuscripts is concerned with the relationship between paper, 
ink, page layout and book form, and questions of provenance and dating. To a 
certain extent we are also concerned with book illumination and ornamentation.
The first and most crucial aim of our Mardzong manuscript project was to 
examine manuscripts in situ in the Choede monastery in Lo Monthang (Figs 2.1 
and 2.2). That means that fieldwork in Mustang was a crucial element of this 
project and not just an adventure.
The first step in the work in Choede monastery involved Nyima Drandul 
Gurung, Rinchen Loden Lama and Kemi Tsewang Gurung organising the 
1	 For more information on methods applied to Tibetan manuscripts see: Scherrer-Schaub 1999, 
3–36; Scherrer-Schaub and Bonani 2002, 184–215; Chayet et al. 2010, Helman-Ważny 2014, 
12–21; van Schaik et al. 2015, 110-132; for methodological approached to material analyses in 
the field of manuscript studies, see: Neate and Howell 2011.

Mardzong folios into volumes, and later in collaboration with Charles Ramble 
producing a handlist of the volumes’ titles and a translation of selected frag-
ments, including colophons. At the same time Sarah Skumanov and I docu-
mented both the codicological features and the state of preservation of the 
books in the collection. During subsequent field trips to Upper Mustang high-
resolution digital photographs of selected folios and specific features, such as 
the structure of paper photographed against light, close-ups of illuminations 
and other manuscript details were produced for the Mardzong collection. The 
writing and painting techniques and the texture of the paper were also docu-
mented using a Dino-Lite microscope. During documentation, visual and tech-
nical features of papers, up to five folios of each of the manuscripts depending 
on the variety of features per volume, were described and sampled for labora-
tory analyses.
In the case of the Mustang manuscripts, collections of appropriate refer-
ence materials did not exist at the beginning of our work and therefore needed 
to be assembled. For this reason, the project undertook an ethnographic study 
of paper- and book-making traditions in the region. These findings, when com-
pared to a sample of manuscripts from the Mardzong cave cache, helped to put 
the collection we studied into context. During fieldwork we looked for places 
where book production, printing and paper-making have continued in order 
to collect references for our work on manuscripts. The technology, methods, 
tools, and materials used in paper-making were documented. However, this 
was carried out mostly outside Mustang, since there are no surviving work-
shops in the district itself. Paper-making plants and other possible resources 
related to book culture were located.
The time between field trips was devoted to laboratory work, translations, 
identification of images, gradual data input and interpretation of our re-
sults. As part of the documentary work, Zdzisław Dobrowolski and Rajmund 
Radziewicz created a portal dedicated to manuscripts from the Mardzong 
caves. A bilingual, English-Polish theme programme for WordPress content 
management was developed, which manages the database and presents re-
search material and documentation of subsequent scientific expeditions to 
Nepal. The information in the portal has been organised for ease of search and 
use. The portal provides images of manuscript pages in full resolution with 
a view to their use in scientific works, as well as in lower resolution for the 
website. The repository contains data on the physical characteristics of the 
books with an emphasis on paper, and aims to identify the dates and regions 
of books and papers on the basis of their material features and information 
derived from their contents. Illuminations were carefully edited. We took over 
8,000 images, including many with different exposures (Figs 2.3 and 2.4). Video

footage illustrating the process of creating manuscripts was also collected and 
edited. These films have been subtitled in English and are also available on the 
YouTube platform. Photographic documentation is stored in the original RAW 
format as well as in Tiff format. In order to make manuscripts more accessible, 
our photographs were also recorded in the form of microfilms, which will be 
made available to university libraries throughout the world.
Data derived from the Mardzong manuscripts were recorded according to 
the codicological template developed by the author of this chapter, and based 
on her earlier work on manuscripts from Central Asia.2 The template is ex-
plained below. It has been organised into six sections, such as general informa-
tion, writing/copying, format and layout, decorative elements, ownership and 
authentication marks, and materials. Each section is described below.
1	
General Information
Collection/Access No.: In general, this is the number under which a document is 
catalogued within an archive and is the designation by which the document is 
known. The collection of Mardzong manuscripts, however, was initially a mess 
of loose folios, inaccessible to visitors. Our team was the first to organise it. 
Unfortunately, there is no cataloguing system for any of the artefacts preserved 
in the monastery. For this reason, we decided to give volumes a short title and 
to number the volume in Tibetan letters in addition to Arabic numerals, as we 
shall do further in this book. The “call number” of each volume can therefore 
be ascertained from this book, making it possible to find the relevant manu-
script in the Choede monastery.
Type of object: This category usually entails a description of the type of ar-
tefact concerned. In the case of the Mardzong collection, the description of 
every volume was “manuscript on paper.”
Dating and provenance: Estimating the date and provenance of Mardzong 
manuscripts is a challenge, but it is fundamental for giving the collection a 
historical context. Rarely, the text may contain a date, or mention a patron, 
artist, owner or other persons whose dates we know. Sometimes the identifi-
cation of individuals, the tracing of lineages, or the determining of names of 
teachers may lead to an approximate date. The Mardzong manuscripts were 
thus examined for biographical evidence, such as when and where the people 
mentioned in the books might have lived, and the dates and places mentioned 
2	 Helman-Ważny 2014; Dotson and Helman-Ważny 2016.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 56 of 332 ---
22
Chapter 2
in colophons, which may suggest dates and regions of origin. This method 
will not, however, distinguish an original text from a copy or a forgery. This is 
especially true in the case of Tibetan books because the textual content and 
book itself may be from two different periods, depending on the methods em-
ployed in the copying of Buddhist texts. For this reason other possible sources 
of information should also be taken into consideration. Additionally, there are 
many books that have no chronological information in the text or colophon. In 
such cases the most reliable results may come from technological and material 
analyses. A variety of features may be used when examining physical books 
to help with dating, notably textual content, paleography, codicology, and sty-
listic analyses of ornamentation and materials. Just as in the case with every 
individual feature and method used in dating a manuscript, every feature and 
method is best used in conjunction with the other tools available to us. The 
typology of writing styles, scripts and other features, such as painting style, 
page layout, and general format may thus prove useful, but only if we have suf-
ficient references for particular times and locations.3 At present, the potential 
for finding ‘fingerprints’ relating to time and origin of materials is promising, 
but we are still in the process of collecting data; we hope eventually to have 
enough reference information to use in the identification of the provenance 
of other books. From the point of scholarly work, knowledge of materials and 
technology helps in dating manuscripts, since some of the materials, as well 
as some of the technologies, were used only in particular regions and during 
specific periods of time. A fundamental approach to the material analysis of 
manuscripts is paper analysis. This procedure may enable us to group papers 
by culture and region, helping us to identify a book’s provenance and revealing 
links between groups of objects with the same distinguishable features. Such 
references may then be used to identify and classify individual manuscripts 
using previously established typologies, such as those developed for Tibetan 
and Central Asian manuscripts.
The main limiting factor in dating of pigments is the relatively small num-
ber of traditional pigments and dyes identifiable on all kinds of paper-based 
materials from antiquity to the 18th century, in total about 25 and 30 com-
pounds. Many are of mineral origin or synthetic reproductions of minerals, 
such as cinnabar, vermilion, and azurite. Even if the materials are identified 
precisely, we cannot determine their origins until they have been linked to par-
ticular deposits in the case of natural minerals, or time of production in the 
3	 Scherrer-Schaub and Bonani 2002; Tschudin 2004; Dalton et al. 2007, Helman-Ważny 2009, 
2010; Helman-Ważny and van Schaik 2013.

A Note on Interdisciplinary Methods
case of synthetic reproductions.4 Unfortunately, the search for unusual ma-
terials which may serve as fingerprinting information yields valuable results 
only extremely rarely on its own. We made an attempt to collect information 
on the geological deposits of minerals used for the preparation of pigment in 
Mustang and to collect reference samples of those minerals. However, we were 
able to obtain only very little information on that subject.
Radiocarbon dating is suitable for manuscripts composed of organic ma-
terials, using milligram-size samples. It is an independent, objective, and the 
only truly quantitative method for dating organic materials. As many schol-
ars have pointed out, however, this technique alone is rarely able to resolve 
issues of authenticity or provide precise dating. Depending on the time pe-
riod, the degree of accuracy can vary between as much as 50 and 300 years. 
Typically, there may be an error of +/−30 years on an uncalibrated date, which 
is then usually calibrated to obtain a date in calendar years with a similar error. 
Additionally, C14 analysis will provide the date when the plant cellulose mol-
ecules in the paper’s fibres were formed rather than when the paper was made, 
and the paper-making process will sometimes entail blending fibres from a 
wide variety of original sources and possibly different times. Furthermore, 
other substances such as dyes or fillers may have been added during the paper-
making process, and these may contaminate samples with the stable C12 iso-
tope (“dead carbon”). This makes results of radiocarbon dating moot in the 
case of single samples.
Find site: A place (or group of physical sites) in which evidence of manu-
script discovery is preserved (either historical or modern). This is important 
especially in case of manuscripts which come to light as a result of archaeo-
logical excavations, such as the objects found in the caves of Mustang.
Location: The place a manuscript is preserved at the time of its being 
described.
Category/Genre: Manuscripts can for example be described as Buddhist, 
Bon, contract, divination, history, inscription, legal, letter, medical, narrative, 
non-Buddhist, ritual, or administration. These are the tags currently used 
by International Dunhuang Project (IDP), Old Tibetan Documents Online 
(OTDO), and other databases. They may need to be expanded or adjusted ac-
cording to the nature of a particular collection. In our sample, the content 
was basically Bon or Buddhist, with a certain number of secular or indetermi-
nate texts.
Title: Here we may add a conventional title if a manuscript has one, or give 
the title by which the document has come to be known.
4	 Neate and Howell 2011.

Chapter 2
Content: This would consist of a short description of the type of text, as well 
as its historical context.
2	
Writing/copying
Language: This refers to the main language in which the text of the manuscript 
is written. In certain cases the text may contain short excerpts or titles in other 
languages, such as Sanskrit or Zhangzhung in the case of Buddhist and Bon 
works respectively.
Script: Here we distinguish between handwriting and print; written char-
acters. It should be noted, however, that a script may not necessarily be the 
same as the language of a manuscript. Excerpts in Sanskrit, for instance, are 
usually written in Tibetan script, whereas passages in Zhangzhung commonly 
use Zhangzhung script in addition to Tibetan.
Text execution: This entails the identification of the technology that was 
used to produce a text; basically whether it is handwritten or printed.
Writing implement: In the case of a handwritten text it may be possible 
to identify the implement that was used to write it. It is not always evident, 
though in some cases a difference between a reed or bamboo pen and a brush 
can be detected.
Number of scribal hands: Over recent years scholars have made some at-
tempts at analysing handwriting styles in Tibetan manuscripts.5 Handwriting, 
understood as the extension of the scribe’s personality defined by extraor-
dinarily detailed individual features, can help us to attribute manuscripts to 
specific scribes and consequently group them in ways that would otherwise 
have been unjustifiable or even unimaginable. The average person may recog-
nise the handwriting of an individual and differentiate between individuals to 
some degree, but only the broader features of the handwriting, such as letter 
formation, size, or slope of the handwriting are observed in such cases.6 Such 
an approach fails, however, to take into account the subtleties in the writing 
that may distinguish it from other very similar hands. Thus, if the identity of 
the scribe is not evident the obscurity should be accepted. Further advanced 
forensic identification may follow in cases of historical importance. In the light 
of a lack of handwriting references for the Mardzong volumes, the manuscripts 
provide little information that would help with identification. On one hand, 
the script used in the Mardzong volumes is formal, and it is extremely difficult 
5	 Dalton et al. 2007; Van Schaik 2014; Helman-Ważny and Ramble 2017, 270–272.
6	 On methods for analysing handwriting, see: Morris 2000.
