A Note on Interdisciplinary Methods
to find individual features which could identify particular hands. On the other 
hand, the name of the scribe of the Khams chen appears in the colophon, so at 
least we can identify the main hand, and look for it in other volumes.
3	
Format and Layout
Format: There is a wide variety of formats for the Tibetan book or document 
on paper. These include the scroll, concertina, codex, and the most familiar 
form of Tibetan book, the dpe cha, which consists of rectangular unbound 
leaves. Sometimes it is referred to as the pothi format, named after the Indian 
term for the model on which it was based. The Indian prototype is made out 
of palm leaves, and it is this form that the Tibetan longbook reproduces in 
paper. From its proliferation from the fifteenth century onward, wood-block 
printing or xylography cemented the dpe cha as the predominant format for 
the Tibetan book. The dpe cha certainly became the dominant book format in 
Tibetan culture and it is also the main format we found in the Mardzong col-
lection; only a few items are documents written on single sheets of paper, and 
one is an initiation card.
Size (h × w): When measuring a manuscript, its height is given first, then its 
width. A pothi leaf, for example, is very wide and not very high.
Overall No of pages: In the case of a pothi here one should give the overall 
number of existing leaves or pages in the volume.
Layout: Where the format is standard, as in some officially commissioned 
canonical works, one can be even more specific by measuring lines per folio, 
along with the measurements of frame and side, the dimensions of the upper 
and bottom margins, and indicating the numbering of folia convention.
Margins: As with many other measurements, this may vary, and should be 
made by taking several measurements and averaging them, noting any signifi-
cant variation, such as where margins might be larger on one leaf than on an-
other. For comparative purposes, however, when measuring several volumes 
from a collection, the same type of folios should be selected and clearly de-
scribed with the page number. It should also be noted whether the margins are 
marked out in ink or in drypoint. When measuring the full frame, the height 
is given first, then the width, and when measuring top margins, one measures 
from the edge of the paper to the head of the first line, whether ruled or un-
ruled. Similarly, for the bottom margins, one measures from the edge of the 
paper to the head of the last line. This “inflates” the measurement, but the al-
ternative is to measure from the lowest pen strokes, which are variable and 
therefore lead to imprecise measurements.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 60 of 332 ---
26
Chapter 2
Lines per page: Here one may measure the number of text lines on the first 
few pages, since the number usually increases until page 3 or 4, and then re-
mains constant for the rest of the volume with the exception, again, of the 
colophon or the last page.
Guidelines: Paper with guidelines is essentially “ruled.” This is often done 
with red or black ink, but it can also be done with the un-inked nib of a pen 
(or other implement), making only a depressed line in the paper. The latter 
process is called drypoint, or “nicking.” Guidelines, such as margins and circles 
around pothi holes, are usually made before writing, as part of the page setting.
Numbering of book leaves: The numbering of leaves, as opposed to pages is 
called foliation and the numbering of pages is pagination.7 Tibetan systems 
for numbering leaves resemble foliation in Latin manuscripts. One prevalent 
method uses a letter of the Tibetan alphabet (e.g., from ka to a, i.e. 1–30) to 
number the volume, and then uses written numerals for each leaf.8 The folia-
tion is written in the left margin of the recto, and the pagination (folio num-
ber) is shared by recto and verso. In both cases it is written perpendicular to 
the text lines of the main text.
4	
Elements of Ornamentation
Here one also records non-quantifiable features, such as the presence of paint-
ed illumination, drawings, and ornamentation. Methods drawn from the his-
tory of art have had a certain limited usefulness in the project. We documented 
the convention, style, technique and materials used for depicting saints and 
divinities in the Mardzong manuscripts. Iconography was recorded from the 
names usually found under the relevant illumination. Most of the manuscripts 
in our sample were illuminated, and the results of descriptions including infor-
mation on iconography, style, technique, and, whenever possible, the painting 
materials and tools, are included in chapter 4 of this book.

5	
Ownership and Authentication Marks
Like legal documents, albeit more rarely, the manuscripts may bear a variety 
of marks that are intended to certify ownership or validate the manuscript, 
among which are seals, fingerprints and other signatures, such as written 
names or crosses. One may record the occurrence of these marks, and propose 
to use them together with other observed features as criteria in the future de-
velopment of a typology of manuscripts. In the context of the Mardzong col-
lection one records seals, which usually signify ownership by a monastery or 
individual. Other marks are rather typical for archival documents.9
6	
Materials
Type of paper: Here one may outline paper features described below in detail, 
but mainly those that may be read with the naked eye; for example, whether 
paper is handmade or machine-made, what type of paper-making mould and 
sieve were used.
Raw material: Fibre analysis informs us about what things are made of, since 
the primary feature of paper is the type of raw material used for its production. 
This is the reason fibre analysis, if applicable, can be helpful in pinpointing 
provenance and sometimes for dating, when using a method that entails over-
lapping typologies. When comparing the results of the fibre analyses of paper 
with the distribution of the same plant, we can obtain information regarding 
the possible region of a manuscript’s origin. The area suggested by plant dis-
tribution may be critically evaluated by other sources of information, such as 
other features of manuscripts. In this way, we may discover whether all features 
originate from the same area (understood as a cultural context, country, or re-
gion). These results should help in answering some questions about the his-
tory of paper in the region, as well as the trade and import of paper and other 
writing materials in the Himalayas and Central Asia, even though much more 
research needs to be done to achieve higher precision for regional attribution.
The laboratory fibre analyses were conducted at the University of Warsaw. 
The aim of the procedure was fibre identification in collected specimens of 
paper using a transmitted-reflected and polarised light microscope with a 
camera attached for photographic documentation. Image Analysis Software 
was used during identification. A varying magnification from 50× up to 600× 
with both plain and polarised light was employed. For study, the sample is 
9	 Helman-Ważny and Ramble 2017, 315–322.

placed in a small beaker, immersed in distilled water, and boiled for about 20 
minutes. The water is then decanted and the sample is drained, de-fibred into 
a fine suspension of individual fibres, and placed onto the slide. Fibres were 
then observed and treated with Herzberg stain. Attention was paid to stain 
colouring, the morphology of fibres, and other cells, and elements of pulp. The 
width of fibres was measured in particular cases to support identification.
Paper-making sieve print (woven, laid lines /3 cm, chain line intervals): A typol-
ogy of the technological features of paper in the Mardzong manuscripts was 
built via the examination of paper leaves against light for paper-making sieve 
print type. The print of a textile sieve differs clearly from one made of bamboo 
(in a laid regular pattern), reed, or other grasses (in a laid irregular pattern) 
(Figs 2.5, 2.6, and 2.7). When sealed in the paper structure, this allows us to 
distinguish handmade woven paper and handmade laid paper characterised 
by the particular number of laid lines within 3 cm. These may be categorised 
as laid, regular where there is unequivocal clear evidence; laid, irregular where 
the pattern is not regular; and, finally, laid, patchy where the pattern can be 
seen only in patches of the paper, but could not have been made by anything 
else. This effect comes from using a doubled sieve, when there is cloth over 
an attached bamboo, reed or grass sieve. The laid type of mould or sieve is 
also sometimes characterised by chain lines, which are vertical lines from the 
screen on which the paper was manufactured. The sequence of measurements 
of the interval between two (or more) chain lines is given where chain lines are 
clearly visible. These intervals often vary within one sheet of paper, and in this 
case, the sequences of span values should be given.
Number of layers: One may note here whether a paper leaf is made up of 
one, two or more layers or sheets of paper that have been glued together.
Thickness of paper: This is a parameter that makes it possible to classify types 
and quality of paper. Using calipers, one should measure the paper in at least 
five different places and give a span value (Fig. 2.8). Good quality paper, for 
example, made from paper mulberry fibres on a very fine bamboo sieve usu-
ally has a very uniform thickness, in contrast to rougher paper made in remote 
mountainous areas with simple mould of textile on frame. This uniform thick-
ness comes from extremely even fibre distribution within the paper-making 
mould that was lifted when shaping the paper. Much of Tibetan and Nepali 
paper is of variable thickness, and this measurement may therefore have lim-
ited value here.
Texture (e.g., smooth, rough, polished): This is qualitative, but it may be an 
important indicator of the “natural recto,” that is, the side of the paper that is 
smoother as a result of the manufacturing process and which is therefore most 
desirable surface for writing. In the case of layered documents, however, and

sometimes evenly along one edge, also makes it possible to describe the type

Ink: The black ink is usually made from carbonised plants or soot, other 
colour pigments, or metal as the ink’s main components with a possible ad-
mixture of gum, honey, borax, or, for a special occasion, other unusual and 
extravagant materials such as blood. The inks and paints were manufactured 
in accordance with different recipes at different times.10 Precise compositions 
of inks and paints, however, are rarely determined due to difficulties in using 
the necessary scientific equipment in the field. Still, even basic descriptions of 
ink colour are useful for classifying books. Most ink is black, but we also find 
red and occasionally yellow in Tibetan books. Most pigments used in Tibetan 
books for illustrations are of natural mineral origin. Many are found in Tibet, 
while others are imported.11 Analyses of this compared to pigment analyses on 
mural paintings will give us an idea whether the same paints were used, and 
at what period. For fingerprinting the pigments there are also possible clues 
as to when they were made. A wide variety of analytical techniques may be

case, however, we were unable to transport more advanced equipment to 
Upper Mustang, and we decided to avoid taking samples of the ink, since it 
would damage the texts.
A record provided for each manuscript according to the template above 
from the Mardzong collection was then used to build independent typolo-
gies and chronologies that then characterise the Mardzong manuscripts as a 
unique collection of books. This allowed us to see the studied collection in 
context; namely to be referenced by other manuscript studies from the region 
using broader criteria. Dating of the selected manuscripts from this cache was 
crucial to place these books both within the local history of the Mustang region 
and also within the wider perspective of manuscript studies in Central Asia 
and the Himalayas. Both typology and dating are solid parameters for compar-
ative research on the history and geography of book craftsmanship, and con-
tribute information on the time-frame of the production of books in Mustang.
The material analyses of particular manuscripts from the Mardzong collec-
tion is supported by archival and bibliographic information and supplemented 
with ethnographic fieldwork in an interdisciplinary approach that cuts across 
terminological boundaries. Developing regional typologies by both studying 
manuscripts and interviewing local people contributes to a better understand-
ing and reconstruction of the lesser-known parts of the history of books and 
crafts related to their production, such as paper-making and printing. The doc-
umentation of differences in the traditional technology of paper production 
in different parts of Asia, according to the available materials and the develop-
ment of paper-making plants, is an independent source of information which 
facilitates the dating and the determination of provenance of still unidentified 
copies of books.
It is vital to remember that the Mardzong manuscripts are objects of the tan-
gible cultural heritage of Mustang. These objects must be studied in the same 
way as all other archaeological objects because preserved documents validate 
memories; the actuality of the object, as opposed to a reproduction or surro-
gate, provides those living with a link to the past. Preservation of these books 
demonstrates a recognition of the past and of the things that tell its story. In a 
wider perspective, it may aid in the reconstruction of the history of craftsman-
ship and trade in Tibet and shed light on the materiality of the Mardzong col-
lection. The issues considered in developing a programme for preserving the 
Mardzong collection and guidelines for conservation procedures to keep the 
collection in an appropriate condition are a topic for Sarah Skumanov’s thesis. 
The overview of the state of the Mardzong collection against a backdrop of the 
situation in Mustang is presented in chapter 9 of this book.

Chapter 3
The Mardzong Texts in Relation to the Bon Canon
Charles Ramble, Agnieszka Helman-Ważny, Nyima Drandul Gurung 
and Bazhen Zeren
The Bon works that form the subject of this chapter are among the most im-
portant and best-known canonical works of the Bon religion. Before discuss-
ing each of them in turn, a few words may be said by way of introduction to 
the Bon Canon itself. While the Tibetan Buddhist Canon has been the sub-
ject of numerous studies, the Bon counterpart has attracted far less attention. 
Fortunately, the few works that have been devoted to the subject include some 
studies of exemplary scholarship, and it is on these accounts that the following 
brief summary is based. The first really significant attempt to present the Bon 
Canon was Per Kvaerne’s “The Canon of the Tibetan Bonpos,” published in two 
issues of the Indo-Iranian Journal in 1974 and amounting to almost 90 pages.1 
When Per Kvaerne published this study, no copy of the Bon Canon was yet 
available outside Tibet—which was at that time of course inaccessible to 
foreigners—and the article was essentially a presentation of a catalogue of the 
Canon that had been written in 1842 by Tenzin Nyima, the 23rd abbot of Menri 
monastery. A much shorter article by the same author that offers an overview 
of the content of this pioneering study is “The literature of Bon,” which was 
published in 1996 as the seventh chapter of Tibetan Literature: Studies in Genre, 
edited by José Cabezón and Roger Jackson.2
Aware of the need for a much more substantial treatment of this complex 
topic, in 1995–1996, with the support of the Center for Advanced Study at the 
Norwegian Academy of Science and Letters in Oslo, Kvaerne coordinated an 
international seven-member team, supplemented by several correspondents 
and associates, “to provide a detailed catalogue—volume by volume, text by 
text, chapter by chapter” of the Bon Kanjur. The outcome of this project was 
a monumental volume entitled A Catalogue of the Bon Kanjur, edited by Dan 
Martin (the main coordinator, who also provided a substantial introduction), 
Per Kvaerne and Yasuhiko Nagano, and published as no. 40 (Bon Studies 8) in 
the Senri Ethnological Reports series.3
1	 Kvaerne 1974.
2	 Cabezón and Jackson 1996.
3	 Martin et al. 2003.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 68 of 332 ---
34
Chapter 3
Several different versions of the Bon Canon are now available, and a num-
ber of others yet are known to have existed at different times and in differ-
ent locations in Tibet. While the pre-1959 versions were either manuscripts or 
blockprints, the first modern print was published in 1985 by Ayong Rinpoche 
(1922–1996) in 154 volumes. The version that provided the basis for the Oslo 
project was the second edition, published in or around 1987 in northwestern 
Sichuan, in 192 volumes. A third edition in 178 volumes (with an additional, 
unnumbered volume) was published in Chengdu in 1995–1996 by the late 
Mongyal Lhasey Rinpoche and Shense Namkha Wangden.4
As is widely known, the Tibetan Buddhist Canon consists of two main bod-
ies of scriptures, the Kanjur and the Tenjur, these names being the Mongolian 
renderings of the Tibetan terms bKa’ ’gyur and bsTan ’gyur, respectively “the 
translated Word [of the Buddha]” and the “translated commentaries.” The 
Bonpo Canon too has a similar dual structure, but with certain notable dif-
ferences. To begin with, the first part is not conventionally known as Kanjur 
but simply as Ka (Tib. bKa’), meaning “the Word [of Shenrab],” while the sec-
ond part is known as Katen (bKa’ brten), “that which relies on the Word.” In 
both the Buddhist and Bon cases the Kanjur/Ka is considered to be the actual 
teaching of the Buddha Shakyamuni or of Shenrab, although we should note 
that the Ka does contain the major biographies of Shenrab; the inclusion is 
justified insofar as these biographies contain extensive teachings given by the 
master. We shall return to respective structures of the Buddhist Kanjur and 
the Bonpo Ka presently, but turning for now to the Katen we see that there is a 
more significant difference between these two corpora. To begin with, whereas 
the Kanjur comprises Tibetan translations of Sanskrit commentarial works, 
the Katen consists of works by Tibetan scholars. Moreover, the content and 
composition of the Katen is highly variable. The term does seem to be rela-
tively old, since it was applied to a quintet of works dating from the eleventh or 
twelfth century;5 but since in that context it denoted a set of just five works it 
could in no way be seen to have the scope of the Buddhist Tenjur. However, in 
1998, Sogde Tulku Tenpai Nima—the third incarnation of the illustrious Karu 
Drubwang Tenzin Rinchen (1801–1860)—published a corpus of Bonpo texts 
amounting to over 300 volumes and containing some 2000 different works. 
Another such collection, also described as “Katen” but containing fewer works 
than its predecessor, was published more recently, but it should be understood 
that the name is slightly misleading in that it implies the existence of some 
sort of coherent corpus of writings, whereas they are both indiscriminate 
4	 Martin 2003, 2–3.
5	 Karmay 1998, 124–25; Martin 2003, 3, fn. 8.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 69 of 332 ---
35
The Mardzong Texts in Relation to the Bon Canon
compilations of a huge diversity of works from across many centuries. This is 
not at all to diminish the value of these collections; on the contrary, the assem-
blage of such a heterogeneous range of materials covering a considerable span 
of time is of immense value for our understanding of what the category “Bon” 
can be seen to embrace, at least from the relatively cautious and conservative 
perspective of the adherents of Yungdrung Bon, the form of the religion that 
hesitates to stray too far from the standards of orthodoxy that it shares with 
Buddhism. Since the works with which this chapter is concerned are all con-
tained within the Ka, in that they are held to belong to the doctrine of Shenrab 
Miwo himself, the Katen, for all the richness of its content, need not detain us 
any further here.
The Buddhist Canon is conventionally referred to as the “Three Baskets”, 
Tripitaka, an allusion to the receptacles that contained the scriptures of the 
Pali Canon of Mainstream Buddhism. In the latter these “baskets” are the 
Vinaya—teachings on monastic discipline; Sutra, the “threads” of discourse in 
which the Buddha delivered his doctrine, and the Abhidharma, the section on 
metaphysics that was held by certain early schools, notably the Sautrāntikas 
(a name that means “ending with the sutras”) to be apocryphal. In the Tibetan 
Kanjur the “baskets” denote Vinaya, Sutra and Tantra. Broadly speaking, there 
are three systems for the classification of the scriptures that are considered to 
belong to Yungdrung Bon—that is, the teachings attributed by the Bonpos to 
their founder, Shenrab Miwo. First, a threefold grouping into “Outer, Inner and 
Secret” (phyi nang gsang gsum); secondly, the “Four Portals and the Treasury, 
the Fifth” (sgo bzhi mdzod lnga); and finally, a scheme that organises the doc-
trines into a series of nine vehicles, the “Nine Ways of Bon” (bon theg pa dgu), 
which itself has a number of variant forms. The organisation of the Canon itself 
corresponds only in general terms to these classificatory schemes. Whereas the 
Buddhist Kanjur has three main divisions, the Bonpo Ka has four, as follows:
1.	
The Perfect Class of Sutras (phun sum tshogs pa’i mdo sde)
2.	
The Pure Class of “the Hundred Thousand” (rnam par dag pa’i ’bum sde)
3.	
The Wonderful Class of Mantras (rmad du ’byung ba’i sngags sde)
4.	
The Supreme Class of Mind (bla na med pa’i sems sde)
This classification, cited by Kvaerne,6 is found in a catalogue of the Canon 
composed by the eighteenth-century scholar Kundrol Dragpa, and though 
variants on the titles of the sections are given in other works, the divisions 
are essentially the same, and are abbreviated as: 1. mDo; 2. ’Bum; 3. sNgags or 
rGyud, and 4. Sems or mDzod. Whereas the first part of the tripartite Buddhist 
canon is devoted entirely to monastic discipline (Vinaya), in the Bon canon 
6	 Kvaerne 1998, 141.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3

--- Page 70 of 332 ---

Chapter 3
the Vinaya (Tib. ’dul ba) is subsumed in the mDo section along with a number 
of other works—most notably, for the purposes of the present chapter, the bi-
ographies of Shenrab Miwo and the Klu ’bum. The second part, ’Bum, contains 
several works of varying length on the subject of the Perfection of Wisdom. 
Since the single largest text retrieved from the Mardzong caves was precisely 
a work in this category we shall have more to say about it presently. The third 
part comprises the tantras, and is therefore known sNgags (mantra) or rGyud 
(tantra). The Mardzong collection contains parts of a groups of works from 
this section. The final part of the Canon, Sems (mind) or mDzod (treasure) 
comprises works relating to rDzogs chen, the “Great Perfection,” which the 
Bonpos regard as the highest of their doctrines. In the fivefold “Four Portals 
and the Treasury, the Fifth” scheme it corresponds to the Treasury, and in the 
nine-vehicle classification it constitutes the ninth way, which is called bla na 
med pa, “supreme.” No works in this category were found in the Mardzong 
collection. Some Bonpo Canons—such as that which formed the basis of the 
Oslo cataloguing project—contain a fifth section of unclassified works. Some 
of these, such as a collection of funerary works entitled Mu cho’i khrom ’dur, 
literally “The great funerary rite (’dur) performed by Mu cho [ldem drug] in the 
public place,” are also to be found in the Katen collection.
The justification for this quadripartite classification of the Bonpo scriptures 
contained in the Canon is to be found in a very early source. This is the mDo 
’dus, the oldest and shortest of the three biographies of Shenrab, and it appears 
in an episode where the Master himself explains how his teachings should be 
organised:
Well, then, gather the scriptures. Now, after my passing beyond suffering, 
divide them up into these four: mDo, ’Bum, rGyud and mDzod. My fol-
lowers will accomplish the collecting of the Bon [scriptures]. Teach ac-
cording to the Five Perfections [which are] the perfections of Teacher, 
Place, Audience, Time and Bon [teachings]. Take confidence in the Bon 
[scriptures] of the mDo collection. The Bon [scriptures] that have been 
collected in ’Ol-mo Gling [and their] explanations inscribe in pothi (po ti).7
1	
The Khams chen
As stated above, the most extensive work in the Mardzong collection belongs 
to the ’Bum section, which deals with the Perfection of Wisdom and therefore 
corresponds to the Mahāyāna Buddhist Prajñāpāramitā. We were very fortunate

that the cache of materials in the cave contained a folio that is evidently the 
introductory dedication from this work. Such dedications are placed at the 
beginning of texts, but in terms of content they are essentially the same as 
colophons (which appear at the end of works) insofar as they contain more or 
less detailed information about the circumstances under which the transcrip-
tion of the text in question was made. As mentioned in an earlier chapter, it is 
thanks to this dedication that we know that the transcription was carried out 
in Bonkhor, a short distance to the northwest of Lo Monthang, during the reign 
of the second king of Lo, A mgon bzang po, and therefore in the mid-fifteenth 
century. A full translation of the dedication is given in chapter 10, but here it 
is worth examining a passage that concerns the text itself. Following an intro-
ductory section of praises to divinities and lineage masters, the text continues:
This precious aggregate (i.e., the present volume), from which every-
thing arises was transmitted pervasively from the mind [of the Teacher] 
to his body and speech by miracles and by skilful means, which admits 
easy access to all the vehicles, this sovereign of discourse, instructions 
and essential teachings, [that presents] conditioned as well as uncondi-
tioned phenomena, the two truths of [absolute and provisional reality] … 
(lacuna) … like the Udumbara flower or a precious jewel: this collection 
of scriptures that established the Eight Domains (of spiritual realisation) 
was promulgated by the Buddha gShen rab, the foremost one of the doc-
trine, at the palace of Mt Meru, at the [divine peak, rtse mo] of lHa ri spo 
’thon, in the Island of Heaped-up Precious things; in the Island of Clear 
Light; at Ba ra ti ta, on the shores of Lake Mu le khyung; on the Island of 
the Great Ocean, and in the Seven Unchanging Families. And to [the dis-
ciples named] Yid kyi khye’u chung; rTo bu ’bum sangs; gSal ba ’od ldan; 
Med khams stong pa rje; Tshangs pa gtsug phud; Klu mo Ma ma ti; gTsug 
gshen rgyal ba; gSang ba ngang rings, [he transmitted the teachings of 
the following eight realms:] Srid pa gleng bzhi; Rin chen rgyun; sNang ba; 
sTong pa rje; So sor thar pa; Rig pa; (lacuna, in which the last two of the 
Eight Realms would have been mentioned: dByings khams and mNyam 
pa nyid kyi khams). These were the Eight Realms that he taught. Thus 
the teaching forms sixteen volumes, in 81 chapters [subdivided into] 360 
sections and 1,001 subsections. To express it in terms of words, that would 
come to many thousands of millions, and because there are 100,000 qua-
trains it is known as “The Myriad” (’Bum).
In this way the dedication identifies and describes the work to which it was 
once attached and summarises the story of its transmission. It is clear from this 
passage that the work in question is the sixteen-volume Khams brgyad (Eight

sTong khams: Med khams stong pa rje

dByings khams: gTsug gshen rgyal ba
mNyam khams: gSang ba ngang ring

2	
The Ka ’dus
The “teacher” and “spiritual friends” in question, like the author of the colo-
phon himself, remain anonymous. In addition to the sixteen-volume Khams 
brgyad, the Mardzong repository was found to contain another work from the 
’Bum section of the Canon. The title page is missing, but it is identified in the 
left margin as Ka ’dus. It is not clear which of the works listed in the Canon cat-
alogue this might correspond to. The penultimate available folio, 444v, marks 
the ending of chapter 80, and although the chapter that subsequently begins 
must be 81, we have no way of knowing if it is the last in the work. All folios are 
marked with a ka in the margin, indicating that the work is in a single volume 
(unless subsequent volumes are missing). The closest correspondence is with 
a middle-length version entitled Khams rtsa rnam nges, which also contains 
81 chapters spread over two volumes of the second edition of the Canon, re-
spectively vols 70 and 67.1. A version of this work that seems to be even more 
similar to the Mardzong text is one that was published in India under the title

Khams rtsa nges pa’i mdo9 in two volumes and containing 82 chapters.10 The

Both of these Perfection of Wisdom texts, the Khams chen (Fig. 3.1) and the 
Ka ’dus (Fig. 3.2), are considered to have been teachings of Shenrab or other di-
vine figures that were concealed as treasure-texts (gter ma) and subsequently 
revealed by gShen chen klu dga’ in 1017. Although the Khams chen is the longest 
extant work in this category, the dedication mentions another text:
As for the Bye ma nyi khri chu ’bum, it is known as the “‘gShen rab’ 
Quatrains”; this collection of the Eight Realms [of realisation] is known 
as the “Hundred Thousand Million-Word Summary”.
The sobriquet “‘gShen rab’ Quatrains” (gshen rab zhes pa sho lo ka) may refer to 
the recurrence of the name “gShen rab” in each of the many verses of this. In 
any event, although the existence of the work is attested it “is said to be no lon-
ger extant, and perhaps was never discovered. Although it is numbered among 
the texts in the 35 treasure caskets that gShen-chen Klu-dga’ left behind …, it 
is said to have been extracted from the Red Chorten at bSam-yas by the Two 
Bande.”11 According to Khenpo Tenpa Yungdrung Rinpoche, the work is said to 
have comprised between 40 and 50 volumes.12

text, mDo rgyud chen—the “Great teaching that is the mDo,” and the fact that 
it is in two volumes, might at first suggest that this is a reference to the gZer 
mig, a two-volume work (though a few single-volume versions are known) that 
is commonly referred to simply as mDo, literally “sutra.” The Mardzong cache 
does indeed contain part of the first volume of the gZer mig; however, for rea-
sons that are discussed in chapter 10 below, it is in fact far more likely that the 
text in question is one of several possible two-volume Perfection of Wisdom 
texts, such as the bKa’ ’dus or the gDal ’bum rtsa ba (Fig. 3.3).
The gZer mig is one of the three main biographies of sTon pa gshen rab, 
whom the Bonpos regard as the founder of their religion. The shortest, and 
probably oldest, version, in one volume, is the mDo ’dus, which probably dates 
from the eleventh century, and the largest is the twelve-volume gZi brjid, which 
was written in the second half of the fourteenth century. The date of the gZer 
mig is uncertain, but since there is a reference to the work in a commentary 
to the Khams brgyad (or Khams chen—see above) by Tshul khrims dpal chen

(1052–1106), it must already have been in existence at that time.13 There are 
some grounds for an even earlier date than this. The work is classified as gter 
ma, and is said to have been rediscovered near Samye monastery by a certain 
Drang rje btsun pa gSer mig. According to Martin:
The dates of the gter ston [treasure-discoverer] are uncertain, but from 
historical accounts he seems to have lived somewhere between the end 
of the ninth century and the beginning of the eleventh.14
After the Klu ’bum—about which we shall have more to say below—the gZer 
mig was one of the first major Bonpo works to attract the attention of Western 
scholars. Of its eighteen chapters, their first seven and a part of the eighth 
were published in English translation by A. H. Francke, from a manuscript 
that had been brought to Europe by Austin Waddell, and that is now kept in 
the Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin.15 Francke’s translation was published as “gZer-
myig, a book of the Tibetan Bonpos: Tibetan text according to the manuscript,” 
in seven numbers of Asia Major from 1924 to 1949.16 In his Religions of Tibet 
(1961), Helmut Hoffmann announced a forthcoming translation of the work, 
but beyond the chapter summaries given in the same book the translation was 
never realised.17 Other studies of the gZer mig are summarised by Martin,18 
but to these we should add a more recent work that undertakes a comparative 
examination of aspects of the three main biographies and traces the Buddhist 
sources on which parts of them are based. This is Kalsang Norbu Gurung’s “The 
Emergence of a Myth”, an unpublished PhD thesis19 that is concerned primar-
ily with the mDo ’dus but also contributes substantially to our knowledge of 
the gZer mig.
The gZer mig is a work of eighteen chapters, and it is fairly clear that the vol-
ume of the that forms part of the Mardzong cache is the first of a two-volume 
set. Apart from the fact that the letter ka that appears on the recto of each folio 
suggests that this is the first volume of a work comprising two or more volumes,

The Klu ʼbum
The Klu ’bum is one of the best-known Bonpo works because it is probably 
the only scripture of the religion that is used—albeit in a somewhat modified 
form—by Buddhist schools. As remarked by Martin et al., “This general popu-
larity would explain why quite a number of copies of various parts of it, mainly 
in manuscript form, exist in public and private collections around the world, 
and no attempt has been made to trace them all.”21
20		
mDo gZer mig 1991.

Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

--- Page 79 of 332 ---

The Mardzong Texts in Relation to the Bon Canon
Broadly speaking, the Klu ’bum is divided into three parts: dkar po, nag po 
and khra bo, “white, black and variegated” (henceforth Klu ’bum WBV),22 al-
though these parts do not correspond exactly to the three volumes in which 
they are usually contained. In fact, the first version that is said to have been 
discovered (in Samye, by three Indian acaryas, in the tenth century) comprised 
four volumes. As Martin et al. observe, the location of the work is not consis-
tent across the different versions of the Canon, appearing in either the mDo or 
the ’Bum section, and in one case in both.23
Western scholarly attention to the Klu ’bum dates from the nineteenth cen-
tury, with two seminal works respectively by Anton Schiefner and Berthold 
Laufer.24 A summary of subsequent scholarship on the Klu ’bum is given in 
Martin,25 but mention should also be made of the work carried out by Rolf 
Stein, as recorded mainly in the Yearbook of the Collège de France for the years 
1966 to 1970.26 At the time of writing, a comparative study of the structure 
and content of most of the known manuscript and blockprint versions of the 
work is being undertaken by Bazhen Zeren in the framework of a PhD at the 
Ecole Pratique des Hautes Etudes in Paris, and it is hoped that the results of 
this investigation will help to clarify our understanding of the transmission of 
this work.
In his overview of the various known versions of the Klu ’bum, the Bonpo 
scholar Khri bde mchog identifies five main versions of Klu ’bum WBV: rTag 
brtan phun tshogs gling (Taten), dGa’ ldan phun tshogs gling, sMan ri mon-
astery, sDe dge par khang, and the version contained in the Bon bKa’ ’gyur 
published by Kun grol lha gsas.27 The first two—the earliest versions—may be 
dated to the 17th or 18th century. The text in each case contains three volumes 
that follow the usual classification of the work as White, Black and Variegated. 
The second and third contain a great number of myths related to the relation-
ship between klu and human or other beings. The white volume, by contrast, 
provides only a very brief introduction to the sa bdag, gnyan, and each of five 
classes of klu, and the promulgation of the doctrine by sTon pa gShen rab 
among these entities. It also gives examples of the kind of damage the klu can 
inflict in retaliation when they are harmed by others. In order to appease the 
22		
For the sake of convenience, the trilogy will be referred to simply as Klu ’bum WBV, denot-
ing white, black and variegated, and the individual volumes as Klu ’bum followed by the 
corresponding letter.

Chapter 3
klu and to restore normal relations, sTon pa gShen rab teaches a series of pro-
cedures for making a mandala offering to them.
To these versions we may add a manuscript of the Klu ’bum W from the vil-
lage of Dzar, in South Mustang, that was documented by the editors of the pres-
ent work. A comparison of the six versions reveals that the content does not 
differ greatly. All comprise fourteen chapters but with a different number of 
chapter and bam po. The two versions of the Klu ’bum WBV that Rolf Stein used 
in his research on the text were Taten and Dege. He found that archaic sen-
tence structures were preserved more faithfully in the former than in Dege.28 
However, it seems that the newly-discovered version from Dzar features ar-
chaic vocabulary, orthographic and other features to an even greater extent.
Among the manuscripts discovered in Mardzong, there are over 300 folios 
that clearly belong to Klu ’bum W. On the basis of the numbers of lines on each 
folio, as well as distinctive codicological features, illuminations and content, 
there are at least two copies of the work, Klu ’bum 1 and Klu ’bum 2 (Figs 3.4 A 
and B). The title pages of both are missing, but the chapter titles in each identi-
fy them as the gTsang ma (sometimes rTsang ma, a more archaic orthography) 
klu ’bum dkar po. The last available folio of Klu ’bum 2 is 251, which announces 
the end of chapter 14 and of bam po 21, and is therefore the last folio of the 
volume. The last available folio of Klu ’bum 1 is numbered 242, and the last sec-
tion ending, an unnumbered bam po—almost certainly bam po 20—appears 
on fol. 232v. Although a much larger part of Klu ’bum 1 than of Klu ’bum 2 has 
survived, it is also incomplete. In spite of the many missing folios, however, 
the available evidence suggests that the chapter and bam po structure of the 
two Mardzong manuscripts is the same as that of the Dzar Klu ’bum W. Where 
modern, edited versions subdivide the chapters into an equal number of bam 
po, in the case of the manuscripts from Mustang there is no such correspon-
dence; bam po and chapter divisions do not always coincide exactly, and in 
some instances a bam po may subsume a number of chapters. The structure 
of the Dzar Klu ’bum, and almost certainly that of the two Mardzong manu-
scripts, is as follows:
	
bam po 1–4 = chapter 1
	
bam po 5, 6 = chapter 2
	
bam po 7–9 = chapter 3
	
bam po 10, 11 = chapter 4
	
bam po 12–14 = chapter 5
	
bam po 15, 16 = chapter 6
	
bam po 17, 18 = chapter 7

The orthography in both versions, but especially in Klu ’bum 1, exhibits a num-
ber of archaic features. Thus, the word gtsang ma in the title is often spelled 
rtsang ma, klu as rlu and gti mug as rti mug, while mi is palatalised as myi; dkar 
regularly appears as kar. The terms gsol, ston and the other syllables ending in 
n often add the da drag. In the name gshen rab, the second syllable is usually 
spelled rabs, which is consistent with usage in some Dunhuang texts.

from the available content that this is a compilation of dhāraṇīs. The fact that

it is the tenth volume of a set (as indicated by the marginal designation cha) 
suggests that it is part of a larger collection of works. Compilations of canoni-
cal dhāraṇīs are well known throughout the Tibetan Buddhist world, as indi-
cated in the opening lines of an article by Karénina Kollmar-Paulenz devoted

The large number of Tibetan as well as Mongolian so called gZungs bsdus 
(mong. Zungdui/ Sungdui) volumes found in libraries around the world 
bears witness to the popularity of these collections that contain mainly 
canonical texts, among them small Dhāraṇīs to ward off illnesses of hu-
mans and cattle, and smaller Sūtras that describe the iconography of the 
more popular Buddhist deities or praise the virtue of reciting particu-
lar Sūtras.29

dhāraṇī with which the compilation begins by comparison with other collec-

bya ba’i gzungs, “the dhāraṇī entitled ‘Containing divisions of all the exalted 
Buddhas’”.30 The volume deserves closer attention that might make it possible 
to determine its possible relationship to other canonical collections of such 
works (Fig. 3.5).

Chapter 4
A Codicological Study of the Mardzong 
Manuscripts
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
The numerous manuscripts of the Bon religion have yet to be clearly identified 
as a distinct corpus. They are usually written in the Tibetan language, and this 
is the reason they are considered a subset of the Tibetan corpus. Unlike the 
Buddhist manuscripts, they have not been subject to codicological, material 
research, in spite of their importance for the study of Central Asian history 
and religion. There is, however, compelling evidence to suggest that Bon de-
veloped a distinctive manuscript tradition that transcends regional and his-
torical peculiarities. This is most evident from the use of certain abbreviations, 
scripts, symbols, and other material features that are rare or entirely absent in 
Buddhist texts. The Mardzong collection consists of a mixture of Buddhist and 
Bonpo works, as well as some that are not aligned with either religion; the pre-
vious chapter dealt with the relationship between the Mardzong texts and the 
Bon canon, and this chapter will offer an independent codicological overview 
of the collection distinguishing Bon from Buddhist works, but following the 
same text order as the previous chapter.
It should be emphasised that, with the exception of one or two stray block-
printed folios in the missing part of the collection, all the Mardzong volumes 
were handwritten with a wooden pen. While Buddhism has a long and wide-
spread tradition of xylographic textual reproduction in Tibet, this has not been 
the case with Bon, which has always favoured the use of manuscripts, partly, 
no doubt, because of the lack of the substantial patronage that would have 
been required to produce the carved blocks. The majority of the handwritten 
Mardzong texts in Choede monastery are in the form of large volumes of the 
pothi (longbook) type known as dpe cha by Tibetans. They all belong to canoni-
cal works, which might possibly represent the unique examples of those texts. 
These volumes typically consist of rectangular leaves, each of which is turned 
over vertically, rather than horizontally, such that the text on the verso is writ-
ten upside-down in relation to the recto, but the text is continuous when the 
volume is opened with the verso at the top and the recto of the following leaf 
at the bottom (Fig. 4.1).
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 84 of 332 ---

Chapter 4
The gilded wooden covers and many of the folios containing illuminations 
had been removed by the time the volumes were extracted from the caves in 
2009, though fragments of what may have been the gold-lettered first folio, 
identifiable by traces of an indigo background at the torn edges, still remain 
(Fig. 4.2 A, B, C, D). The Mardzong volumes are currently wrapped in a new 
blue cloth called dpe ras (‘dpe cha cloth’) added for protective purposes when 
the books were moved from the cave. Both covers and cloth are typical ele-
ments of Tibetan bookbinding, even though Tibetan books have a loose-leaf 
format. Studies of the visual and technological features of the paper, along 
with the raw materials themselves, were performed on 129 samples from the 
Mardzong collection. It is noteworthy that it is the first collection of Mustang 
books to be subjected to systematic codicological research and material study 
(See Tables 1 and 2 in the end of this chapter).

Chapter 4
period between the fourteenth century and the beginning of the fifteenth 
century by the radiocarbon method. Volume ga (3) is dated later, to the time 
between the fifteenth century and the beginning of seventeenth century. The 
dimensions of individual folios included in the Khams chen volumes are simi-
lar, albeit not identical. All the measurements of form and layout are present-
ed in table 4.1 (Table 4.1). They range from 20.3 cm to 21.5 cm in height and 
from 64.2 cm to 66 cm in width. There is thus a variability of 1.2 cm in height 
and 1.8 cm in width. Whether these differences result from inaccuracies in 
the preparation of the leaves, or whether individual groups of volumes might 
have been made at different times or by different people is unclear.1 Despite 
some differences, however, the technique of execution, the composition of the 
page and the type of writing materials used indicate that the volumes belong 
to the same collection of Khams chen produced in Mustang or the neighbour-
ing areas. At the same time, differences may signal the replacement of some, 
perhaps destroyed volumes, with newer, later volumes.2 It is also important 
to note that such differences in measurements also occur between particular 
folios in the same book.3 The full edition would be prepared by a large group 
of craftsmen, scribes and artists. Despite the obvious codicological unity of the 
set, it should come as no surprise, even within the same set, that individual fea-
tures are not evident in individual texts. Some folios in the volumes, however, 
differ distinctly from others in terms of handwriting and other codicological 
features, although they conform with the continuity of the text. It remains a 
mystery whether they are replacements for original, damaged book fragments 
or whether they originally belonged to different sets of the same section of the 
canon produced in Mustang.
Tibetan canonical books are typically large folios with neat and elegant 
script centrally positioned on the page. However, just as the size of the fo-
lios in the Khams chen may differ, so does the page layout (Table 4.1). Here 
differences in the dimensions of margins and distances between circles vary 
even more than the sizes of the leaves do. All Khams chen volumes feature 
the ‘headed’ Tibetan dbu can script in a frame drawn with a single or double 
red line on the first few folios, and then between side margins drawn with the

same implement and in the same colour in subsequent leaves. The number of 
lines of text per page usually increases over the first few pages beginning with 
a lower number of lines of text. The first five folios were sometimes missing 
and in these cases confirmation of the number of lines of text per page was not 
possible. Incompleteness notwithstanding, with the exception of the first fo-
lios featuring two, five, six or seven lines of text per page, volumes tend to have 
eight lines of text per page. The only exception to this is the colophon (or dedi-
cation), where there are fifteen lines of text per page. The distinctive character 
of the colophon derives from the fact that in neither Tibetan Buddhist nor Bon 
books do colophons tend to exceed one folio. The layout is thus separately de-
signed so the colophon page contains all the necessary information for a given 
edition. This is also probably the reason the colophon features no red circles 
as a part of its layout. When dealing with the layout of Khams chen volumes it 
is worthy of note that the average number of lines of text per page in Tibetan 
books tended to range from five lines of text per page to nine, although in the 
course of time six lines of text per page became customary.
Other typical elements of the book form that are present in Khams chen 
volumes are circles drawn with red ink in places where traditionally a string 
would be pulled in Indian books made out of palm leaves, a form that the 
Tibetan longbook reproduces in paper. The folios of Indian books tended to 
be bound together with one or two strings that passed through their entire 
thickness. Tibetan books have no such strings, but some books, especially cer-
tain larger-format categories of canonical works, have two small circles drawn 
on each folio as a sort of pious vestige of the ancestral design. Unlike their 
Indic models, Tibetan dpe cha were generally unbound. In terms of strings and 
string holes, one observes a general movement away from function towards or-
namentation before they are eventually largely abandoned. These holes were 
often ringed, so that the scribe would avoid writing in them. This way of or-
namentation forms an initial part of the page layout and was generally added 
to the page at the same time as the inking of its guidelines and margins. The 
string hole has become truly ornamental in its absence, when only the circles 
remain, but the string hole formerly at its centre was no longer punched. Even 
these ornamental circles eventually fell largely by the wayside and ceased to be 
an element of the dpe cha layout (Fig. 4.3). Such circles are present in all Khams 
chen volumes with the exception of a few folios at the beginning of volumes 
ta (9), tha (10) and the colophon.
The writing surface measuring approximately 12 × 60 cm is in the centre 
with more or less equal margins at the top (4.5 cm) and the bottom (4.5–5 cm) 
and left and right margins (5.5 cm). The side margins are usually wider than 
the top and bottom margins. There are no fixed proportions and the width of

the margins in relation to the writing surface on particular folios varies. The 
distance between guidelines varies from 1.4 cm to 1.9 cm in Khams chen vol-
umes. They are inked, but this is often faint, or in a thin ink such that it is 
barely visible (Fig. 4.4 A). They are sometimes marked with little holes as seen 
in figure 4.4 B (Fig. 4.4 B).
The folios contain Tibetan foliation placed in the left margin perpendicular 
to the text, written, as text, in dbu can script. A fragmented guideline leading 
the heads of Tibetan lettered numerals is sometimes visible on some folios 
(Fig. 4.4 A). The use of the “+” sign for the designation of one hundred leaves, 
on the other hand, is a characteristic element rarely present in books from re-
gions other than Mustang. The use of “+” is, however, inconsistent and is to be 
found in only part of the collection (Figs 4.5 and 4.6). Numerals written solely 
in Tibetan letters are also found.

Chapter 4
Traditionally one finds illustrations containing drawings of buddhas, bodhisat-
tvas, important masters or sacred objects such as stūpas on the verso of the 
title page and on the last page or pages of a dpe cha. One commonly finds 
either two illustrations per page, one on each side inside the writing surface, 
or one illustration in the centre with text to the left and right. Here all Khams 
chen volumes are illustrated; each usually containing between two and ten 
illuminations.4 The iconography depicts local deities, lamas, holy places and 
patrons. The composition of the images and the way figures are presented are 
standardised in pictures throughout all volumes. This suggests that the same 
template was used for most of illuminations. Figures therefore tend to be com-
posed centrally and embraced by the shaded and decorated nimbus with ha-
loes above their heads. The figures differ mainly in terms of posture, colour, 
mudra and attributes, such as sword, vajra, lotus flower, stupa, rosary. The posi-
tion of the paintings was marked on the page at the same time as the layout 
was prepared (Fig. 4.7).5 Images were then painted in layers of flat colours and 
finally marked with a black outline sketched in carbon ink in either thick or 
thin lines (Figs 4.8 A and B).
The images are painted in stages: first, the sky; then the background; then 
the earth, and finally the foreground. The painting of the figures tended to be 
done by order of colour. The first colour to be applied would be dark blue. 
This is due to the working properties of azurite, the usual dark blue pigment, 
which tends to smudge and require burnishing.6 The next colour to be applied

outline standard iconographies. This is not the case, however, in the Khams chen volumes, 
which are hand-drawn.

is green (malachite), followed by light orange (minium), and then pink. Some 
colours, including pink, are produced both from mixing pigments and from or-
ganic dyes from plants. Deep orange (minium), red (vermillion), yellow (orpi-
ment) and skin tones are then applied. White and gold are the last flat colours 
to be applied. After the flat colours have been applied outlining takes place 
by applying outlines to the edges of forms. This is usually done with the two 
main organic dye colours: indigo and brownish red lac. In Khams chen vol-
umes, however, black ink was used to outline other forms in quite an unskilled 
hand, which gives the impression of being cheap and hasty. Black ink tended 
to be used to colour the hair of the figures. Other features may be observed that 
suggest that the quality of painting is casual and sloppy, such as the lines of 
yellow that frame the miniatures, which are freehand and clearly not straight. 
There is also the observable effect of heavier layers of paint, when a thicker 
coat is applied. This is characterised by a less subtle blending of colours. This 
feature of painting is, however, in general more typical of the Western and 
Central Tibetan style rather than the Eastern styles such as the Karma Gardri 
(16th century) and the Menri (17th century), where a colour would be applied 
in many thin layers.7 In the Khams chen volumes a wet shading method was

used. This is done by blending together two pools of wet pigment with a third, 
clean brush. This may also be done with organic dyes. The last step was the 
application of gold ornamentation called ser ri. Gold lines were painted over 
figures, marking their crowns, jewellery and attributes hold in hands.
Both the dimensions and the positions of these illustrations vary. This is 
interesting because in the Tibetan editions of the Buddhist canon illustrations 
tend to be placed either side of the text in a fixed layout. Such vertically sym-
metrical composition is found in vol. ga (3) on folio 358 depicting two stupas 
(Fig. 4.9). In most other volumes, however, the illustrations are incorporated 
in different places of the text and have different dimensions (Fig. 4.10). This 
type of composition of miniatures may appear in books from Western Tibet.

In some cases, the first leaves of the book include geometric decorations in 
the side margins instead of miniatures with representations of characters, as 
shown in vol. ta (9) (Figs 4.11 and 4.12).
The manuscripts also contain other features characteristic of the Bon reli-
gion. An example is the use of an inverse swastika in the text (the arms point-
ing anticlockwise), one of the main symbols of the Bon religion. The swastika 
tends to be a solar symbol or a symbol of prosperity, happiness and truth. 
Here swastikas were used as editorial marks. Annotations and additions to the 
text were thus signalled by the inclusion of this sign in either red or black ink 
(Figs 4.13 and 4.14). When there is more than one annotation per page, how-
ever, marks other than swastikas would be used, as seen on folio 59 recto from

the Khams chen vol. cha (6) (Figs 4.15 and 4.16). Other marks more universally 
used in Tibetan books and found in the Khams chen volumes include begin-

low paint, as may be seen on folio 171 verso from the Khams chen vol. ta (9) or

is a textual caret mark in Tibetan script, a possible marker for a new text or 
page. There are, however, many variations of this mark, such as a double, triple 
or quadruple yig mgo, depending on whether it is the main title page, internal 
title page, or regular page (Fig. 4.17 A, B, C).
The seals indicating ownership were found in two volumes. Examples can 
be seen in volumes ka (1), kha (2) and ca (5) (Figs 4.18, 4.19 and 4.20 A B). At 
this stage, however, we were unable to identify the owners of these seals or the 
provenance of these volumes.

The use of black and red ink in Mardzong manuscripts is often significant. As 
well as frames, margins and circles, we find red ink used for marking text, an-
notations and marking off a volume number with Tibetan letters (Fig. 4.21). 
Red ink is sometimes used for some corrections and seals. In Mustang, the red 
seal seems to have been reserved for the exclusive use of the king.
All Khams chen volumes were written on hand-made paper composed of one 
or two layers heavily polished on the surface (Figs 4.22 and 4.23). The majority 
of paper used in the Khams chen was made with a floating mould equipped 
with a woven screen which may be discerned from the structure of the paper 
observed against light (Figs 4.24 and 4.25). However, a singular folio 348 on laid 
paper was detected in volume ta (9). The thickness of the folios varies signifi-
cantly, which clearly indicates that the paper was made using simple ­methods.

Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

--- Page 100 of 332 ---
66
Chapter 4
Some sheets of paper were uneven, and gluing sheets of paper together made 
the final leaves even more uneven in thickness.8
Examinations under the microscope of 99 samples taken from all sixteen 
volumes of the Khams chen and colophon folio revealed the presence of two 
types of fibres, but in different proportions in particular samples. (Table 4.2) 
The first type of fibre was narrow with typical broad sections stained olive-grey 
with Herzberg stain; with both lumen and fibre walls varying in width (some-
times with scalloped fibre walls); with clearly visible dislocations and cross-
markings; pointed, blunt, rounded, spatulate fibre ends; and parenchyma cells 
present in the pulp. On the basis of such characteristics these fibres were iden-
tified as Daphne, Edgeworthia, or Wikstroemia spp. Bearing in mind the tra-
ditional plants used for making paper in this western part of the Himalayas, 
however, one may assume that those fibres were probably from Daphne bholua 
plants. The other type consisted of ribbon-like fibres with a very wide, irreg-
ular lumen and thin irregular fibre walls identified as Stellera chamaejasme 
(Figs 4.26, 4.27 and 4.28).
2	
The Ka ‘dus
One volume of the Mardzong collection is part of a Perfection of Wisdom 
text entitled Ka ’dus. It is composed of 143 folios measuring 22 × 65.5 cm. It 
is written in Tibetan dbu can script well calligraphed in eight lines of text per 
page at a distance between the guidelines varying from 1.5 to 1.8 cm. The text 
is composed between two side margins drawn approximately 5 cm from the 
sides. The writing surface measures 10 × 56 cm. The upper and lower margins 
measure 5.4 cm and 6 cm respectively (Figs 4.29 and 4.30). Unlike the Khams 
chen folios it does not feature the circles as a part of its layout. The volume’s 
recto sides contain foliation in the left margin. The volume number is marked 
in black rather than red ink.
The Ka ’dus paper comprises layers and ranges from 0.11 mm to 0.45 mm in 
thickness. There is 0.11 mm variability in thickness within one page on average 
when thickness is measured at five points per page. The surface of the paper 
was processed and highly polished before being written on (Fig. 4.31).

0.12–0.46 mm; vol. ga (3) 0.15–0.4 mm; vol. nga (4) 0.15–0.58 mm; vol. ca (5) 0.17–0.49 mm; 
vol. cha (6) 0.09–0.42 mm; vol. ja (7) 0.13–0.4 mm; vol. nya (8) 0.14–0.48 mm; vol. ta (9) 
0.11–0.34 mm; vol. tha (10) 0.14–0.4 mm; vol. da (11) 0.11–0.58 mm; vol. na (12) 0.15–0.38 mm; 
vol. pa (13) 0.16–0.44 mm; vol. pha (14) 0.16–0.38 mm; vol. ba (15) 0.14–0.38 mm; vol. aa (30) 
0.15–0.35 mm.

This paper is handmade with a woven type of mould equipped with a fabric 
sieve. When observing it against light it is characterised by an uneven distribu-
tion of fibre with fibre bundles and impurities present in the pulp (Fig. 4.32). A 
large amount of outer bark (dark particles) indicates that the raw material was 
not cleaned thoroughly and it was insufficiently cooked (Fig. 4.33). The fibres 
were therefore not loose enough and are still visible in bundles, giving the ef-
fect of unevenly thick and thin places in the paper structure.
Examination under a microscope of six samples taken from the Ka ’dus folios 
showed the presence of fibres of Stellera chamaejasme mixed with Daphne sp.

3	
The gZer mig
The gZer mig, a canonical text probably composed in the eleventh or twelfth 
century, tends to be in two volumes. The version found here, dated to the 
thirteenth century, consists of only the first volume. This Mardzong volume 
comprises 178 folios written in the Tibetan dbu can script. Like all previously 
described volumes the gZer mig is in loose-leaf form with folios measuring 
22 × 65.5 cm. The text is written with a wooden pen in black ink and composed 
in a full frame drawn with a double red line. The first folios feature no circles, 
but they appear from folio 13. Throughout the rest of the volume the text is 
written between two side margins also sketched with a double red line. The 
number of lines of text per page rises over the first folios, beginning with two, 
rising to three, finally reaching eight lines of text per page recorded throughout 
the rest of the volume (Figs 4.34, 4.35 and 4.36).

The gZer mig page layout is characterised by a writing surface measuring 
11 × 51 cm, side margins 5.5 cm and upper and lower margins 5 cm and 4.5 cm 
(Figs 4.37, 4.38). The text was written in between six and eight lines per page 
with guidelines at a distance of 1.5 cm. Like the Khams chen volumes, the gZer 
mig contains foliation in the left margins of each recto side and there are no 
illustrations (Fig. 4.38).
This volume was written on paper comprising up to three layers.9 It was 
hand made with a woven type of paper-making sieve made of cloth and

Microscopic examination of six samples taken from the gZer mig folios 
showed the presence of fibres of Daphne sp. mixed with Stellera chamaejasme. 
The rigid fibres with broadened portions, an angular shape, scalloped with 
rounded and spatulate endings, as well as irregular cross-markings in a “v” 
shape were identified as Daphne, and the ribbon-like fibres with a very wide, ir-
regular lumen and thin irregular fibre walls were identified as Stellera chamae-
jasme. Crystals and glue particles were often present in the pulp.

4	
The Klu ʼbum
There are two volumes of the Klu ’bum text in the Mardzong collection. One 
(1) contains 189 folios measuring 21.2 × 53.5 cm, and the other (2) 186 folios 
measuring 22 × 67 cm. The volumes have been dated by the radiocarbon meth-
od to different times. Klu ’bum 1 is dated to the fourteenth century, and Klu 
’bum 2 is dated to the thirteenth century. Both volumes are written in Tibetan 
dbu can script. Unlike the above-described volumes of the Khams chen, the 
Ka ’dus and the gZer mig, which feature maximum eight lines of text per page, 
the text of Klu ’bum 1 is written in seven lines per page, and Klu ’bum 2 in six 
lines per page at maximum. The writing surfaces measure 12 × 44.3 cm and 
12.5 × 58 cm respectively. The text on the beginning pages is composed in a dis-
tinctive multi-coloured frame between 0.5 cm and 1 cm wide in Klu ’bum 1, and 
a 0.4 cm wide frame in brownish paint in Klu ’bum 2 (Figs 4.40 and 4.41). The

The guidelines, 2 cm apart in both Klu ’bum volumes, were made in diluted 
brownish ink in Klu ’bum 1, and by touching the folio with a string soaked in 
brownish dye in Klu ’bum 2. They are more visible on some folios than on oth-

the left margins perpendicular to the text (Figs 4.46 A and C). Some folios, 
however, also contain short phrases in the right margin (Fig. 4.46 B). These

witness. The right margin of this folio contains text in Tibetan dbu can script, 
literary Sangs rgyas la phyag in the left, and in dbu med script, literary Chos

There were 12 illuminations painted in flat colours found in Klu ’bum 1. 
These may be illustrated by folios 55, 56, 196 and 227 (Figs 4.49 A, B, C and 
D). They contained figures predominantly of local Bon deities, important mas-
ters, and votive scenes, as seen on folio 227 (Mchod pa ’bul ba) in blue and red 
colours. The images are of varying sizes and composed in different positions. 
Two illustrations sometimes appear per page, one on each side on the writing 
surface (Fig. 4.50). There is occasionally one small illustration in the centre or 
somewhere on the page with text to its left and right. The figures are outlined 
in a black inked contour (Fig. 4.51). The damage to layers of paint as a result of 
the thick-coating application technique appears as deep cracking through the 
one thick layer (Figs 4.52 A and B). Such damage is more common here than in 
illustrations in the Khams chen volumes. It is also clearly visible that damaged 
illuminations were later painted over, possibly aiming at filling in a missing 
layer of paint (Fig. 4.52 B).

Chapter 4
Unlike the painted illuminations that usually illustrate Buddhist canonical 
works, the doodle drawings were documented in the margins of Klu ’bum 2 
(Fig. 4.53). These are mainly animals, birds, figures possibly involved in rituals, 
and symbols, such as umbrellas, swastikas, and swords (Figs 4.54 A–G). These 
sketches are untypical of canonical volumes, which tended to be illustrated 
in accordance with the strict principles of Tibetan art. Interestingly, we find 
many such simple drawings in Dunhuang manuscripts.11 They are more like 
exercises, personal drawings executed in black ink with a wooden pen. These 
drawings are composed freely in various parts of the margins.
Klu ’bum 2 must have been frequently used judging by the many repairs vis-
ible throughout the volume. Interestingly, the torn paper is often sewn in such

Buddhist dhāraṇī Volume
The preserved Mardzong collection contains only one volume with a compi-
lation of dhāraṇī texts belonging to the Buddhist, and not the Bon Canon. It 
is handwritten in Tibetan dbu can script with a bamboo or wooden pen. It is 
a manuscript on paper in loose-leaf form (dpe cha) dated to the period be-
tween the fourteenth and fifteenth century by the radiocarbon method, the 
same time as part of the Khams chen volumes. This volume contains 139 fo-
lios measuring 15.5 × 65 cm. It is written in four lines of text per page at the 
beginning rising to seven lines per page throughout the rest of the volume 
(Figs 4.60–4.62). The page layout (measured at page 2) is characterised by a

Chapter 5
Comparative Study of Paper Found at 
Archaeological Sites in Mustang
Emilie Arnaud-Nguyen and Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
The objects that archaeological excavations bring to light inevitably bear wit-
nesses to the past, but they are usually unearthed without much by way of 
context. Given that paper decomposes relatively quickly, it is usually of pe-
ripheral interest to archaeologists. This lack of both availability and interest 
has resulted in a dearth of references for comparative studies, which together 
with a lack of established methodology has made archaeological paper arte-
facts especially difficult to describe meaningfully. Should there happen to be a 
context, as in the case of Mustang, such material is especially valuable to stud-
ies. These paper fragments were found together with other archaeological ar-
tefacts in the caves of Mustang, located along the famous ancient Kali Gandaki 
trade route by Mark Aldenderfer’s team from the University of California, 
Merced, USA. The Kali Gandaki river cuts south through the Himalayas form-
ing a route that for centuries carried people, goods and religions between the 
Himalayan plateau, Nepal and the Indian plains.1 This route also constituted 
access to India from China. There were thirteen access roads that traversed the 
highest Himalayan passes (Fig. 5.1).2 Our paper samples were thus found in a 
dynamic, busy area along one such route traversing Mustang.
The archaeological paper fragments under study serve as good reference 
material for the Mardzong collection. They were found in the caves of Samar 
and Rhirhi (Figs 5.2 and 5.3). Samar is a village to the north of Tsele and south of 
Geling.3 It has a red hill (hence the name—Tib. Sa dmar meaning “red earth”) 
on which stand the ruins of a convent. It is reached by a long climb after Tsele.
After the trail crosses the Kali Gandaki, it climbs steeply to the village of 
Samar, considered the wettest and greenest place in Mustang. The Samar frag-
ment was found in a cave just to the south of the village where other ritual 
artefacts had been abandoned, such as arrows and spindles, leftovers from ran-
som rituals, and a protective amulet, painted on paper, which was left there. 
1	 Harrison 2010, 26.
2	 Jest 1975, 35.
3	 Tsele is also sometimes written as Chaile, Geling as Gelung, and Rhirhi as Rele.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

Chapter 5
The collection of other fragments we studied was found in the Rhirhi Cave 
outside Chusang, located to the south of Samar, also in Upper Mustang, Nepal. 
These caves were primarily used as burial chambers dating back to 800 BCE, 
as two different types of ceramic objects from that time (800 BCE) were found 
there. Other objects were also excavated: firstly ritual exorcism-related vo-
tive figurines made of undated clay and pieces of woven fabric dated to circa 
1000 CE, dyed blue and some also in natural colour.4 Some other fragments 
bearing short mantras or series of unidentifiable words were preserved in 
Chusang Guest House which is located among some trees on the south side 
of the stream. These artefacts found during excavations are now stored at the 
Police checkpoint in Chusang waiting to be moved to a museum.
4	 The textile artefacts were examined by Dr Margarita Gleba, a specialist at Cambridge 
University.

101
Comparative Study of Paper Found at Archaeological Sites
1	
Links between Paper Dating and Manufacturing Process
The samples studied were dated using the radiocarbon method in accordance 
with the highest probability density method and the method of interception.5 
Analyses dated the Samar cave paper to between the late thirteenth century 
and the late fourteenth century and the Rhirhi samples to between the late 
fifteenth and the early seventeenth century. The Samar and Rhirhi paper 
samples were produced at different times, so they belong to different time 
­periods in paper-making history. The process of paper-making in Himalayas 
is the result of years of development and a great deal of experimentation. 
The technology and skills disseminated from China and Tibet, thus reached 
Nepal along the east-west axis.6 Paper-making in Mustang developed under 
the influence of both Nepal and Tibet. The first Nepalese writing materials 
were tālapatra (palm leaves) in accordance with the Indian tradition,7 bhoja-
pattra (birch bark), kārpāsikapatra (rags), wooden boards, pieces of bamboo 
and animal hides. The first two have a religious, spiritual and ritual meaning. 
Nepalese paper-making technology is said to have begun between the elev-
enth and the thirteenth centuries, which means that the Samar cave samples 
were produced when paper-making technology first reached Nepal. The use 
of palm leaves lasted until the fifteenth century, but this decreased, whereas 
manuscripts written on paper increased. From the middle of the seventeenth 
century the number of manuscripts on paper exceeded the number of manu-
scripts on palm leaves. The papers found indicate that the fragments from the 
Samar cave originate from a time when the use of paper was a good deal rarer 
than the use of palm leaves and those from Rhirhi from a period when paper 
manufacture was a growing industry. Paper manufacture increased greatly, not 
only as manuscripts but for all kind of purposes (wrapping paper, medicine, 
printing, cards, kites, etc.). Interestingly, the Mardzong volumes dated to the 
thirteenth and fifteenth centuries were produced during these very same two 
windows of time.
5	 Further explanations of the method are available on the CEA (French Atomic Energy Centre) 
website: CEA, n.d. (accessed on 21.12.2018).
6	 Konishi 2013, 3.
7	 Konishi 2013, 56.

2	
Characteristics of the Samples Studied
To the naked eye the colours of the paper ranged from creamy white to brownish 
(Figs 5.4, 5.5 and 5.6). Four fragments are made up of at least two layers,8 whilst 
the thickness of the other samples suggests that the paper is either thicker or 
consists of a number of layers glued together, but this cannot be determined.9 
The particular hue may be explained by various factors, such as the thickness 
of the paper, the presence of pasting, or the state of conservation, which is in-
fluenced by the environmental conditions of its manufacture and storage. We 
cannot ascertain the extent to which the colour is a result of the paper-making 
process or cellulose degradation. The colour of the paper therefore lends itself 
neither to dating nor other historical conclusions. Pulping processes that rely 
on low-alkaline products, such as ash lye, and hand-pulp beating usually bring 
about impurities in the pulp, such as small pieces of bark, which are a charac-
teristic of paper from Nepal (Fig. 5.7). These features are visible in transmitted 
light. The majority of the fragments consist of at least two layers (Fig. 5.8).10
Broadly speaking, the surface of the paper looks smooth and shiny despite 
a slight irregular texture. This effect may be caused by the finishing process,

the floating mould used in archaic paper-making.13 These floating moulds are 
usually a rectangular frame on which a cloth is stretched and pinned (Fig. 5.9).
It is set above water and pulp is scooped with a measuring device and manu-
ally spread evenly on the cloth (Fig. 5.10). The water is then drained for a few 
minutes. The paper-making technology involves an observable fibre dispersion

Process of polishing paper with a bamboo 
stick with a smooth stone fixed to one end
Photograph by Gajurel and Vaidya 
(1994)
This step is very specific to the preparation of leaves of paper for the produc-
tion of manuscripts. Generally, starch paste is made from wheat, rice or bar-
ley flour15 depending on local availability.16 Polishing might be done with a 
bamboo stick, at the end of which might be fixed a rounded mineral stone 
(Fig. 5.12).
This bamboo stick is attached to the ceiling and the paper sheet is buffed 
with it until the surface is evenly smooth and glossy. Paper may also be buffed 
by a variety of other tools, such as a conch shell, an iron tool or a smooth stone 
such as agate. This finishing process was usually carried out so that the paper 
might be written upon. In our sample we observe the results of polishing in 
five papers. Seven fragments show at least two layers, which may indicate that 
the paper was intended to be written on both sides. Nonetheless, paper with 
only one layer may also have been used as a writing material. The Rhirhi P1.2 
sample, for instance, is a single-layered fragment with ink and we can even see 
the remnants of a binding thread (Fig. 5.13). The picture also shows a slightly 
uneven, shiny surface. The differences in colour on that sample may be ex-
plained by the presence of coatings, the number of layers, contact with other 
materials and the process of degradation. Through a Dino-Lite microscope we 
can clearly see the presence of layers: a final coating and the polishing process 
(Figs 5.14 and 5.15).

3	
The Process of the Degradation of Paper
Like all organic material, paper degrades with time. The process varies accord-
ing to the many causes, but is eminently observable in fragments. A darker 
browning and a higher rigidity may be observed in multiple-layered fragments 
due to a greater abundance of starch paste, which may act as a catalyst accel-
erating the process of degradation (Fig. 5.4). We may also observe the presence 
of external elements in most of the samples, such as sand, dust and even feath-
ers inlaid in the paper fibres (Fig. 5.16). These come from the samples’ poor 
storage conditions, stored as they were on the cave floor. The Rhirhi samples 
are delaminated, while Klu ’bum 1, Klu ’bum 2 and Samar cave samples dis-
play a fluffiness, a loss of condition and flaking of the paste (Figs 5.5 and 5.8). 
A great deal of further damage was caused to the samples by environmental 
storage conditions. Some small holes, for instance, may have been caused by 
insects or mould. As seen in figure 5.16, some papers were indeed eaten by 
insects. We observe typical Lepismae damage which leaves a furrow-shaped 
abrasion on the surface of the paper and traces of mould may be seen under 
UV light as micro-organisms become fluorescent (Fig. 5.17). Both Nepalese and 
Tibetan papers are said to have been of good quality and durable thanks to 
the natural poisonous properties of Thymelaeaceae plants, which protected 
them from mould and pests. Examination of the paper, however, contradicts 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University
