Chapter 5
that assertion. We may wonder whether that is due to the paper-making pro-
cess, the presence of starch or whether that property naturally diminishes with 
time. It must be added that paper and starch paste are sources of nourishment 
for insects and micro-organisms.
A high relative humidity, the lack of air circulation, the accumulation of 
dust and dirt, the absence of noise and vibrations may combine to favour the 
proliferation of insects. Paper also degrades because of its main components, 
plant fibres. Fibres are actually composed of cellulose, hemicellulose and lig-
nin (Fig. 5.18). Lignin lends rigidity to the stem and the fibres are held together 
by pectins. The amount of each element differs for each plant and causes dif-
ferent types of paper degradation.17 D. G. Suryawanshi and O. P. Agrawal, on 
the basis of their research, came to the conclusion that the amount of each 
element varies according to the plant species rather than the passage of time.18
Paper degrades in accordance with two main chemical processes: oxidation or 
hydrolysis mainly caused by the acidity of the paper. Acidity is expressed as a 
pH value linked to the amount of lignin fibres (a non-cellulose element, far less 
17		
To read more about the effect of lignin and pectin on the degradation of paper, please 
consult Inaba and Sugisita 1992, 5.
18		
Suryawanshi and Agrawal 1996, 69.
Figure 5.18	
Presence of feather in Rhirhi P1.0 observed under 
Dino-Lite digital microscope
Photograph by Emilie Arnaud-Nguyen (2018)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 145 of 332 ---
111
Comparative Study of Paper Found at Archaeological Sites
stable than cellulose, which degrades when exposed to air).19 The alkalinity of 
paper may derive from the residue of chemical products used in the cooking of 
the fibres.20 The process of degradation by acid leads initially to discoloration, 
firstly to yellow and then to brown, or darkening and to a dislocation of cellu-
lose fibres insofar as they lose their mechanical resistance and cohesion.21 On 
contact with water or acidity the cellulose decays by hydrolysis.22 The loss of 
mechanical resistance is a slow process due to the material hygroscopic prop-
erty leading to the swelling of the fibre through the absorption of humidity 
and the contraction of fibre when releasing it. As a consequence, paper will 
eventually lose its elasticity and resistance, and its appearance will change, 
as we observe in our samples.23 The degradation caused by insects, rodents, 
birds and micro-organisms is slow and cumulative. We must also consider 
the importance of temperature, relative humidity and the presence of light, 
dust and vegetation, as they may accelerate the process of degradation and 
promote the development of micro-organisms or insects, which represent the 
main threat to paper. Micro-organisms develop in a relatively dark and humid 
environment (around 55% relative humidity) and insects are generally at-
tracted by light (with some exceptions for those who eat paper). Insect larvae 
need food to grow, so they are the main causes of damage to paper. They eat 
vegetable products; silver fish eat paper or vegetable glue; the book louse eats 
paper, starch, protein and micro-organism (25–30°C, over 77HR); and beetles 
(Dermestidae) are attracted by starch, but they also eat wood or paper (23°C 
70%–90%). The reason a material decays may thus be either natural and with 
immediate and disastrous effect or slow and cumulative, and human interven-
tion may also play a part.
19		
The pH is a measure of the molar concentration of hydrogen ions in the solution and as 
such is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of the solution. The usual range of pH values 
encountered is between 0 and 14, with 0 being the value for concentrated hydrochloric 
acid, 7 the value for pure water (neutral pH), and 14 being the value for concentrated 
sodium hydroxide. According to the French Chemical Society, lignin is one of the main 
components of wood with cellulose and hemicellulose. Lignin acts like cement between 
fibres and also like a solidifying element within fibres. It is located in the adjoining part 
between cells, which strengthen the cohesion and mechanical resistance. Lignin is prob-
lematic in the paper industry because it is responsible for the yellowing of paper because 
of inter alia exposure to light.

Olmer 1942, 24.

Lepeltier 1977, 50.
22		
Lienardy and van Damme 1989, 62.

4	
Fibre Analysis
As early as in 1903 Julius Wiesner emphasised the difficulty in identifying the 
genera of plants from these papers by examination with a microscope because 
the manufacturing process causes a great deal of damage to fibres.24 The fibres 
used by Nepalese people come from the Thymelaeaceae family, where many 
features are so similar that it may not be possible to differentiate between 
plants’ genera (Fig. 5.19). 
Today’s microscopes, however, are much better than they were in 1903 and 
we can discern some characteristic features in order to identify fibres: thin fi-
bres with irregular cross-markings of which some are “v” shape; a thin lumen 
and thick walls; broadened portions; a wide variety of endings, such as round-
ed endings with a variety of shapes (spatula, forked, scalloped, scimitar-like); 
and the presence of crystals are typical (Figs 5.20 and 5.21). These are very com-
mon in the analysis of fibres and may come from soil plant nutrients, from the 
24		
Wiesner 1903, 432; Rischel 2001, 179–188.

Scientific classification of plants from Thymelaeaceae family based on 
Ferrari 2002

Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

paper-making process or even from storage conditions, such as dust or sand 
from the cave floor. Such crystal elements may be distinguished, as they shine 
under polarised light (Figs 5.22 and 5.23). Anna-Grethe Rischel explains that 
plants of the Thymelaeaceae family are relatively rich in pectin, which is an 
amorphous substance coating the fibres (Fig. 5.24).25

Chapter 5
The samples are made from the Daphne and Edgeworthia species, both of 
which are characteristic of both Nepalese and Tibetan paper-making and 
come from the bast of shrubs. These plants were first identified as integral 
to paper-making in an article written by Nathaniel Wallich in the early nine-
teenth century.26 Tibet, however, seems to have been the first to use Daphne, 
Wikstroemia, Stellera and Edgeworthia. All these plants grow widely in the 
Nepalese mountains where papermakers lived. Analyses of the Upper Mustang 
papers indicate that the same combination of fibres was used there. They were 
found in an area of ancient Tibetan cultural importance, as defined by Jest, and 
we may assume that they were made there or imported from Tibet.27 With the 
exception of Rhirhi 2 all samples contain a small amount of Stellera 
26		
Wallich 1820.
27

Comparative Study of Paper Found at Archaeological Sites
chamaejasme, fibres of which were taken from the roots of the plant, and 
which suggests that those papers were rather made in Tibet or Mustang than in 
southern valleys of Nepal (Fig. 5.25).28 It has a characteristically wide, irregular 
lumen with thin walls which produces a flat fibre. Species of Daphne grow at 
an altitude of up to 3500 metres above sea level, whereas Stellera develop along 
the Himalayan range at an altitude of between 2600 metres to 4500 metres. 
Daphne also requires more humidity than Stellera.29 In some regions fibres 
from these species were thus blended from the outset. As paper is damaged 
and subject to ageing, the same is true for fibres. The scale of degradation is de-
termined by observing the length, rigidity and shape of the fibres, as well as the 
variety of the ends of the fibres and the presence of external agents. We may 
discern fibres with a higher degree of deterioration (Rhirhi 1 and the Samar 
cave) from a medium degree (Rhirhi 2, Rhirhi P1.0, Rhirhi P1.1, Rhirhi P1.2, gZer 
mig, Mardzong Klu ’bum 1 and Mardzong Klu ’bum 2) and from a lesser degree 
(not observed in Mardzong cave samples).
5	
A Higher Degree of Deterioration
A higher degree of deterioration is characterised by shorter and more rigid fi-
bres (Fig. 5.26). Two samples show these characteristics: the Samar cave sample 
and Rhirhi 1. These papers were stored on a cave floor for centuries, subject to 
variations in climatic conditions. Other samples, however, were subject to the

Fibres suffering from a medium degree of degradation show the same features, 
long, thin fibres mixed with shorter, more rigid fibres (Fig. 5.28). Even if many

Rhirhi 0 1.1 and Rhirhi P 1.2.

to identify (Mardzong Klu ’bum 1 and the Samar cave). These samples were

coloured with Herzberg stain in order to try to determine whether the fibres 
come from the same plant family. The fibre was found in the Mardzong Klu 
’bum sample, which may suggest that its presence are accidental. The fibre is 
defibrillated, which means that it may be pulped (Fig. 5.29).30 Herzberg turned 
the fibre purple, whereas others remained green (Fig. 5.30). This means that 
the fibre comes from a vegetable source different from other fibres used in the 
manufacture of paper, but also from the other unidentified fibres. Herzberg 
stain also enables us to observe more accurately the lumen, which is thick and 
flat. Further research is required in order to find the plant from which the fibre 
comes. It may then be possible to assess whether its use in the manufacture of 
paper was intentional and local or accidental.
On the other hand, unidentified fibres in the Samar cave fragment are pres-
ent in greater quantity. The Samar cave fragments contain long, thin, rigid fi-
bres (Fig. 5.31). We observe the regular presence of so-called “knees” and small 
“wings” along the fibre (Fig. 5.32). Herzberg stain causes no change in colour, 
but it reveals a large irregular lumen. More extensive study is required in order 
to ascertain from which plant these fibres came and to determine whether its 
use in the manufacture of paper was intentional or accidental.

along the fibre. So it is said to be fibrillated.

--- Page 154 of 332 ---
120
Chapter 5
7	
Conclusion
The papers from Upper Mustang caves follow the traditional Nepalese and the 
Tibetan paper-making processes and use traditional raw material, i.e. Daphne, 
Edgeworthia, and Stellera used only by Tibetan communities. The presence of 
unidentified fibres suggests that some details remain to be studied. As these 
plants are sparse, their use can be said neither to have been widespread nor 
common and may correspond to the local attempts in paper-making technol-
ogy. Their ageing process may be observed with the naked eye and under the 
microscope. It corresponds to the natural mechanism of paper degradation, 
but it has been accelerated by poor storage conditions provided by cave floors. 
Further study of the fibres is required in order to identify them and provide 
information about less traditional raw materials used in Tibetan and Nepalese 
paper-making.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

Chapter 6
Missing Manuscripts from the Mardzong 
Cave Repository
Charles Ramble
1	
The Context1
This chapter will pick up a part of the story of the Mardzong manuscripts that 
was touched on in the preface to this book: the contents of a bundle of texts 
that went missing after the collection had been moved to Choede monastery. 
As a reminder to the reader, we may summarise briefly the background to their 
disappearance. During the expedition of 2009, following a discussion with 
the main decision-making bodies of Lo Monthang, it was concluded that the 
manuscripts should not be left in the caves, since erosion of the cliffs was in-
creasingly exposing them to the wind, as several stray folios lying on the river 
bank in the valley floor clearly testified. It was agreed that the best course of 
action would be to remove all the material from the caves, and to store it in the 
Choede, a proposal to which the management of the monastery consented. 
Although the texts were to be removed to what everyone assumed would be 
the safe keeping of the monastery, it was nevertheless felt that some record 
should be kept of what was to be put into storage. However, the volume of 
material ran to several thousands of folios, and it would have been impossible 
to photograph each one in the very limited time available. It was clear, how-
ever, that the majority of the texts belonged to large canonical works com-
prising several volumes, and since published versions of these works existed, 
there was less urgency to photograph these than the many remaining folios of 
which the identity was unknown, and which might possibly represent unique 
examples of those works (which was indeed later found to be the case). Works 
that could be identified as canonical were then taken to another tent where 
two people who were able to read Tibetan could organise the folios into piles 
corresponding to the volumes in which they belonged, though not in the prop-
er order within each volume. As far as possible, the folios of most of the un-
identified texts were taken to a third tent, where three people with cameras 
1	 I am indebted to Helmut Tauscher for his valuable comments on an earlier draft of this 
chapter.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 156 of 332 ---
122
Chapter 6
photographed them, recto and verso. There was no time to organise these fo-
lios into coherent volumes, but at least a basic photographic record was kept of 
a large part of the material that was not immediately identifiable. Brot Coburn, 
one of the organisers of the expedition (see preface), purchased a quantity of 
blue cotton cloth from a shop in Lo Monthang, and this was then used to wrap 
each of the volumes. All the unidentified miscellaneous material was gathered 
into a single large pile and similarly packaged. The entire collection was then 
transported up the hill to Choede.
The folios had been photographed using three different cameras. These pho-
tographs were later pooled, and over the course of several weeks Kemi Tsewang, 
a young man from Lubrak in Southern Mustang, sorted the images into fold-
ers corresponding to different works on the basis of their physical appearance 
and handwriting.2 At the end of this process, some of the folders contained 
a handful of images and others up to several dozens. A tally of the images re-
vealed that 360 items had been photographed before being wrapped up in the 
collection of miscellanea. With the exception of some badly-damaged ritual 
initiation cards (tsa ka li) and the two block-printed leaves (Mardzong AA, AH), 
all were manuscript folios. In all cases the texts were incomplete. There were 
altogether forty-seven works in varying states of completeness: some, as stated 
earlier, were represented by just one or two folios. Of the works that might 
be said to have a markedly religious content, twenty-three were Buddhist and 
thirteen Bon, and the remaining eleven would be best described as “secular” or 
“indeterminate.” As we have seen in chapter 3 above, the remaining seven texts 
comprised one Buddhist and six Bon canonical works. While the number of 
Buddhist works in the entire collection outnumbered Bon texts by twenty-four 
to nineteen, the presence of several multi-volume Bon scriptures—especially 
the sixteen-volume Khams chen—meant that in terms of sheer quantity Bon 
textual material was more abundant.
Although we have visited Choede on several subsequent occasions, it has 
unfortunately not been possible to examine the collection of miscellanea more 
closely, since that particular bundle is missing. It is to be hoped that it has sim-
ply been misplaced, and will soon be found lying unnoticed in a dark corner of 
the monastery. Pending its rediscovery, all we have to go on is the photographic 
record that was made at that time of the folios’ removal from the cave. To em-
phasise a point that was made earlier, the circumstances in which the manu-
scripts were photographed in situ were difficult. There was very little time in 
which to make the selection of folios to be photographed, and the conditions 
2	 All the photos of folios that appear in this chapter were taken by a team of people comprising 
Kristoffer Erickson, Nyima Drandul Gurung, Renan Ozturk and Charles Ramble.

Missing Manuscripts from the Mardzong Cave Repository
themselves were far from ideal: the strong wind meant that the task had to be 
carried out under cover, and the only cover available—apart from the stygian 
darkness of the caves—was the interior of a yellow tent, which resulted in all 
the texts being suffused with a sulphurous light. In view of the disappearance 
of this particular collection the existence of this basic photographic record is 
a source of considerable relief. Nevertheless, the fact that the manuscripts are 
unavailable obviously means that they are not susceptible to the types of anal-
yses that could be carried on five canonical works, as documented in chapter 4. 
This is certainly a matter of regret, since an indication of the dates of some of 
the items would enable us to situate them more clearly in the corresponding 
domains of Tibetan literature, and could contribute in significant ways to our 
understanding of the development of these fields. To take only the intriguing 
example of Mardzong K, which consists of just five folios of a much longer 
legal text: the work contains a number of codicological features that would 
normally be considered indications of its antiquity, such as the form of the 
gi gu (the diacritical vowel i) and the presence of the “superabundant ’a.” It 
is known that several archaic features that were abandoned in Central Tibet 
continued to be used in the Tibetan hinterland for centuries later. Is this the 
case with Mardzong K? The text belongs to a category of legal works known as 
zhal lce (or zhal ce), and merits comparison with other known works in this 
class. According to Dieter Schuh, the oldest known manuscript of this type of 
zhal lce compilation dates from the seventeenth century.3 Is it possible that 
the Mardzong caves have provided us with an even earlier version? This is just 
one of the many questions raised by the manuscripts. Even if the missing texts 
themselves fail to reappear, it is hoped at least that the available photographs, 
that are all accessible on the project website (see acknowledgements, fn. 1) will 
provide scholars with a basis for future research on the collection.
2	
The Missing Manuscripts: Content Description
The following section provides a description of each of the works in the miss-
ing collection based on the photos that were taken at the time. The works have 
been grouped into three categories: Buddhist, Bon and secular or indetermi-
nate. The third category includes items related to law, aphoristic literature, 
correspondence and suchlike, and although Buddhist imagery may appear in 
some of them the religious component is incidental enough that they do not 
warrant inclusion in the first group. The description of each text contains the 
3	 Schuh 1984, 297–298.

following rubrics: 1. Subject, in which the nature of the text’s content is briefly 
described; 2. Title, where available; in most cases the first folio is missing, but 
the title of the work is sometimes repeated in the chapter headings given in 
the available material; 3. Form, in which physical and codicological features 
of the text are noted. Unless otherwise indicated—and this applies to only a 
handful of items—the texts all have a longbook (dpe cha) format; and finally, 
4. Number of available folios. Note that in the identification of folios, the scrib-
al convention of using “+” to signify hundreds has been retained; thus “+++10” 
signifies fol. 310.
3	
Buddhist Works
3.1	
Mardzong A
3.1.1	
Subject
A text, purporting to be a tantra, identified in the opening line by the Sanskrit title 
(rgya gar skad du) Rad na bhan dha mo rtsa na ma (i.e. Ratna-bandha-mocana 
[nā]ma), followed by the Tibetan Rin po che bcings ’grol gyi rgyud ces bya ba, 
“The so-called tantra of the liberation from the bonds of the precious items” 
(Fig. 6.1). The text is effectively a manual of poisons, presented as a discourse by 
Mahādeva. The text presents various categories of poisons, with information of 
how they should be administered, and also lists their antidotes. Fol. 1r is blank 
except for simple doodles and short writing exercises, apparently in a later 
hand. The absence of a title may be intended to avoid advertising the nature of 
the contents. According to Péter Szantó, the work is not canonical.4 A transla-
tion and discussion of this text are given in chapter 11 of the present volume.
3.1.2	
Form
Handwritten in an ‘archaic’ dbu med, with some dbu can features, such as the 
diacritic na ro and gi gu. The script is an archaic form of dbu med. Other ar-
chaic features include: the form of the yig mgo on fols 6r, 9r and 10r; the ‘nega-
tive’ yig mgo on fol. 1v; the frequent use of the syllable ṇa (reverse na) as an 
abbreviation for med, “not”, as in srog can dang / srog ṇa (i.e. med), “things 
with life-force and things without life-force”, fol 1v, line 8. Number of lines is 
inconsistent: fol. unnumbered r and v: 7 lines; fol. 1v 8 lines; fol. 2r 9 lines; 2v: 
8 lines; fols 6–10: 8 lines. Fols 1v and 2 r contain short passages in red ink. Some 
passages are marked with a transparent orange highlight, probably an infusion 
of calendula.
4	 Péter Szantó, personal communication, 28 February 2018.

Mardzong B
3.2.1	
Subject
sKyed rim (utpattikrama)—commentarial (not canonical) text for the genera-
tion of divinities during meditation (Fig. 6.2).
3.2.2	
Form
Written in a rough tshugs script, with nine lines per folio. The fact that not 
much attention has been given to calligraphic elegance, and that little margin 
space has been left on any of the folios, suggest that this manuscript was pro-
duced for pragmatic purposes, as instructions for consultation. To the left of 
the marginal folio number on each recto side is the syllable kye, probably an 
abbreviation for skyed rim.
3.2.3

Subject
Text for the practice of Guru yoga, i.e. visualisation of the lineage of lamas and

ma’i rnal ’byor or Bla ma’i sgom rim (Fig. 6.4).
3.4.2	
Form
Six lines per folio side, written in a square dbu med script, with short descend-
ers. Marginal folio numbers are given in both figures and words, followed by 
the syllable bla.

3.5	
Mardzong L
3.5.1	
Subject
“Songs of experience” (nyams mgur) of an unidentified lama (Fig. 6.5).
3.5.2	
Form
Two folios, written in dbu med tshugs thung script, with 8 lines per folio side. 
The fact that the marginal folio numbers are preceded by the syllable ka may 
suggest that these folios from the first volume of a multi-volume set. However, 
in certain older manuscripts a system of counting is used whereby the mar-
ginal syllable ka accompanies folio numbers from 1 to 100, kha appears in those 
numbered [10]1 to 200, and ga [20]1 to 300. Separate texts within a single vol-
ume may also be identified by sequential letters of the alphabet.5
3.5.3	
Folios Available
2, 4.

3.6	
Mardzong M
3.6.1	
Subject
Precepts (gdams ngag) and preliminary practices (sngon ’gro) attributed to 
Sakya Pandita Kunga Gyaltsen (Sa skya Paṇḍita Kun dga’ rgyal mtshan, 1182–
1251) (Fig. 6.6).
3.6.2	
Form
The script is an archaic form of dbu med. There are seven lines of text per folio 
side. Fols 2v, 5v and 6v contain rubrication on the phrase bla ma dam pa’i zhabs 
la phyag ’tshal lo (I prostrate to the perfect lama’s feet), with which successive 
sections begin. Variations in duct thicknesses indicate (for example on fol. 5r) 
that different styli were used, though the scribe is apparently the same. The 
marginal folio numbers are preceded by the syllable kha, for the possible sign-
ficance of which see notes to Mardzong L.
3.6.3	
Folios Available
2, 3, 5, 6.
Figure 6.6	 Folio 5 recto of Mardzong M
3.7	
Mardzong N
3.7.1	
Subject
According to the concluding line of the work (fol. 12v), written in red ink, the 
text consists of teachings on Hevajra given by the Sakya lama Drakpa Gyaltsen 
(Grags pa rgyal mtshan, 1147–1215) in response to a request by an unknown 
patron. Drakpa Gyaltsen was the grandson of Khön Könchok Gyalpo (’Khon 
dKon mchog rgyal po), the founder of the Sakya school, and the nephew of 
Sakya Paṇḍita (Fig. 6.7).

3.7.2	
Title
bCdoṃs (bcom ldan ’das) dpal kyai’i [rdo’i] mngon par gtogs pa’i rim pa.
3.7.3	
Form
The syllable rdo’i in the title, written in pale ink in dbu can script, is a later in-
sertion below the line. Kyai’i rdo’i signifies Kye rdo rje, that is, Hevajra, and the 
text is therefore an iconographic visualisation (mngon rtogs) of this divinity. 
The name appears as Kyai’i rdo rje on fol. 1v. The title is framed in a rectangular 
box with an outline of double red lines. In addition to the title, fol. 1r has three 
lines of scribbled mantras in a later hand, as well as doodles of a triple gem, a 
parasol and a bird. The other folios have seven lines of text per side. On fol. 2v 
there are empty boxes to the left and right of the text, apparently intended for 
illuminations. The outer edges of the boxes are decorated with chu ris motifs. 
Lines 3–4 of fol. 12r contain a phrase in red ink.
3.7.4	
Folios Available
1, 12.

3.8	
Mardzong O
3.8.1	
Subject
Part of a work belonging to the category ’Khor lo’i gzungs ’jug: sacred formulae 
for insertion into stupas, prayer wheels etc. (Fig. 6.8). Information is given on 
the recto of fol. “unnumbered”, a folio that appears to be an accompanying set 
of brief instructions that is not part of a sequence with the other folios. This 
folio opens with an invocation (na mo gu ru) and the text ends on the recto 
side; the verso is blank. The text specifies that it is to be used for the “subjuga-
tion of male and female yakshas” (gnod byin pho mo mnan pa).

3.8.2	
Form
The script is a rather rough headless tshugs thung, with a varying number of 
lines per folio side. Fol. unnumbered r: seven lines; fol. 4r, 4v: eight lines; fol. 8r: 
six lines; fol. 8v: seven lines. The irregularity of the number of lines and the 
general scruffiness of the handwriting suggest that this is a hastily-made copy 
intended for practical use.
3.8.3	
Available Folios
Unnumbered, 4, 8.
Figure 6.8	 Folio 4 recto of Mardzong O
3.9	
Mardzong P
3.9.1	
Subject
Part of a text dealing with the manufacture of protective amulets, specifying 
the location of divinities at different points of the body (Fig. 6.9).
3.9.2	
Form
Written in a rough ’khyug ma tshugs script; the second folio, numbered drug 
’og ma, “fol. 6, part 2”, is a supplement to the missing folio 6. The handwriting 
here, albeit probably by the same scribe as that of fol. 5, is more condensed 
and there are more lines per folio side, probably in an effort to condense all the 
supplementary information into a single folio. The number of lines per folio 
therefore varies: fols 5r and 5v: seven lines; fols 6 ’og ma r and 6 ’og ma v: nine 
lines. On fol. 6 ’og ma, immediately after the yig mgo, is a line drawing of an eye. 
Since the preceding folio is unavailable its significance is unclear. Since the 
first term of the line, rtsa’i, could account for the first two syllables of a nine-
syllable line, and the line makes sense as it stands, the drawing is apparently 
not intended to stand for a syllable and may be purely decorative.

Subject
Part of a manual of physical yogic techniques (rtsa lung ’phrul ’khor) (Fig. 6.12).

an illegible contraction presumably representing a short form of the title of the 
work. There are six lines per folio side.

3.13	
Mardzong T
3.13.1	
Subject
Part of a text concerned mainly with procedures in the performance of 
rituals—in this case, secret tantric rituals (Fig. 6.13). The work therefore be-
longs to the zin ris category. The opening, superscribed line on fol. 1r states: 
Thob yig bzhugs lags. The term thob yig denotes a list of teachings received, and 
the opening passage provides information about the author’s teacher and the 
circumstances under which he received the instructions contained in the later 
part. The instructions for the ritual itself begin on fol. 2r, line 4.
3.13.2	
Form
See notes to Mardzong S. Each folio side has six lines. Below the margin folio 
number on fol. 1r is the syllable thob, for thob yig; below the number on fol. 2r 
is the syllable gtor, an abbreviation for gtor ma, a reference to the ritual with 
which the text is concerned. Below the folio number for fol. 5r we again see the 
syllable thob: between fols 3 and 5—on the missing fol. 4—the text has again 
changed from being a ritual manual to being a list of teachings received.

Mardzong AR
3.22.1	
Subject
Part of a text containing a prayer composed by a certain Gelong Drakpa 
Gyaltsen (dGe slong Grags pa rgyal mtshan) in response to a request by a nun 
that she should never be separated from her teacher in future lives, and that 
this should be her last incarnation as a woman (Fig. 6.22). It is uncertain which 
of several possible historical figures by the name of Drakpa Gyaltsen is meant 
here. However, the hand in which this text is written appears to be the same 
as that of Mardzong N, containing a teaching attributed to the twelfth-century 
Drakpa Gyaltsen who was the nephew of Sakya Pandita.

which may or may not be intentional. The folio number is there for either 10 or

Bon Works
4.1	
Mardzong C
4.1.1	
Subject
A ritual text for the praise and invocation (stod pa) of the Wrathful Divinities 
(khro bo) of Bon, principally Trowo Tsomchog Khagying (Khro bo gTso mchog 
mkha’ ’gying) (Fig. 6.24). This is not one of the canonical tantras relating to 
Trowo, but a ritual based on them. There is no title page or colophon, but it 
may be possible to identify the particular work by comparison with ritual texts 
for this divinity in other collections.
4.1.2	
Form
dpe cha, written in a simple dbu can script. Fol. 100v bears a few interlineal 
glosses in dbu med script, representing Tibetan translations of Zhangzhung 
terms that feature in the text. The first available folio has the marginal number 
ka gsum (vol. 1, fol. 3), and has five lines of text both recto and verso. The next 
folio is marked ka lnga (vol. 1, fol. 5). From here on most folios have seven lines 
of text. The last available folio has the marginal ka + go lnga (vol. 1, fol. 95). One 
folio is marked ka + yang cig, probably signifying vol. 1, fol. 101 supplement. The 
folio bears two discoid marks on the lower margin that may be seals.

4.4	
Mardzong E
4.4.1	
Subject
A canonical Bon tantric treasure-text (terma, gter ma) dealing mainly with 
the divinity Trowo Tsomchog Khagying (Khro bo gTso mchog mkha’ ’gying) 
(Fig. 6.27).
4.4.2	
Title
gSas mkhar rin po che spyi spungs gsan ba bsen thub de kho na nyid gsang ba’i 
rgyud, aka rGyud gSang ba bsen thub. The work appears in the Bon Katen, 
vol. 193, no. 7, pp. 817–1073. It is classified as a gter ma discovered by Rigdzin 
Gyermi Nyiö (Rig ’dzin Gyer mi nyi ’od [12th century]).
4.4.3	
Form
Most folios have nine lines of small ‘archaic’ dbu med, with some dbu can fea-
tures, such as the diacritic na ro and gi gu and the form of the consonant m. 
Fol. 1r has only four letters, all in Zhangzhung script. Fol. 1v carries the short 
title, a roughly-written gSang nga bseṇ thub bzhugs, followed by eight lines 
of text. The first line contains a section of Zhangzhung script followed by the 
words zhang zhung skad du, “In the language of Zhangzhung”, followed in turn 
by the transcription of this text into Tibetan script. The second line begins 
with the phrase bod skad du, “In the language of Tibet”, followed by a Tibetan 
rendering of the Zhangzhung text, which is the full title of the work. Some syl-
lables at the ends and openings of sections, as well as interlineal phrases and 
some shad punctuation marks are written in red ink.

Mardzong F
4.5.1	
Subject
A Bon canonical tantric text concerned principally with the Benign Divinities 
(zhi ba) (Fig. 6.28).
4.5.2	
Title
No title appears in the available folios, but the work seems to be part of the Kun 
’dus rin chen rtsa ba’i rgyud, which appears in the Katen, vol. 191. It is classified 
as gter ma, and is said to have been discovered accidentally by three Indian 
acāryas at Samye monastery.
4.5.3	
Form
Twelve folios in dbu med script, usually ten lines per folio side. Fol. 6r and 6v 
have respectively six and seven lines of widely-spaced text with extensive in-
terlineal annotation. Marginal folio numbers consist of numbers written as 
figures for fols 6 and 7, and of abbreviated words for higher numbers (presum-
ably above 9; fols 8 to 14 are missing). One folio is numberd bco lnga gong ma, 
“upper fol. 15”, suggesting that the scribe was attempting to preserve the folio-
number of an original that he was transcribing. Fols 17v, 36v, 37r, 37v contain 
punctuation marks in red ink.

4.7	
Mardzong X
4.7.1	
Subject
A tantric text connected with the divinity Trowo Tsomchog Khagying (Khro 
bo gTso mchog mkha’ ’gying) (Fig. 6.30). Texts X, Y and Z are three tantras that 
belong to a set of six works collectively entitled Khro bo dbang chen gyi spa gro 
yi rgyud drug, “Six tantras of Khro bo dbang chen from Paro”, a reference to the 
location in Bhutan where they were discovered as gter ma by Khutsha Dawö 
(Khu tsha zla ’od; b. 1024). The six tantras are collected in volume 193 of the 
Katen. Texts X, Y and Z correspond respectively to the second, first and sixth 
works in the collection.
4.7.2	
Title
Title page missing, but the title is repeated in subheadings, e.g. on fol. 39r: 
gSang ba dgu’ ’phar g.yung drung gsang ba’i rgyud. This work is contained in 
the Katen, vol. 193 pp. 173–329.
4.7.3	
Form
There are eight lines of text per folio side. The script is dbu med with a number 
of archaic features, such as the frequent use of the reverse gi gu, the super-
abundant ’a, and the form of the consonant ph, which resembles the dbu can 
form but with the diagonal stroke almost on the horizontal.
4.7.4	
Available Folios
1, 3–7, 9, 11, 16, 19, 23–25, 30, 32, 33, 37–39, 40, 42–46, 56, 63, 68–71, 75, 77, 81–85, 
98, +13, +25, +30, +33, +34, +39, +40, +44-+46, +49, +50, +67, +67 ’og ma, +72, +85, 
+88, +96, ++2, ++3, ++9, ++12, ++15, ++16, ++18-++22, 26, ++27, ++29, ++31, ++39, 
72, ++83, ++86.

4.8	
Mardzong Y
4.8.1	
Subject
See notes to Mardzong X.
4.8.2	
Title
Given on fol. 1v and repeated in certain chapter headings as: lTa ba khyung 
chen g.yung drung gsang ba’i rgyud (corresponding to Katen vol. 193, no. 1, 
pp. 1–172) (Fig. 6.31).
4.8.3	
Form
Fol. 1r contains six lines of text. The middle part of the folio is badly damaged 
by water. The text to the left is also damaged and unreadable. It appears to be 
in a different ink that was less water-resistant. Line 3, which is only a few sylla-
bles long, may be an interstitial insertion. Lines 1 and two are headed (dbu can) 
text in red ink. The visible sections of lines 4 and 5 are in headless script (dbu 
med). The other lines appear to have been written in black ink. The fact that 
the red-ink inscriptions on lines 1–2 and 4–5 appear not to have anything to do 
with the text, and that their content is apparently identical, suggests that all 
the writing on the page may be nothing more than a blank folio side being put 
to use for scribal exercises. Fol. 1v begins with a “negative” yig mgo followed by 
two short phrases in Zhangzhung script, and the corresponding text in Tibetan 
script, announced as “the language of Zhangzhung” (zhang zhung skad du). 
There are eight lines of text per folio side, in black ink, in ‘archaic’ dbu med.

5	
Secular or Indeterminate Works
5.1	
Mardzong J
5.1.1	
Subject
Mardzong J is an unusual example of a category of Tibetan literature known as 
michö (mi chos) (Fig. 6.37). A full transliteration and translation are provided 
below in Chapter 10. Literally “religion of humans”, the term michö seems to 
have two rather different meanings. On the one hand it may have signified a 
code of laws that applied to non-aristocratic laity during the Tibetan imperial 
period (7th to 9th centuries); and on the other, it designates a genre of aphoris-
tic literature comprising wise sayings and maxims for sensible living. It is in the 
latter sense that the term is used here. While the tenor of the aphorisms is quite 
familiar from other works, what is more interesting is the framework in which 
they appear. The absence of the first folio means that we lack the scene-setting 
that would surely be there, but the context is clear from the surviving material. 
The structure belongs to a genre that is known as bstan chos, a term that in 
a Buddhist context translates the Sanskrit shastra, “commentarial literature”, 
but in the present case is best glossed as “follower’s speech”. The setting is a 
gathering—probably a feast to celebrate a military victory or the building of a 
temple—of high-ranking aristocrats of the imperial period. The aphorisms are 
presented as a series of discourses uttered by a number of eminent nobles. 
Each discourse opens with a brief introduction to the speaker, “Then so-and-
so spoke the following words,” and closes with a declaration by the speaker 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 188 of 332 ---
154
Chapter 6
of the personal achievements that have given him the authority to speak. Six 
individuals are named, in the following order: the great ministers (blon chen) 
Gar Tongtsen Yulsung, Ngam Tara Lugong Nakpo and Na Chenpo; Chim Dorje 
Trechung, minister Thönmi Sambhoṭa, Cokro Klü Gyaltsen, Nanam Kyi Düjom, 
Nanam Trompakye, Lönchen Lhabu Gokar and Cang Asö.
The names of nearly all are well-known from early Tibetan sources (al-
though the actual spellings of some of the names differ from the more famil-
iar renderings).6 The first speaker whose name has survived is Gar Tongtsen 
Yulsung (590–667), a towering figure of early Tibetan history who was a loyal 
servant of the emperor Songsen Gampo and also served his two successors. 
In view of the fact that the others are speaking in descending order of rank, 
it is probably safe to conclude that the speaker immediately preceding Gar 
was none other than Songtsen Gampo himself. Should we take this text to be 
a genuine record of a particular court gathering? Certainly not: although some 
of the figures are known to have been members of Songtsen Gampo’s entou-
rage, others are associated with his descendant Tri Songdetsen, who occupied 
the throne several generations later, and one, Lhabu Gokar, was a legendary 
hero from the time of Esholek (E sho legs), a semi-mythical king who, if he did 
exist, would have lived several centuries earlier. The point is that this literary 
format is a very archaic one, and examples can be found in some of the earliest 
known Tibetan texts that were discovered in the Mogao caves of Dunhuang, on 
the Silk Road. A distinctive feature of the genre is the self-vaunting with which 
each of the speakers closes. Such gatherings were presented as verbal contests 
in which each of the speakers ends by boasting of his high deeds and achieve-
ments. In the best-known example from Dunhuang, such speeches had a sig-
nificant political component, and were directed at rivals. In one well-known 
case, the political ambitions of a general who challenges Songtsen Gampo’s 
authority are thwarted by a young speaker who defeats him in a verbal duel, 
and is accordingly rewarded by the delighted emperor. This tradition of ora-
torical competition was known in several European societies, and is frequently 
referred to by the Scottish term, “flyting.” Elsewhere I have suggested that the 
popularity of flyting in Tibet may have been partly responsible for the enor-
mous succcess of autobiographical literature, which is a largely Buddhist adap-
tion of the exemplary presentation of oneself.

Klu gong nag po, Blon po sNa chen po, ’Brims (sic) rDo rje spre chung, Blon po Thon mi sam 
bho ra, Co ro Klu’i brgyal mtshan, sNa rnam skyi bdud ’joms, sNa rnams khron pa skyes, Blon 
chen lHa bu mgo dkar and sPyang A so.

Title folio missing, but the surviving folios contain 10 section headings. These

mula to the effect “… then spoke as follows”:
blon chen mgar stong rtsa yul bsrung gi bstan chos ’di tsam so / fol. 4r, line 1
blon chen ngam ’da’ ra ba klu gong nag po’i zhal nas / fol. 4r, line 5

yang sna rnams kyi bdud ’joms kyi zhal nas / fol. 5v, line 3
yang sna rnams khron pa skyes kyi zhal nas / fol. 5v, lines 7–8
blon chen lha bu mgo dkar kyi zhal nas / fol. 5v, line 8
yang spyang a sos gsungs pa / fol. 6v, line 8
5.1.3	
Form
Five folios written in ’khyug ma tshugs, with unusually long backward-curving 
descenders (especially on the consonant da) that are reminiscent of the 
Bhutanese joyig script, but that have also been observed on 11th century frag-
ments of paper found in caves in the Muktinath Valley. There are eight lines 
per folio side. The format of the folios is also unusually long; the folios have 
been folded once down the middle, and also apparently a second time.7 This 
suggests that the text was designed to be easily transportable, perhaps with a 
view to being read aloud at gatherings. Fol. 3 bears the stains of a red fluid that 
was presumably spilled onto it; a few passages have intentional rubrication, 
possibly with an infusion of calendula.
7	 According to Helmut Tauscher (personal communication) several folios matching this de-
scription have been found in Matho (Ladakh), and have been dated to the 11th or 12th century.

5.2	
Mardzong K
5.2.1	
Subject
Mardzong K is a highly unusual text that is concerned with the application of 
Tibetan law (Fig. 6.38). A full transliteration and translation are provided below 
in Chapter 10. The six available folios are not consecutive but are numbered 46, 
47, 48, 50 and 52. While this is an indication of how much has been lost before 
fol. 46, there is of course no way of knowing how much missing text there is 
after fol. 52. The work seems to be related to the well-known body of law codes 
known as zhalce (zhal ce or zhal lce). These are lists of laws, ranging in number 
from thirteen to sixteen, and dated to different periods and regimes in Tibet’s 
history. Although too little of Mardzong K remains to allow any conclusions 
about how it relates to the known examples, its content is similar to the com-
mentaries to some of the clauses (which are themselves rather terse) that are 
found in certain versions. The first and last chapter titles in the surviving folios 
are numbered 28 and 35, suggesting that, if these correspond to the zhal ce lists 
of the known works, it is much longer than these. Secondly, the chapter itself is 
considerably more elaborate than the similar zhal ce commentary mentioned 
above. And finally, if our estimate (based on codicological features) that the 
work dates from no later than the fifteenth century, and may even be earlier 
than that, it is at least two centuries older than the earliest available zhal ce 
text (see Schuh 1984: 298).
The language is archaic and difficult to understand, with many words that 
are not found in dictionaries. The surviving folios are concerned primarily 
with the process of dispute resolution through mediation, and the work relates 
to a society in which there is no supreme authority that can enforce laws—
something was characteristic of most of Tibet for most of its history, either 
because there was no such authority or else because people preferred to sort 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 191 of 332 ---
157
Missing Manuscripts from the Mardzong Cave Repository
out their issues without approaching official judicial authorities. Although it is 
a legal manual it has features of archaic ritual texts. First of all, it establishes 
a series of precedents for disputes and reconciliation that are said to have oc-
curred among humans, gods and animals:
Once upon a time, the gods and titans fought because they did not reach 
an agreement over the wish-fulfilling tree; the serpent-spirits fought be-
cause they did not reach an agreement over a jewel;… dogs fight over 
food. Generally, then, there is conflict even in the realm of the gods, while 
resolution may be achieved [even] in the realm of the demons. (fol. 46r)
Here we see a striking case of the human world of legal reparation being com-
pared to disputes in non-human realms, including both animals and gods. The 
idea seems to be that the dispute that is currently taking place is nothing new; 
it is a feature of life in the world. But just as disputes are natural, so too is their 
resolution, even among demons. In the absence of a supreme ruler, resolu-
tion is achieved by the bar spyi, which the context suggests is a term meaning 
‘mediator’. On the one hand the mediator must try to persuade the aggrieved 
party not to demand an impossibly high compensation. He is advised to say: 
“Something that has caused no more harm than the point of a needle doesn’t 
demand recompense as great as a sword.” And at the same time he must per-
suade the offender that peace can only be achieved if he actually pays the com-
pensation: “there can be no reconciliation without discussion, or without the 
payment of the compensation.”
The mediator is not more powerful that the disputing parties, and he even 
acknowledges this fact, warning them that they have nothing to gain from 
harming him:
If you kill me, you are the one who will suffer tormented feelings; if you 
beat me, your laughter will be in tears; if you scratch me, it’s your nails 
that will hurt … Since you are proud and powerful, and I, the mediator, 
am weak, the two of you should not compete in strength.
There is even a hint that the role of the mediator is a priestly one, since his 
healing role is compared to that of the Buddha:
We are feeling unwell as if we were in a sickbed; we are as if in a prison-
pit. If you are arrogant and unwise, I, the mediator, would be like the 
Buddha whose medicine has little benefit when he comes.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3

Mardzong AF
5.6.1	
Subject
Part of a document that appears to be the opening of a draft of a letter from a 
senior figure—possibly the king of Mustang—to his lay and monastic subjects 
(Fig. 6.42). The second line of the main text mentions par tsho gyi ling, “[the 
settlement of] Geling that is the par tsho”, where par tsho probably stands for 
bar tsho, the “middle county”; Lower Mustang consisted of eight counties (Lo 
smad tsho brgyad) and Upper Lo of seven (Lo stod tsho bdun); Geling, which 
had broken away from Upper Lo after the secession of Lower Lo, was consid-
ered to be a separate entity between these two blocs.
5.6.2	
Title
Rang re la stogs (< gtogs) pa’i skya mser (< ser) drag bzhan (< zhan) snams 
(rnaṃs); the title is excerpted from the opening line, the inscriptio, that names 
the addressees: rang re la rtogs (< gtogs) pa’i kya (< skya) ser grag (< drag) gzhan 
(< zhan) snaṃs (< rnams) la spring pa / “to all laity and clerics, both mighty and 
humble, who belong to us”.
5.6.3

line is in a large dbu med script, with backward-curving descenders in the 
style of the Bhutanese joyig script. The main text is written in a small and neat

5.10	
Mardzong AP
5.10.1	
Subject
A receipt involving barley and cash (Fig. 6.46). The details are vague due to the 
unclear handwriting. A certain ’A pha lHa ’bum has received 1 khal of barley in 
a Dog year; bZang po lha sbyin has given half a zho, which may be a reference 
to a quantity of precious metal or else a coin. (In the nineteenth and twenti-
eth centuries a zho signifies eight rupees.) The title ’A pha is probably a refer-
ence to the clan of that name (pronounced Awa), which is still represented 
in certain settlements in Upper Mustang. A member of this clan is named in 
Mardzong AT.
5.10.2	
Form
A single slip of paper, wide and short, of the sort that is typical for receipts. 
There are three lines, written in an indistinct ’khyug. In the middle of the docu-
ment is a mark that may be either a seal or a rubbing from an unidentified coin,

Chapter 7
On the Dating and Origin of the 
Mardzong Manuscripts
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble
We do not know if the manuscripts re-discovered in 2008 were all deposited 
in the Mardzong cave at the same time, or if they were placed there over a 
protracted period. Furthermore, we do not know what were the actual condi-
tions in which these texts circulated in the society, and why they were finally 
consigned to the cave. What we know is that the Mardzong texts are unique 
and preserved until our times, thus, they are “the witnesses” of their own prov-
enance. Thus, by undertaking a multidisciplinary study of this collection we 
attempted to date and localise the place of its production, as well as to identify 
and interpret patterns of transmission and use of manuscripts and the texts 
they contain. We looked at various textual, codicological and material features 
of these books (see chapters 3 and 4), and tried to determine the various stages 
of their life. The story we were able to reveal is still fragmented, but at the same 
time it helped us to put the Mardzong collection in context.
Sponsoring the creation of manuscripts was—and continues to be—a 
meritorious, prestigious, and very costly affair, and such texts are therefore 
generally accompanied by a lengthy dedication. The dedication is usually a 
separate folio that is placed at the beginning of the work, contrary to the colo-
phon which is usually placed at the end. However, in case of loose-leaf format 
it is not always clear if such a loose folio was originally intended for the begin-
ning or the end, unless it has foliation on the margin. Dedications begin with 
verses of homage to the major and minor divinities and the lineage lamas of 
the scribe’s school, before singing the praises of the pious patrons who spon-
sored the production of the text, and naming the members of his illustrious 
family. The location in which the commission was carried out is also specified, 
and described in conventionally idyllic terms.
The Khams chen dedication is damaged—between eight and twelve syllables 
of each line at the left end are missing—but the most essential information has 
survived.1 The dedication provides the names of the sponsors—apparently 
1	 For examples of such dedication folios from western Himalayan manuscripts of the same 
period, see Heller 2009. A full transliteration and translation of this work is found in chap-
ter 10 (Dedication 1).

a group of brothers or cousins (sku mched). The list of names falls exactly be-
tween two long lacunae of around nine syllables, and although the name of 
the lineage is missing it is said to belong to the Dong (lDong), one of the six 
Tibetan protoclans. Numerous members of the sponsors family are named.
The place where the commission was carried out is named in the text 
as Bonkhor, a location described as being to the east of Mount Kailash and 
south of the Pakshu River, a reference to the Brahmaputra (Yarlung Tsangpo 
in Tibetan). The Mt Kailash area in Western Tibet (part of the TAR) is one of 
the most venerated religious sites in the world, a destination of many pilgrims, 
since four religions consider the mountain to be holy.2 The Yarlung Tsangpo, 
with its origin in the Manasarovar Lake region, located on the northern side of 
the Himalayas in Purang County of Tibet, flows across southern Tibet to break 
through the Himalayas in great gorges and into Arunachal Pradesh (India); 
from here it runs southwest through Assam as the Brahmaputra and then 
south through Bangladesh into the vast Ganges Delta, emptying at last into the 
Bay of Bengal (Fig. 7.1). Nowadays Bonkhor is the name of an extensive area of 
ruins above the valley named Tshonub (Tsho nub)—the “Western County”—
to the northwest of Lo Monthang.
We know very little about Bonkhor. It appears in a brief biography of a Bon 
lama of the eleventh to twelfth centuries named Rong Tokme Zhikpo (Rong

rTog med zhig po), who came to the land of Lo to escape from a war in Central 
Tibet and took up residence in Bonkhor. Current oral tradition has it that the 
place was a Bon monastery; the name means “Bon community”, but the mo-
nastic tradition of Bon was not properly established until the fifteenth century, 
and there are unlikely to have been large monasteries of celibate Bon monks in 
the Himalayan region at that time. Just as Bonkhor means “Bon community”, so 
Chöngkhor (Chos ’khor) means “Buddhist community”. Chöngkhor is the name 
of a village in southern Mustang, close to Muktinath. It is not a monastery but 
a settlement of married Buddhist lamas. It is quite probable that Bonkhor was 
something similar—a loose association of lay lamas (we know that Rong Tokme 
Zhikpo, in spite of his towering reputation as a master of the spiritual system 
called Dzokchen [rDzogs chen], was not an ordained monk) as well as laity: the 
dedication strongly implies that the scribe’s patrons were actually living there; 
it is worth noting that one of the patrons, Gyaltsen Yungdrung, has a Bon name. 
However, the text provides no information about where the gZer mig and the Klu 
’bum, which are dated earlier than the Khams chen, were produced.
Immediately before the identification of the place and the list of patrons we 
have the following eulogy:
You who are descended from a line of mighty leaders, who promulgate 
the doctrine of the enlightened one far and wide, who are victorious over 
your enemies, rooting out savagery and diligent in lawfulness—praise 
to you, the minister Agön Zangpo and your brothers!
The earlier members of the dynasty were not generally identified by the usual 
Tibetan word for king, and for centuries after the foundation of the king-
dom the ruler of Lo was more commonly referred to by the title dewa (sde 
pa, which was also incidentally used of Bhutan’s rulers). Amepal had been a 
minister of the king of neighbouring Gungthang (Gung thang) before declar-
ing the independence of the territory of Lo that he had originally governed on 
his behalf, and his immediate successors also bore a title meaning “minister” 
(drungchen, drung chen); “the minister Agön Zangpo”3 can be none other than 
the son and successor of Amepal. If, as we must conclude, this dedication was 
written during his reign,4 then on the basis of the above information we could 
date this copy of the Khams chen to the middle of the fifteenth century.
However, the question we asked at this point was if all the Khams chen volumes 
were produced at the same time. We had begun with 4230 loose folios that we

Radiocarbon analysis indicated that the dedication (colophon) folio, as well 
as volumes cha (6), ta (9) and tha (10) belonged to the time window between 
the beginning of the fourteenth and the beginning of the fifteenth century. It 
is therefore slightly earlier than the middle of the fifteenth century, the period 
suggested by the appearance of the name of the ruler, Agön Zangpo. This slight 
difference could be due either to the lack of precision of the C14 dating meth-
od, or to the possibility that the paper had been stored for some time before it 
was used for the commission.
It should be noted here, that paper in Mustang must still have been a very 
valuable commodity in the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries. As mentioned 
earlier, paper-making in Mustang developed under the influence of both Nepal 
and Tibet. The technology of paper-making eventually reached Tibet from

China, most probably around 650 CE. Tibetan and Chinese historiography 
traditionally links it to the arrival of the Chinese wife of Emperor Songtsen 
Gampo. The majority of early Tibetan manuscripts found in the cave library 
at Dunhuang in Western China were executed on paper made of both rag and 
bark, components widely found in manuscripts in Chinese and other languages 
along the Silk Road. Nepalese paper-making technology is said to have begun 
between the eleventh and the thirteenth centuries, which means that the gZer 
mig, and Klu ’bum 2 volumes were produced soon after paper-making technol-
ogy just reached the regions that are now part of Nepal.6 This was a time when 
paper manufacture was a growing industry in the Central Himalaya and when 
the number of manuscripts written on paper was increasing, and the use of 
palm leaves decreased. (They continued to be used until the fifteenth century). 
Vol. ga (3) is dated later to the time in between the fifteenth and the beginning 
of the seventeenth century. Interestingly, the Mardzong volumes dated by the 
radiocarbon method to the thirteenth and fifteenth centuries were produced 
during these very same two windows of time as the archaeological samples 
that were examined (see chapter 5). The text of gZer mig, originally from the 
twelfth or thirteenth century, is usually in two volumes, and the version found 
in the Mardzong cave complex, comprising just the first volume, is dated to 
the thirteenth century. The Klu ’bum 1 is dated to the fourteenth century, and 
Klu ’bum 2, like the gZer mig, to the thirteenth. These texts are considered to 
be “treasure texts” (terma, gter ma) that were discovered by treasure-revealers 
(tertön, gter ston) in the 10th or 11th century.
The style of the script and decoration offer no clues to the dating of the col-
lection. However, these observations were helpful to assess the style and layout 
of painting which can give some hints on geographical origin of the Mardzong 
volumes. This kind of research sometimes makes it possible to identify the par-
ticular school to which the artists belonged, and to establish approximate dates 
for the image thanks to our existing knowledge about the period and range of 
the school in question. For Mustang however, this is quite a difficult task since 
we have very limited reference material, with the exception of work by Amy 
Heller on Dolpo manuscripts from Bicher and David Jackson’s more general 
work on the painting traditions in Ngari Province.7 Thus, only general obser-
vations can be made here, such as specific layout of illuminations, a feature 
shared by many manuscripts from Western Tibet, as well as a painting tech-
nique that uses heavier paint layers (entailing the application of thicker coats) 
characterised by the less subtle blending of colours. This painting feature is 
generally more typical for the style of Western and Central Tibet, rather than 
6	 Konishi 2013.
7	 Heller 2009 and Jackson 2012, 135–156.

Chapter 7
others such as the Karma Gardri (16th century) and the Menri (17th century) 
styles of Eastern Tibet where a colour would be applied in many thin layers.8
The surviving manuscripts from the Mardzong caves are written on very similar 
paper in terms of raw materials. The presence of fibres from the Thymelaeaceae 
family of plants, such as Daphne and Stellera spp. was revealed by microscopic 
examination. These fibres are typical for Tibetan books, but given the area of 
occurrence of these plants, we can conclude that the raw materials for paper 
production came from various sources. The location of the Tibetan Plateau and 
its extreme climatic conditions make the vegetation there different from veg-
etation in other parts of Asia. Thus, on this basis, we know that the Stellera sp. 
identified in the paper of the Mardzong books was used only in Tibet at altitudes 
greater than 3000 m above sea level, where there are no other plants suitable for 
paper production. This suggests that paper originating from the higher areas of 
the Tibetan Plateau as well as the areas south of Mustang, commonly known for 
the cultivation of paper-making plants, were used. Daphne fibres, on the other 
hand, probably came from the area south of Mustang, corresponding to today’s 
Myagdi, Baglung or Parbat, or from more remote Tibetan areas where the plants 
could grow (although this is less probable). The areas where Daphne grows are 
characterised by lush forests, milder temperatures and land suitable for agricul-
ture; they are still known for their tradition of paper production and constitute 
the area from which plants are used for paper-making.
These Stellera root bast fibres create a very specific soft type of paper 
that is considered to be of lower quality than bark paper made of Daphne, 
Edgeworthia, or Wikstroemia spp. The roots are especially difficult to harvest, 
which places a serious limitation on the quantity of paper that can be pro-
duced. Moreover, they require a longer time for processing, and they are there-
fore generally used in paper-making only when other sources are not available. 
On the other hand, the poisonous properties of these plants make the paper 
resistant to damage caused by insects, meaning that it may ultimately be more 
durable than other types of paper.
This suggests that usage of particular raw materials is strongly tied to geo-
graphical regions. This is supported by the fact that the altitudinal range of 
Daphne sp. extends up to 3500 m above sea level, that Stellera sp. is widely 
distributed along the Himalayan range at altitudes of 2600 to 4500 m, and that 
Daphne plants need much more moisture than Stellera. All this adds up to the 
fact that these two species very rarely grow in the same habitat. Thus, it sup-
ports our hypothesis that the raw material composing of particular volumes 
comes from different sources, as Daphne probably originated from the south-
ern valleys, and Stellera was probably brought from Tibet, or collected locally.
8	 Shaftel 1986, 98.

Chapter 8
Book-Making Practices in Mustang
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny
The most important work on Nepali paper is Jesper Trier’s book Ancient Paper 
of Nepal. Result of Ethno-technological Fieldwork on its Manufacture, Uses and 
History—with technical analyses of Bast, Paper and Manuscripts published 
in 1972. This study lists paper-making workshops involved in the produc-
tion of traditional lokta paper visited by Trier in 1970.1 Only two surviving 
paper-making workshops are, however, listed for Western Nepal. There are in 
addition a number of reports on paper-making methods in Nepal and the sur-
rounding regions.2 These reports are extremely informative as paper-making 
has changed relatively little over time compared to Europe. None of the paper-
making and book-making workshops recorded was in Mustang.
This chapter collates information garnered from monks, paper-makers, 
Mustang village elders and officials interviewed during our field trips between 
2015 and 2018. Such information is especially valuable from the perspective of 
craftsman, and tells us about the local history of paper- and book-production 
in Mustang. It is usually a jealously guarded professional secret, and there-
fore rarely made public. With the help of Nyima Dandrul, Kemi Tsewang and 
Rinchen Loden Lama we were able to interview villagers and monks who still 
actively produce manuscripts in the traditional way.3
Some interesting information about paper and trade in Mustang was col-
lected from a seventy-six-year-old trader from Tiri, who died a year after our 
interview in 2016. He was a textile merchant. In the south close to the Indian 
border he bought cotton and other raw fibres: kora (clothes), margen (for flags) 
and chir (cloth for women). He thus brought cloth from India and sold them 
in Upper Mustang. He sometimes made flags or clothes in Upper Mustang, or 
moved on to sell them in Dolpo. Lower quality cotton textile was usually used 
for flags (and probably also for making paper-making sieves) and it was the 
1	 Trier 1972.
2	 Tschudin 1958; Sandermann 1968; Hunter 1978; Richel 1985; Koretsky 1986; Helman-Ważny 
2001; Upreti 2004.
3	 These interviews were recorded, and short films were also uploaded onto the website of the 
Mardzong manuscripts project with English subtitles.

Chapter 8
cheapest. Other textiles were used for clothes. He occasionally also brought 
paper, but it was never a focal point of his business.
He remembered people who made paper in Tiri and collected plants for 
paper-making from the nearby forest. Interestingly, there is no longer a forest 
there. He also mentioned that paper was a commodity traded in the region. 
There was a toll (bhansar) point to the south in Tukche. People from Tibet and 
Upper Mustang would bring salt to Tukche, and on the return journey they 
would bring rice, corn, other grains, and sometimes paper from Tukche. There 
seems to have been another toll point, Nyizhung, above Lo Monthang. The 
interviewee was unsure about Jomsom. He travelled with subbas, tax collec-
tors from the Nepalese Government. The merchant would often go to Butwal, 
a trading centre on the way from India to Nepal on Nepal’s southern border. 
From Butwal he brought things to Dolpo and Jumla further west. His trading 
trips would sometimes last a year. Tansen near Pokhara was another important 
place. People would also go there to settle disputes as there was a court there. 
This information complies with Charles Ramble’s research suggesting that the 
toll point would move from time to time, and was even as far south as Jomsom 
at certain times. It was eventually fixed in Nyizhung, just south of the Kore La 
pass, on the border with Tibet.
We learned that in Jharkot the last craftsmen able to produce paper and 
books died fifteen years ago. We ascertained, however, that books were pro-
duced elsewhere. One of our team members, Nyima Dandrul, reported that 
his grandfather was a paper-maker in Upper Tshognam, Mustang, around 1930. 
His grandfather learned to make paper in Trakartaso, near Kyirong in Tibet, 
and went on to practise his craft in Upper Tshognam. Nyima recalls that he 
made his paper from recakpa (re lcag pa, Stellera chamaejasme).
The situation changed once the borders between Tibet (China) and Nepal 
were all but closed, and trade became impossible. Writing materials were 
thenceforth supplied exclusively from the southern provinces and no longer 
from Tibet and the North, and soon thereafter the Kathmandu valley become 
the main trading centre, where all goods were transported before they were 
sold on. There has been a great deal of migration out of Mustang in recent years 
and people from various regions have mixed, and many of them have moved to 
Kathmandu or larger cities in India. Most paper-making workshops and craft 
centres are therefore now located in the Kathmandu valley. Large Buddhist 
and Bon monasteries in the Kathmandu house monks who still know how to 
produce books, which is not the case of the small monasteries in Mustang.
Manu Yungdrung Tharchin, a monk from the Triten Norbutse monastery 
in Kathmandu, shared his knowledge with us and demonstrated his skills. He 
was, however, originally not from Mustang, but the skills he learned from Bon 
masters in eastern Tibet were greatly appreciated.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

--- Page 209 of 332 ---
175
Book-Making Practices in Mustang
1	
Paper
Many varieties of paper were used for making books, and different types required 
different processes. Any evaluation of the quality of paper requires us to examine 
the raw materials and technologies used, as well as the aesthetics and the pur-
pose for which it was intended in particular historically defined period of time. 
We must therefore bear in mind that such descriptions of paper quality may be 
subjective and would not always correspond with general standards of paper 
quality elsewhere. According to monks of Triten Norbutse monastery, Tibetan 
paper (bod shog) is considered the best suited for calligraphy (Fig. 8.1). The raw 
material has a crucial influence on the quality of the paper. Manu Yungdrung 
Tharchin knew paper made from gumbu plant, which is probably Stellera 
chamaejasme called gumbu recakpa or sibri mentok (se bri me tog) in Mustang (re 
lcag pa in Tibet). Daphne plants no longer grow north of Jomsom (2743 m above 
sea level). They did, however, grow at this elevation in the past, especially in the 
valleys nearby. Interestingly, the area of what is now the border of Lower and 
Upper Mustang may also at one time have been a natural border between the 
distribution of Daphne and Stellera plants; Stellera sp., for example, now grows 
abundantly in the environs of Muktinath beginning at 3700 m above sea level.
Figure 8.1	 Paper used for calligraphy by Manu Yungdrung Tharchin from Triten Norbutse 
monastery in Kathmandu
Photograph by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2017)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 210 of 332 ---
176
Chapter 8
The relatively basic methods of paper production mean that the original 
Tibetan paper tended to be darker and have a good many small pieces of outer 
bark in the paper itself. Whilst this may have been the result of too many im-
purities in the paper pulp, it became an aesthetic feature typical of Himalayan 
paper. Nevertheless, one of the most important distinguishing characteristics 
of good quality paper according to the people in Mustang was its whiteness, 
smooth surface, and uniform thickness of the sheets, the most difficult crite-
rion given such simple methods. Because it was extremely difficult for them to 
achieve such uniform thickness, in order to achieve this, paper-makers tended 
to resort to gluing a number of layers of paper together using a paste made 
from boiled wheat flour. A sheet of paper would consist of the sieve side, which 
would be naturally smooth, and the opposite side, which would tend to have a 
slightly rough surface. The smooth side would be rubbed with clay, after which 
two sheets of paper would be glued together, the rough sides glued to each 
other and the smooth sides facing outward. The sheets would be glued togeth-
er with drochu (gro chu, wheat water which works as a weak glue). Cooked 
properly Tibetan wheat is especially valued for its adhesive properties.
The surface of the paper would then be rubbed with a white clay, called 
bhar, with the help of a felt made from grass. This clay was used to smooth 
the surface of the paper, eliminate creases and mask any other flaws. It thus 
helped to make the surface of the paper even, smooth and white. Once that 
papermaker had dipped the paper into the wheat glue (wheat water) he would 
place it on a wooden plank to dry the white, rubbed surface facing down. He 
would do the same with another sheet of paper and place it on top of the first 
sheet, the white, rubbed surface facing up, thus sticking them together. The 
white sides of the paper would thus both face outwards. The paper is then 
drained of excess water and the sheet were laid out to dry in the sun and col-
lected once they are dry. In summer nomads would dry paper in meadows. 
The papers would sometimes separate and to stick such sheets together again 
they would lay them on the grass in the early morning. The dew from the grass 
would stick the paper together again and at the same time stretch the sheets.
Another trait of good quality paper from a calligrapher’s perspective is a 
glossy surface. The paper-maker would tend to use a smooth, flat stone calen-
dering the paper surface. Manu Yungdrung Tharchin reported that in the past 
a stone known as urkhang was used, and that it made the paper extremely 
shiny. Today, however, river stones are used, as seen in figure 8.2 (Fig. 8.2). This 
process continues until the paper is clear, glossy and free of lumps.
Once the sheets of paper were glued together and the surfaces of both sides 
were polished, they would have to be cut to the same dimensions as all the pages. 
Once the measurements were decided and checked with a rule, the edges would 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 211 of 332 ---
177
Book-Making Practices in Mustang
be cut with a blade. In the past bone would be used as a blade for this purpose, 
but today a steel blade is used to ensure that the sheets are evenly cut.
This traditional Tibetan method of preparing paper no doubt contributed 
to the lettering style. The surface of paper which would consist of layers that 
had been glued together and polished would be more or less unabsorbent and 
smooth. This would facilitate the use of a wooden pen for writing, which would 
be impossible on absorbent Chinese-style paper, which would require a brush 
to write on it. The writing supports used in the production of Tibetan books 
would have been determined primarily by their local availability, but also by 
the conditions of patronage, aesthetic preferences, or a book’s status and func-
tion. Materials were carefully selected for their intrinsic qualities, as well as 
their suitability for specific types of writing, as well as for engraving.
2	
Ink
Most ink used in inscribing the manuscripts from Mustang is black, but we also 
find red for highlighting text phrases such as titles, important names and even 
annotations, in a similar way to rubrics in medieval manuscripts, as well as for 
Figure 8.2	 A monk in Triten Norbutse monastery in Kathmandu polishing the surface of the 
sheet of paper in preparation for writing
Photograph by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2017)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

--- Page 212 of 332 ---
178
Chapter 8
sketching the frames and margins. In Mustang black ink tended to be made of 
soot and animal glue, similarly to the way it was done in Tibet. It is worth look-
ing at a number of formulae for the preparation of ink here, since all craftsman 
may have had their own methods. This information from people living today is 
important in interpreting the results of material and codicological analyses of 
old books from the Mustang region.
During our interview Manu Yungdrung Tharchin emphasised that among 
commercially available products today Tibetan ink is the best for writing. The 
cheapest now, however, is an ink from China (gyaney). According to monk, 
the Chinese ink sticks were very good in the past, but today the quality of 
those available has declined. This may be the reason monks sometimes prefer 
to make the ink themselves, and therefore collect the soot from butter lamps. 
This soot would need to be soaked in trinchu (glue) for a few days, before being 
rubbed by hand until smooth in order to eliminate any solid substances. Once 
whole the black substance (soot with glue) was in hand, the craftsman would 
need to wet his hands with a little water and rub it again until it began to thick-
en. He would then rub a small amount of butter into the ink until it became 
darker, but not entirely black. His estimation was that it would take him two 
days to make three ink sticks. The more the ink is rubbed, the better the final 
result is. The ink is then dried. During the winter, the ink should be protected 
from cracking, and in summer from damp. It needs to be kept in a dry environ-
ment. Rubbing butter or oil into ink helps to protect it from cracking.
The best quality source material for red ink is a mineral called gyab tsar cho 
nga, which was also used for paint used for making thangkas. The mineral was 
ground into the ink on a hard-stone surface with a handle (Fig. 8.3). Those large 
and flat stones used for grinding found in water are considered the best ink 
stones. Many different kinds of ink stones are commercially available today. 
Some are more suitable than others and, since the grounds find their way into 
the pigment, the colour of the ink changes when the stone is too soft. In such 
cases the ink turns greyish and, when used for writing, such ink does not look 
clear. For a grinding handle, hard wood (nyug) or the horn of an animal, either 
a yak or a sheep, may be used until the red stone looks like red sand. It has to be 
ground extremely finely, and should eventually turn orange. No solid particles 
should remain at the bottom. Once the fine pigment particles are ready, a trin 
(glue, binder) should be added. Whilst this is readily available today, in the 
past in Tibet, yak’s skin would be boiled for a couple of hours to make the glue. 
During the boiling process, the liquid would have to be skimmed until it was 
clear. It was then dried in a cool place out of the sun. Drying at night yielded 
the best results because the glue would then be more likely to be firm. The 
glue was then mixed with the ink until it reached the right consistency. The 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 213 of 332 ---
179
Book-Making Practices in Mustang
temperature should be controlled during this process. The ink would crack if the 
temperature fell too low in winter. Some time into the mixing process the ink 
would begin to thicken, but it should not be too thick. Once the mixture seems 
ready, a small amount of water should be added and it is ready to be used.
3	
Layout
Whether the book is in pothi or deb ther format, the writing surface is typically 
central and often limited by a full frame or left-hand and right-hand margins. 
There are no fixed proportions and the width of the margins in relation to 
the writing surface may vary depending on the original text to be copied. The 
guidelines and frames had to be sketched on the paper before the text might 
be written upon it.
According to Manu Yungdrung Tharchin, a needle was used to make marks 
through a couple of sheets of paper at once (Fig. 8.4). One outlined page would 
then be used to pin the distances between frames, margins and guidelines on 
other pages by placing an outlined folio on the top of new sheets and making a 
mark with the needle by pressing it. It must be absolutely straight with no paper 
overlaps. This tracing is called khabthik (khab thig). The guidelines and frames 
Figure 8.3	 Natural pigments used for ink and paints in Tibetan culture
Photograph by Agnieszka Helman-Ważny (2018)
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
