Chapter 8
would then all be sketched straight and even with a bamboo or metal rule, 
and this process is called dongthik. Manu Yungdrung Tharchin stated, however, 
that, according to Tibetan tradition, it is actually better to draw a line with a 
thread soaked in yellow dye, called chithik. In Tibet for this purpose the yellow 
juice of a flower called ga way chag shog was used. If the juice of this flower is 
unavailable, using water dyed yellow is better than using a pen.
These guidelines are drawn to regulate the size of the letters and keep the 
lines of text even and neat. The fainter the lines are the better aesthetically. 
When sketching the lines, the rule had to be turned over four times to make 
five lines (Fig. 8.5).
4	
Calligraphy
The place, materials and tools all needed to be prepared before writing could 
commence. The place should be quiet and the scribe should sit on the floor 
in silence. He should be relaxed. As Manu Yungdrung Tharchin pointed out, 
the scribe should focus and hold the pen properly (Fig. 8.6). He will therefore 
miss no letter. A brush must then be softened. As is the case in Tibet, books 
in Mustang are written with a wooden pen, but a brush is used as an auxiliary

tool on which ink is kept during the process of writing (Fig. 8.7). It sometimes 
grows very cold at higher altitudes, and such brushes freeze, and absorb ink 
poorly. In order to prevent this, tools should be made of animal fur. Before writ-
ing the brush should therefore be cleaned and ink should be prepared. If the 
ink is too thin, it needs to be taken from the bottom of the ink well, but, if 
the ink is thick, it may be taken from anywhere in the ink well. One should not 
take too much ink on the brush because it may drip. Just enough ink to convey 
the right amount to the wooden pen should be taken at one time. Using the 
brush thus helps to control amount of ink acquired by a pen.
Depending on the writing style, the bamboo pen has to be properly cut into 
the right shape, and the pen nib must be held in a particular way. If the pen 
nib wears down, it must be cut upwards with a minute tool. It must then be 
twisted. A handle on the blade would make it a great deal easier to cut. In this 
way the pen nib is fashioned. This is the way the nib of the pen is checked. If 
there is white on the background, it will be easier to see the tip. The width of 
the nib needs to match the width of pervious letters. A saying goes that, when 
one writes with the pen, one’s hand should be so steady that, even if one were 
to place an egg between one’s thumb and index finger, it would not fall and at 
the bottom one’s little finger should be so tight that not a drop of water could 
get beneath it.

1	
Preservation of Cultural Heritage in Mustang
1.1	
The Institutions of Conservation and Preservation in Nepal
Protecting cultural heritage is quite a young discipline in Nepal, having de-
veloped in the second half of 20th century. Nepal’s cultural heritage has sur-
vived thanks to the kings and influential ministers who occasionally convened 
committees to restore the most important monuments, and wealthy bene-
factors who offered donations to bolster their religious standing (karma). An 
important role was played by the guthi community,1 which helped keep the 
main municipal buildings in good condition, allowing their continued func-
tion. Another historic organization dedicated to the protection of important 
objects in Nepal was the Chhen-Bhadel, which was probably founded at the 
beginning of the Malla dynasty (1201–1769).2 Most of the urban and rural areas, 
however, developed at their own pace, without feeling the need to protect or 
renovate their monuments. The policy of isolation that had held sway in Nepal 
until the 1950s was a further obstacle to the development of conservation. The 
ban on foreign visitors prevented the development of ideas, technologies and 
sciences, including methods of preservation and protection of cultural heri-
tage. Opening up Nepal to the world has allowed a flow of information, includ-
ing the exchange of goods and the migration of the population, which has led 
to rapid changes in the country in different fields. The Building Committees 
1	 Guthi is a kind of community formed in the Newar ethnic group, the original inhabitants of 
the Kathmandu Valley. Guthi acts as a community united in the achievement of a common 
goal for the whole group. Guthi’s history is as old as the civilization of the Newars. Some re-
searchers view guthi as an organised institution created by members of the group to improve 
the common standard of living. The inspiration and willingness to form such a community 
was closely linked to the religion and spirituality of the community, which in turn facilitated 
the strengthening of religious significance in Nepal. There are many types of guthi that fulfil 
the diverse needs of Nepali society. See Vajracharya, Phanindra Ratna. 1998. Role of Guthi in 
Newar Buddhist Culture. Accessed August 19, 2017.
2	 Chapagain 2008, 3. Accessed September 26, 2017.

Chapter 9
and the Department of Public Works have been involved in the restoration of 
the old buildings. On the occasion of the coronation of King Mahendra, and 
then his heir, King Birendra, numerous public offices and agencies have at-
tempted to preserve important cultural heritage sites. The establishment of 
the Department of Archaeology (DOA) in 1953 brought a breakthrough, which 
four years later led to the announcement of the first state document on the 
protection of Nepal’s cultural heritage: the Ancient Monuments Preservation 
Act.3 This document concerned the protection of historical monuments by 
preserving archaeological, historical and artistic objects, as well as control of 
archaeological excavations carried out on the site of ancient buildings and the 
trade in archaeological objects. So far, the Ancient Monuments Preservation 
Act has been an overarching legal act for the preservation of Nepal’s cultural 
heritage. Further development of the cultural heritage preservation occurred 
in the 1960s, when foreign conservators came to Nepal to create a general plan 
for the protection of Nepalese monuments. The Department of Archaeology is 
the main conservation institution in Nepal. Its official website states that it is 
in charge of the “protection and maintenance of archaeological sites, ancient 
monuments including remains of national importance, museum and archive 
management.”4 Its tasks are for example to establish museums and provide 
technical and financial support to the local communities and agencies for 
monument conservation, and raise awareness among inhabitants for the pro-
tection and promotion of cultural heritage. The Department of Archaeology is 
controlled by the Ministry of Culture, Tourism and Civil Aviation of Nepal. The 
institution is divided into four departments: the first is responsible for muse-
ums; the second is the Central Laboratory of Cultural Heritage Conservation; 
the third is Monument Preservation and Palace Management (OMPPM); 
and the last is the National Archive.5 The department also cooperate with 
UNESCO’s Kathmandu Office. The DOA also publish a journal, Ancient Nepal, 
which focuses on pre-history and field archaeology in the Himalayan Region.6 
Due to a lack of consistent conservation, the department is, however, unable 
to fulfil all of its duties regarding the protection of Nepal’s legacy. Their work 
focuses mainly on “ancient” and “archaeological” aspects of monuments, and 
most of the projects and funds go to Kathmandu Valley, a fact related to the po-
sition and role of UNESCO in Nepal. The DOA is responsible for rebuilding the 
monuments after the earthquake in 2015. The works are conducted slowly, so

international conservation agencies, such as UNESCO, are very concerned and 
exert pressure on government institutions. The government itself is unable to 
provide sufficient money for rebuilding the destroyed monuments and build-
ings, and pays such meagre salaries that educated, qualified people choose not 
to work in this field. Instead, they employ unqualified people. The Department 
of Archaeology needs a stronger policy and a thorough plan for modern con-
servation, amending legal regulations as times have changed since the estab-
lishment of the initial Ancient Monuments Preservation Act.
The main institution responsible for the preservation of books is the 
National Archive of Nepal. It was established in 1967 under the authority of 
the Ministry of Culture, Tourism and Civil Aviation.7 There are four main sec-
tions: administration, archives, manuscripts and preservation. Each of these is 
divided into microfilm and conservation sections. The National Archive also 
runs the Conservation Laboratory to treat manuscripts. The manuscript sec-
tion is responsible for the conservation of the vast manuscript collection re-
lated to the history and culture of Nepal. More than 180,0008 Hindu, Buddhist 
and Tibetan texts constitute the collection. They are in different languages and 
scripts, such as Sanskrit, Newari, Tibetan, Nepali, Maithili, Hindi and Avadhi.9 
Most of them are written on traditional materials used in Nepal, India and 
Tibet, such as palm leaf, colour-coated Nepali paper and birch bark, which are 
quite fragile. The whole collection was microfilmed between 1970s and 2002 
by the Nepal German Manuscript Preservation Project.10 Then a paper cata-
logue of this collection was prepared by The Nepalese-German Manuscript 
Cataloguing Project between 2002 and 2014 and is available on the website 
of Hamburg University’s Centre for the Study of Manuscript Cultures.11 The 
National Archive attempts to adapt to the developments in the fields of con-
servation and preservation, but it needs financial support to be able to docu-
ment and safeguard their own collection properly. The microfilms created in 
the 90s are mostly pale and blurred. There is no digital database or catalogue 
of the collection. The record of manuscripts is obsolete and incomplete, lack-
ing, for example, the provenance of texts and in some cases even their cur-
rent location. The database is unspecified and this creates difficulties for users 
when searching through the manuscripts. The locations of manuscripts from

1.2	
The Influence of UNESCO on the Conservation Policy in Nepal
UNESCO has the greatest influence on the preservation and conservation poli-
cies of all NGO s involved in the care of monuments in Nepal. The Kathmandu 
Valley has become the main focus of international organisations involved in 
the preservation of cultural heritage since the establishment of official regula-
tions in the country. Supportive programmes and specialists made it possible 
to educate and train the staff of the Nepali Department of Archaeology. The de-
velopment of conservation in 1970s in Nepal was possible thanks to UNESCO. 
The activities sponsored, however, were conditional upon Nepal’s preparation 
to join the international movement for preservation and protection of cultural 
heritage managed by UNESCO under the 1972 Convention on the Protection 
of the World’s Cultural and Natural Heritage.12 Eventually Nepal signed the 
Convention in 1978 and reported four sites: Kathmandu Valley, Lumbini, 
the Chitwan National Park and the Sagarmatha National Park. Kathmandu 
Valley itself has seven protected monuments on the list: Kathmandu Durbar 
Square, Patan Palace Square, Bhaktapur Durbar Square, Swyambhunath Stupa, 
Boudhanath Stupa, Pashupatinath Temple and Changu Narayan Temple.13 
UNESCO monitors the conservation efforts in Nepal. Kathmandu is the larg-
est city in Nepal, and at the same time the political, economic and cultural 
centre of the country. Uncontrolled urbanisation, mass migration and the 
development of tourism have aggravated the condition of monuments in the 
Kathmandu Valley. The sites in Kathmandu were placed on the List of World 
Heritage in Danger twice: first in 1997 (two monuments) and secondly in 2003 
(six monuments).14 The last earthquake caused tremendous damage and Nepal 
is slowly rebuilding the ruined monuments with the help of UNESCO, which 
acts in an advisory capacity and supports the government and local agen-
cies with education. The potential for organization is limited and resources 
are limited. UNESCO does not grant projects, but rather takes long-term over-
sight to ascertain whether its member countries fulfil their obligations. It is 
clear that the eyes of everyone are focused on the destroyed monuments, but

Kathmandu Valley is not the only area to suffer damage. The other regions of 
Nepal seem to have been forgotten by the authorities. There are other proj-
ects run by UNESCO, for example The Annapurna Conservation Area Project15 
launched in 1986, but this focuses more on the conservation of nature, the de-
velopment of tourism (with minimum damage to the natural, socio-cultural 
and economic environments) and ensures the social and economic develop-
ment of the local people.16
1.3	
Conservation of Cultural Heritage in Upper Mustang
The conservation of cultural heritage has improved significantly since Nepal 
joined UNESCO, but the fact that most funds find their way to supporting re-
search projects solely in the Kathmandu Valley is a persistent problem. The rest 
of the country seems excluded from its plans, so the inhabitants feel neglected 
and complain about the unfair distribution of resources. Local communities 
are proud of their heritage, but they lack the resources and knowledge to main-
tain them in good condition. The very few generous grants given to preserve 
cultural heritage objects in the Kathmandu Valley also led them think that 
conservation is the procedure that people in more rural areas cannot afford.17 
There is no doubt, however, that the interest of foreign scholars has brought 
about great changes. One of the areas where significant transformations have 
taken place is Mustang, the former Kingdom of Lo in the north of the coun-
try. Culture and geography have forged strong connections between Mustang 
and Tibet.18 In the past, the people of Mustang claimed their kingdom to be a 
border region of Ngari, which was a part of Tibet.19 Most of the scientific re-
search on the history and culture of this region was run in the 1950s and 1980s 
by foreign Tibetologists, especially from USA and Europe.20 Over the last two 
decades, Mustang has changed many of its policies and procedures regarding 
tourism and cultural heritage. The mysterious Mustang kingdom was closed 
to foreigners until 1992,21 when part of it was opened to tourists. Permits and 
regulations22 were issued to control the number of tourists allowed to enter 
every year. These regulations help to protect the wildlife and small villages

from large groups of tourists, and ensure the heritage of Mustang is protected

money only for Upper Mustang from trekking permits, getting around 30% of 
all permits from 2018 onwards. The inhabitants must, however, face another 
problematic political issue connected with the Mustang region. Jigme Dorje 
Palbar Bista, the last King of Mustang died in 2016 leaving no successor. The 
King had one son who died at the age of eight. After this tragedy Jigme Dorje 
Palbar Bista adopted his nephew Jigme Singhe Palbar Bista. With the death of 
the king, the dynasty founded by Ame Pal, the first King of Mustang, ended. 
Today Mustang has no king and a serious dispute concerning claims to the 
throne persist. Relatives of the king have been arguing since the death of Jigme 
Dorje Palbar Bista and the issue remains unresolved. However, the most dis-
turbing problem in Mustang is lack of strong leader capable of negotiating 
with the Government.
The effects of tourism have been mixed, but there is no doubt that local 
works of art and architecture are in need of immediate professional care. 
Natural forces have caused monuments to fall into ruin. Many of the historic 
buildings have been abandoned and exposed to the ravages of time, among 
them ancient settlements, fortresses, palaces, monasteries and cave complexes 
in many villages, such as Lo Monthang, Lubra and Jomsom. Mustang is where 
research projects conducted by state institutions and international organisa-
tions such as the Annapurna Conservation Area Project under the leader-
ship of the King Mahendra Trust for Nature Conservation and the American 
Himalayan Foundation take place. One of the most important projects today is 
the conservation of the Thubchen Lhakhang temple, built in the 15th century 
in Lo Monthang. The building has forty-nine pillars; the interior has been deco-
rated with a variety of wall paintings. The murals are examples of Newar art 
from the 14th and 15th centuries. The walls are covered with murals of divini-
ties and mandalas, most of which have been destroyed or have not survived 
in good condition.23 The American Himalayan Foundation took care of the 
temple and hired the Italian restorer, Rudolfo Luian, in 1999. He was in charge 
of this until 2005, when his assistant and student, Luigi Fieni, took charge of the 
renovation project. He now comes to Lo Monthang to work for six months every 
year from April to September. He has also included the local population in the

scholars are genuinely devoted to the people and the culture of this region of 
Nepal. Luigi Fieni has worked with locals for several years on the preservation 
of two great monuments in Lo Monthang. The first is the above-mentioned 
Thubchen Lhakhang and the second is Jampa Lhakhang, the royal monastery. 
The Jampa Lhakhang, the oldest temple in Lo Monthang,24 was also includ-

wall paintings were in urgent need of care and a three-storey building needs 
structural work. Luigi Fieni is a specialist with a great admiration for Mustang’s 
culture.
Another well-known scholar focusing on the preservation of the traditions 
of Lopa people and safeguarding the cultural sites is Susanne von der Heide. 
She works with the inhabitants of Mustang for the maintenance of the cultural 
and regional identity of the Lopa people.25 Migration, climate change, natural 
disasters and many other factors influence the life of the people of Mustang. 
It is important, however, to try to understand the people living in Mustang; 
that they want to improve their quality of life and live comfortably. Von der 
Heide cooperates with the HimalAsia Foundation, which works for cultural 
heritage and education in Mustang, helping to build new houses and schools, 
educate children and women and create the health centres.26 She also helped 
to get Mustang recognised as a UNESCO World Heritage Site. There have been 
a few attempts in the past to include some monuments from Mustang on this 
list. Two places from Mustang were included on the Tentative List of Nepal:27 
one is the cave architecture of the Muktinath Valley of Mustang in 1996,28 the 
second is the Medieval Earthern Walled City of Lo Monthang in 2008.29

Nepalese scholars are also interested in Mustang’s heritage. Prakash Darnal

meaning and conservation issues. He writes about villages, monasteries and 
cave excavations.30 In his article, The Significant Heritage of Upper Mustang 
and the Issue of Conservation, the conclusion is patently clear: without conser-
vation and the involvement of specialists many places will not survive for long 
in the future. Darnal 2017, 5–20. Thanks to people who work in Mustang and 
spread the ideas of projects run in Mustang, there is nevertheless still hope for 
increasing awareness among Nepalese society. Nevertheless, the exchange of 
knowledge and experiences among scholars is crucial because all documenta-
tion of work is as important as the conservation itself.
1.4	
Manuscripts as Religious Artefacts
Holy books have one important role: to spread the teachings of Buddhism and 
Bon. One of the most important religious practices is the copying of holy texts 
by monks in order to gain religious standing and merit in order to improve 
their karma.31 The copies thus produced take on the value of the original copy 
and are treated equally. Monks do not distinguish between religious books as 
originals or copies, as every book is a manifestation of the words of Buddha.32 
The material qualities of the book itself were not as significant as the spiritual 
words within, with every book being created to further spread the words of 
Buddha. This was therefore the main reason for the production of manuscripts. 
Books are one of the pillars of faith and are seen as ritual objects.33 Mustang 
is one of the remaining places where Tibetan culture and Bon tradition34 are 
still present. In the Tibetan culture monasteries were the artistic, religious and 
scientific centres of country, where the most valuable objects were gathered. 
Many of them included large libraries.
The character and location of specific buildings themselves are largely de-
pendent on the landform of the Himalayan region. The mountains and vast 
stone deserts form the typical landscape of Mustang. The mysterious caves hol-
lowed out in the high mountains are not rare across the region, but there is no 
certainty about their function or origin. Their primary function was as settle-
ments for people. Some caves served as houses with layouts of rooms including

32		
Helman-Ważny 2009, 155–156, 185.

Bon, incorporating the teachings of Tonpa Shenrab Miwo, is considered by Tibetans to 
have been the dominant religion of their country until the consolidation of Buddhism 
in the 8th century. There are many common aspects in Bon and Buddhism. Today both 
religions coexist in Mustang. To read more on this topic see: Karmay and Watt 2007.

kitchens and bedrooms. The local population call these caves “cliff houses.”35 
The caves also served as a religious refuge for hermits, people who had decided 
to remain in the dark and isolated caves to meditate and pray. The discoveries 
of human skeletons in the caves bear out this theory. Another theory presents 
the caves as Buddhist and Bon monasteries, where monks lived alongside the 
sacred manuscripts, historical books and pieces of art. Old frescoes are still 
visible on the walls of a few caves. Most of them are sacred manifestation of 
Bon and Buddhist gods, godesses and saints. As was mentioned in the preface 
the Mardzong caves are probably an abandoned monastery where sacred texts 
were buried once they were no longer in a fit condition for use. This is the ritual 
of the burial of religious books in Tibetan tradition, because as sacred objects 
the books cannot be destroyed at all. This ritual could explain the reason these 
valuable texts were abandoned for such a long time.
2	
The State of Preservation of the Mardzong Manuscripts
2.1	
Procedures after Removing the Manuscript Collection from the 
Mardzong Caves
The process of removing the manuscripts from the Mardzong caves is de-
scribed above in the preface (pp. 21–22), and need not be repeated at length 
here. In short, following a decision by a majority of the town councils and other 
authorities (the Lo Monthang Youth Club being the sole exception) to remove 
the collection to a safe location, the texts were extracted from the caves by the 
climbing team, wrapped in cloth and taken to the Lo Choede monastery. It was 
later decided that a museum should be built in order to house the manuscripts 
and other cultural artefacts from the area.
During our visit in Lo Monthang in April 2018, the principal of the Shree 
Mahakaruna Sakyapa Vidhyalaya Monastic School in Choede Monastery, 
Chhimek Rinzen Gurung, showed our team the site ready for the construction 
of the museum (Fig. 9.1). The monks want to build a suitable building for all the 
artefacts, and have been raising funds for the project for several years. There 
are three monasteries in the village: the Choede Monastery belongs to monks, 
and the Thubchen and Jampa monasteries are the common property of villag-
ers. The temporary museum in which the Mardzong collection is stored was 
founded in the Choede monastery, but in reality it was just one small, dusty 
room with neither shelves nor glass-cases to exhibit the objects (Fig. 9.2). 
The manuscripts were lying on the floor without protection. The room was 
dry and dark, so the folios were not exposed to sunlight. Some alterations and

2.2	
Description of the Museum of Antiquities in Lo Monthang and the 
Condition of the Mardzong Collection
The Museum of Antiquities is run by the Student Welfare Committee from the 
monastic school of the Choede Monastery (Fig. 9.3). This tiny museum is in 
fact a single room where a number of objects are housed, such as ceramics, 
thangkas, paper masks used during the Tiji festival, copper plates, statues of 
Tibetan Buddhist and Bon deities, chortens, medieval helmets and, of course, 
the Mardzong manuscript collection. Initially the room was without furniture, 
so the artefacts lay on the floor, but this changed in 2017 with the addition of 
shelves and boxes which today house the artefacts. The room has one door 
with a padlock, the key to which is held by the monks. There is no security sys-
tem. The Museum of Antiquities is generally closed and opens only for visitors. 
There are two three-wing windows which let the light in, but are insufficient to 
illuminate the room, which remains dark even during the daytime. The muse-
um has electricity and a weak electric light. The air is dry, and there is no access 
to water. The ceiling is about three metres high and the floor-space of the room 
measures about 9.5 × 5.5 metres. The floor and ceiling are made of wood; the 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM

--- Page 228 of 332 ---

Chapter 9
walls are built of bricks. The museum is not equipped with a fire extinguisher, 
which is quite dangerous due to the wooden floor and ceiling.
None of the artefacts has an information label describing its origin and func-
tion. The monk who accompanied me during my visit in 2018 was unable to an-
swer most of the questions I put about these objects. The objects are organised 
by type: all statues, for example, stand in one place. Within these categories 
they are mixed and their ages and provenance are unclear, which is unhelpful 
for the museum’s visitors.
The Mardzong manuscript collection is a part of the museum artefacts. 
Each volume is wrapped in a blue cotton sheet and tied with a khata36 to pre-
vent the loose folios from falling out. All volumes are piled on the window-
sill, blocking the sunlight, although a more significant problem is the weight 
of these manuscripts, i.e. the volumes on the bottom subject to damage. This 
kind of stacking is unsafe for any objects. People cannot see what is under the 
sheets, as the Mardzong manuscripts are not exhibited to visitors. This is an-
other misunderstanding and something the museum does which flouts a good 
many protective guidelines.
After moving the manuscripts to the museum the folios were organised 
and arranged in order by volume and page. This was done by the Mardzong 
Manuscript Project. The collection is quite damaged, which has largely 
been caused by aging and the place the manuscripts have been stored. The 
Mardzong cave complex has several entrances, so birds had access to rooms. 
Most of the folios originally had bird droppings on them, but some of them 
were cleaned after their removal from caves. They are therefore, still covered 
with dirt and dust, as well as with traces of micro-organisms. In many cases 
the layer of droppings is thick, making it difficult to read or even see the text 
(Fig. 9.4). The other problem is dust, which is detrimental to one’s health. The 
paper has deteriorated with visible spots of stains, blots and fungus, but the 
most visible are feathers, which are stuck to the paper and cotton wrapping 
(Fig. 9.5). Despite all visible damage the quality of paper is quite good and it 
has not lost flexibility; only a few pages have become brittle. Almost every page 
has jagged edges, probably the result of frequent use. A few pages are torn or 
have holes where the illuminations from the manuscripts were cut out with a 
knife or scissors, with precise lines serving as evidence (Fig. 9.6). It could have 
been done by the local inhabitants and was surely done before the discovery 
of the caves in 2008. There are various possible reasons for such action: some

aspects of Tibetan art, every image of Buddha or Bodhisattva being treated as

Preservation of the Mardzong Manuscripts Collection
Considering that the manuscripts have been in the caves for such a long 
time, the general condition of the Mardzong collection could be much worse. 
Nevertheless the manuscripts were found in a poor condition (Fig. 9.8). 
Appropriate preservation of the valuable manuscripts is therefore urgently 
needed. All volumes should be cleaned and protected against dust, fungus and 
pollution. The Museum of Antiquities itself is not a bad place to house the 
manuscripts, but it should have a better security system; as a single padlock 
is insufficient to deter a thief. One advantage is that the room they are being 
kept in is neither humid nor mouldy. The Himalayan climate, its cold, dry, 
bacteria-free air help to keep the Mardzong collection in good condition. The 
question is what the people of Lo Monthang want to do with the manuscripts. 
The monks have no plans to improve the museum in the near future. There 
is no one trained in the conservation of paper in the village and without that 
knowledge and money this situation will not change. I asked several residents 
of village what they would like to do with the Mardzong collection, but I re-
ceived the same answer over and over again: a vague vision of a museum in the 
“Kathmandu style”, something big and modern. One of those who think this is, 
for example, Tsewang Bista, the president of the Lo Monthang Youth Club, who 
runs the shop selling antiques from Mustang. He underlines that the collec-
tion belongs exclusively to the residents of Lo Monthang and openly admits he 
does not trust any foreign scholars. I also spoke with Pajang Gurung, a thangka 
painter who sells his works to tourists. He is working with Luigi Fieni on the 
restoration of frescoes in the Thubchen monastery. He understands the impor-
tance of undertaking conservation work, and he is passionate about Mustang 
tradition and culture. He was able to explain how important it is for the religion 
Figure 9.8	 Damaged folio from a Bon manuscript

to restore the old wall paintings. Other inhabitants, however, were of different 
opinions. For now there is no plan to upgrade the museum, change the fixtures 
and fittings in the exhibition room or organise the objects according to dat-
ing or provenance. The only measures described in this book were taken by 
our project. A possible reason for the locals’ attitude to the Mardzong collec-
tion and their rejecting the help of foreigners is their lack of both knowledge 
and money, which influences their motivation for change. For the sake of the 
Mardzong collection it would be advisable for the inhabitants of Lo Monthang 
to act as a united community, as the collection is their common legacy and a 
cultural treasure which tells the history of Upper Mustang.
2.3	
A Programme for Preserving the Mardzong Collection
Preservation work on the Mardzong manuscripts needs to begin as soon as 
possible. Since there are no local specialists on paper and book preservation, 
certainly, co-operation with foreign conservation specialists would be helpful 
in protecting their cultural heritage. This would also be important in main-
taining balance between the inhabitants of Lo Monthang and international 
organisations. NGO s should limit their actions to education and support, and 
refrain from imposing western standards. The committee responsible for the 
collection needs to be chosen. All the inhabitants of Lo Monthang need to be 
involved in a discussion regarding a preservation programme. This would help 
the following:
2.3.1	
Conservation Training and Education
The first step in a preservation programme needs to focus on developing aware-
ness of cultural heritage among the inhabitants of Lo Monthang. People need 
to familiarise themselves with the theory of conservation and the regulations 
regarding the protection of relics. Those who take care of the collection must 
also be trained in conservation procedures. Someone amongst the Choede 
Moanstery or Lo Monthang community should be responsible for the collec-
tion. Without foundation further work may be useless. The support of interna-
tional organisations is crucial, especially in the beginning. They may provide 
experts and teachers to guide the locals in the preservation programme.
2.3.2	
Conservation and the Safeguarding of the Collection
Many volumes of the collection are badly damaged and need immediate con-
servation work. Most of the folios are covered with dust, bird feathers and 
droppings, and stains. A number of them have jagged edges. Many pages are 
torn or have holes. A great many painted miniatures and lines of text were cut

The purpose and assumptions of conservation work:
photographic and descriptive documentation before conservation 
treatment;
disinfection and pest control;
cleaning (mechanical removal of dirt, especially bird droppings);
basic repair of paper support;
reconstruction of traditional Tibetan book binding elements;
improvement in the storage condition (shelving arrangements);
periodic checks of the collection

2.3.3	
Preparation of Manuscript Information
Each exhibition needs information about relics, such as the place of origin, 
period and a short description of their function. This information is required 
for the better orientation of visitors. Boards or flyers are very helpful and are 
simple to make. All data should be translated into English, as most of the tour-
ists read English. Another good idea is the promotion of the museum as a tour-
ist attraction in Upper Mustang.
2.3.4	
The Exhibition of Manuscripts
A museum needs to present cultural heritage appropriately. Objects need to be 
sorted into suitable groups, for example, a set of objects from the same period 
of time. Furniture is needed, such as shelves and glass cases and tables. Several 
folios of the collection should be displayed to visitors in a secure manner. The 
miniatures will certainly attract attention. A museum needs adequate electric 
lighting. There should also be an attendant who can answer visitors’ questions.

Chapter 10
Translations of Selected Works from the 
Mardzong Collection
Charles Ramble
This chapter presents the transliterated text and translations of five short 
items contained in the Mardzong repository. The first three are the dedication 
sections of at least three different works—“at least” because in one case the au-
thor mentions works other than the principal text, the Khams chen (or Khams 
brgyad), that were sponsored by the patrons whom he acknowledges. The rea-
son for the selection of these three dedications for translation is that they pro-
vide a range of valuable information: the period concerned and the location 
where the work was carried out, the identities of the sponsors and their fami-
lies, certain codicological features of the works and the names of the different 
craftsmen—scribes, painters and wood-carvers—who were involved in the 
projects. Since the scribes in all these cases were Bonpo monks, in two cases we 
are also given the names of their spiritual master. As discussed below, it is by 
no means sure that one of the three dedications (Triten Norbutse Dedication) 
was found in Mardzong; it is in the possession of Triten Norbutse Monastery 
in Kathmandu, and I am indebted to the abbot, Khenpo Tenpa Yungdrung 
Rinpoche, for permitting me to photograph it.1 Although it is known to have 
been removed from a cave near Lo Monthang in the 1990s, it is not certain that 
the cave in question was in the Mardzong complex. Nevertheless, it is included 
here because it is roughly contemporary with the other two, and because all 
such documents are important parts of an emerging picture of patronage net-
works in the region. The remaining two works have been chosen for translation 
because they are rare examples of little-studied genres of Tibetan literature.
1	 I would like to acknowledge the considerable help given me by Khenpo Tenpa Yungdrung 
Rinpoche in clarifying many of the difficulties encountered in all the texts presented in this 
chapter.

1	
Khams chen Dedication
Khams chen Dedication is a single folio, written in “headed” (dbu can) script, 
with 15 lines on the recto and eight on the verso side. A portion of the lower 
left-hand corner (seen from the recto side) has been torn off, resulting in the 
loss of the first parts of several lines on each side. The figures that are evoked 
by the author are listed in six groups, in descending order of their spiritual and 
social status, as follows:
Group A, transcendent divinities and lineage masters: 1. The Ever-Excellent 
One (Kun tu bzang po), representing the non-dual state of absolute being; 
2. The Five Buddhas Families; 3. sTon pa gShen rab mi bo; 4. The (unnamed) 
masters of the main clans associated with Bon; 5. The discoverer of the Khams 
chen, gShen chen klu dga’.
Group B, the semi-transcendent protective divinities of Bon, who “move on 
the margins of nirvana and samsara”. The only one to be named is Srid pa’i 
rgyal mo.
Group C, the eight disciples of sTon pa gShen rab mo bo who were the first 
recipients of the teachings of the Khams brgyad. All are named.
Group D, three royal lines that have ruled over the region Lo: first, the Yarlung 
Dynasty; secondly, the lineage of Zhang chen tsan tog, and finally the family of 
the current ruler, A ghon (sic, more usually mgon) bzang po (1420–1482) and 
his unnamed siblings. The former, who appears in other sources as Zhang pa 
(or Zhang rung) bTsan thog, was a scion of the Men Zhang sNa tshags pa clan 
that exercised power in the region before the establishment of the kingdom of 
Lo by the forebears of A ma dpal, the father of A mgon dpal, who would come 
to destroy the clan’s remaining influence in the region.2 The attribution of 
the epithet dharmarāja (chos rgyal) to the descendants of bTsan thog is ambig-
uous: on the one hand, insofar as the text describes them as righteous judges 
of this world and the next, the attribution may be an evocation of Yama, the 
arbiter of the dead; alternatively, it may be a thinly-veiled declaration of loyalty 
to a line that the author continued to regard as the legitimate rulers of Lo.
Group E, the sponsors: 1. Kun thub rgyal po, as well as seven named brothers 
(or cousins) and unnamed siblings; 2. The sponsor’s wife, rTod mo srid skyong;3 
3. Other ladies of the family, possibly unmarried sisters of the sponsor; 4. The 
2	 Concerning Zhang pa (or Zhang rung) bTsan thog and the fate of his successors at the hands 
of A mgon dpal, see Vitali 2012: 57, 96, 155–57.
3	 Probably “Srid skyong of the rTod [clan].” Mardzong BA refers to a clan by this name living in 
Tsharka, in Dolpo. The term srid skyong, here apparently a personal name, is usually encoun-
tered as a title, meaning ruler or governor.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

Translations of Selected Works from the Mardzong Collection
sons (three of whom are named) and daughters of the family. The patron’s fam-
ily are described as being residents of Bonkhor (Bon ’khor), a now-abandoned 
settlement located a short distance to the northwest of Lo Monthang. This is 
also the probable location in which the commission was carried out.
Group F, the artisans: 1. The painter dBang chen tshe rtan (sic) and a team 
of unnamed craftsmen from Lo and Dolpo; 2. Two named craftsmen who were 
responsible for the ornamental covers—and were therefore presumably wood 
carvers; 3. The unnamed “teacher and spiritual friends” of the scribe, who 
proofread the work.
2	
Triten Norbutse Dedication
The main part of the text here is preceded by a line of praise to the scribe’s 
teacher, a prominent member of the Ya ngal clan that was, and continues to 
be, widely represented in Dolpo. At the time this text was written the clan was 
also present in Lubrak (Klu brag), in southern Lo, which had been founded by 
one of its members in the 12th century (Fig. 10.1).
The recipients of praise follow the conventional order of precedence:
Group A, transcendent beings, former lineage masters and the teachings of Bon.
Group B, more recent lineage masters: the first is Yang ston dPal bzang, who 
is especially well known as the author of what may be the earliest history of the 
Bonpo rDzogs chen Zhang zhung snyan rgyud lineage.4
Group C, sacred sites in mythical Bonpo lands.
Group D, the realm of Lo and its current ruler, Trashi Gön (bKra shis mgon), 
together with his queen and his sons. The particular location given here is 
Gering (Gad rings), “not far to the south of Lo Monthang.” A reference to this 
site appears in a work composed in 1512: “At Gad smad phu, an area some two 
dpag tshad away from the great capital of sMon thang which was the seat of 
power of chos rgyal bKra shis mgon, [three brothers] established an estate at a 
place known as Ku ti in the centre of Gad ring situated above a protuberance 
of land”.5
4	 Certain authors have (very tentatively) placed him in the 13th–14th centuries: Karmay 1977: 
nos 22, 23; Blezer 2009–2010 passim; Achard 2010: 139. However, since he is mentioned below 
(in group G) as the editor of the work accompanying this dedication, this dating probably 
needs to be revised.
5	 Vitali 2012: 200; 201, fn. 315.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 240 of 332 ---
206
Chapter 10
Group E, the region and people of Sha ru. The identity of this location is uncer-
tain, but it may be a variant of the more usual Sha ri or Sha ris, the eastern part 
of Lo. Two individuals are singled out for praise here. One is named Shes rab 
’od zer, and the other Drang srong brTson tshul. The first is mentioned in the 
context of monastic discipline, and could conceivably refer to Mes ston Shes 
rab ’od zer (1058–1132), an important figure in the transmission of the Bonpo 
vinaya.6 However, in view of the context, it is more likely to denote an un-
identified lama who was a contemporary of the author. The next name, brTson 
tshul, is presumably an abbreviation for brTson grus tshul khrims; the title he 
bears, drang srong, may designate a fully-ordained Bon monk (corresponding 
to the Buddhist dge slong) or else it may be the family name that was adopted 
by the Bya ra sgang clan when some of its members moved to Lo Monthang 
from a nearby settlement to act as the Bon chaplains of the royal lineage.
Group F, The sponsor and his family. The sponsor himself is named vari-
ously as ’Dzom brtan and as gYung drung ’dzom brtan. His wife is named Hor 
lcam, “Hor lady,” which may be a personal name or else may indicate that she 
came from a family with Mongol antecedents. Other members of the fami-
ly are then listed. Not all the individuals mentioned in this group are family 
members since it apparently embraces a larger entourage, perhaps including 
an extended household. One of these is a “southern girl” (mon mo) rGyal po 
dar, who is like a “servant of the gods”, and who is enjoined to “preserve and 
adhere to [her] ancestral tradition”—implying, perhaps, that should not have 
ideas about rising about her station as a hereditary slave or servant from the 
lowlands. Another figure is a certain Dod mo of the A pa clan—that is still to 
be found in some settlements of Upper Lo—who is identified as a local priest 
(lha bon) specialising in the rituals of the two lowest vehicles of the Nine Ways 
6	 See Karmay 1972: xvii.
Figure 10.1

of Bon. Two members of the household’s maternal lineage are worth mention-
ing for the picturesque apotropaic names they bear: one is bDud kyi[s] sbyin—
“gift of the demons”, and the other is Kyi skyugs rgyal—“dog-vomit victor”.7
Group G mentions certain figures who were involved in producing the 
volumes commissioned by the patrons: Yang ston dPal bzang, who is ac-
knowledged (ADD “L”) here for overseeing the proofreading; and one or more 
craftsmen who made the wooden covers and the straps with which to bind the 
volumes. The commissions are identified here as “the mDo’i rgyud chen, the 
most profound of the scriptures, in two volumes, and also the gSer ’od.”8 It 
is not clear which work the title mDo’i rgyud chen—“the great sutra transmis-
sion”—refers to. A clue may be provided on the first available folio (fol. 2r) of 
the gZer mig—itself a possible candidate—that is contained in the Mardzong 
repository. A somewhat enigmatic two-line inscription below the title names 
the couple who feature here, “the patron named ’Dzom brtan and the patron-
ess Hor lcam.” However, the inscription refers to their sponsorship not of the 
gZer mig but of “the scripture(s) of the one known as Seng ge zab mo.” Now, 
Seng ge zab mo (sometimes Seng ge bzang mo) is gShen rab mi bo’s interlocu-
tor in several Perfection of Wisdom works, and it is therefore likely that the text 
in question is one of these—for example, the bKa’ ’dus or the bDal ’bum rtsa ba, 
each of which has two volumes.
Group H, various craftsmen, including wood carvers, calligraphers and art-
ists. These include legendary figures from bygone times as well as others who 
were involved in the commission.
2.1	
Mardzong BA
This work is substantially shorter than the two dedications presented above, 
with just seven lines of dbu can on the recto and eight on the verso. The struc-
ture broadly follows that of the others, beginning with the scribe’s spiritual 
teacher before evoking the ruler of the realm, and finally the patron and his 
family. We are told that the commission, a transcription of a short canonical 
work entitled Na rag pang kong, was ordered by a certain bsTan ’grub ’phan 
ne, a member of the rTod clan in Tsharka, in Dolpo. His wife is named rDo rje 
smal; it may be noted that the name sMal is a common Tibetan rendering of 
the name Malla, suggesting that the lady in question may have been related to 
7	 The term for dog, usually spelt and pronounced khyi in Tibetan, is pronounced kyi—without 
aspiration—in the dialects of Lo.
8	 Possibly a reference to a ritual text for the preservation of good fortune and prosperity en-
titled gSer ’od nor bu mchog rgyal gyi phywa g.yang skyabs pa’i le’u ’dod dgu char ’bebs, con-
tained in the Bon bKa’ rten (vol. 285, pp. 1–19).

the former royal family of Jumla. As in the previous dedication, the scribe pays

2.2	
Mardzong J, Mardzong K
The general features of these two works have been presented in chapter 6, and 
need not be repeated here. It should be noted that the translations provided 
below are provisional, and, especially in the case of Mardzong K, more para-
phrastic at certain points than close renderings. It will be evident that many 
uncertainties remain. Although I hope to undertake more detailed studies of 
these texts in the future, with emendations and more extensive annotations, 
these preliminary treatments are presented here in order to give as broad as 
possible an idea of the content of the works in the Mardzong repository.

3.2	
Translation
Homage to the state of the body of perfection, the Ever-Excellent One, the base 
of everything in samsara and nirvana that has appeared, without exception, 
from the state of the uncontrived pervasive ultimate state of existence, that 
naturally subdues the worlds that are as [numerous as the sand-grains of] the 
River Ganges, primordially pervasive and manifesting throughout the expanse 
of the earth! Within the state of appearances, there is no absolute inherent 
existence; the gods of wisdom have no going or abiding, yet for the sake of sen-
tient beings they appear as if they had conceptual reality, and manifest as the 
five wisdoms that are the antidotes to the five poisons: I prostrate myself to the 
Buddhas of the Five Families! I bow in reverence to the body of the omniscient 
teacher (sTon pa gshen rab), who bears the thirty[-two] major and eighty minor 
signs of excellence, who gives voice to the mute through the [forty] letters (tho 
yig) [that admit access] to the absolute (nges), who has acquired the power of 
non-forgetting and is endowed with omniscient wisdom. The hundred million 
cosmic systems are guided by his manifestations.
I prostrate myself to the body of the omniscient teacher! The doctrines of 
the various vehicles of the teacher’s transmitted words, and especially the 
’Phan yul9 teachings that pacify afflictive emotions: the three Bon teachings 
on the “prospering” outer tantras, that are the thirty-two divine citadels [that 
include] rNam dag sras yum,10 the Four Great Myriads (canonical sections), 
and the Eight Myriads (i.e., Khams brgyad).11 I pay reverence to the Gru, the 
Zhu, the sPa, the rMe and the great Cog lha,12 and to [their master] gShen 
9	 	
One of the categories in the fivefold scheme for classifying the teachings of Bon : “the Four 
Portals and the Treasury, the Fifth” (sGo bzhi mdzod lnga). The Phan yul section contains 
all the sutra and tantra teachings of Bon.
10		
One of the outer tantric cycles.
11		
The “Four Great Myriads”, and the “Eight Myriads”: respectively, the sections of the canon 
and Khams brgyad.
12		
Important clans for the Bon religion.

Chapter 10
chen klu dga’ of the dMu lineage,13 the mandala of the fine qualities of whose 
bodies are resplendent, whose speech emits the sunbeams of the sixty aspects 
[of pure sound], and whose minds do not waver from their space-like nature 
that is free of conceptual elaboration. You, who are endowed with compassion 
and have knowledge through study, and who, as the emanation of the buddhas, 
guide living beings to liberation; the supreme individual who are in full posses-
sion of the immaculate teachings; who show the path to liberation to any living 
being that sees, hears, remembers or touches you; I make offerings to you, who 
are the ornament of14 the outer, inner and secret [lineages]!
And you, who promised to protect the doctrine [in the presence of the 
Teacher [gShen rab], mighty ones who move on the margins of samsara and 
nirvana, judging (ltang ’dzin) right and wrong and keeping watch over the 
Buddha’s word: may you, the unique mother Srid pa’i rgyal mo and the hosts of 
oath-bound protectors, be victorious! How marvellous! You, who manifested for 
the sake of living beings (lacuna) … in the miraculous display of the unmoving 
state (sku) from the manifestation of the unmanifest state, … single in the state 
of equality in the body of the ultimate nature of phenomena; this precious 
aggregate15 from which everything arises has been transmitted pervasively 
from the mind to the body and speech by miracles and by skilful means, which 
admits easy access to all the vehicles, this sovereign of discourse, instructions 
and essential teachings, [that presents] conditioned as well as unconditioned 
phenomena, the two truths of [absolute and provisional reality] (lacuna) like 
the Udumbara flower or a precious jewel: this collection of scriptures that es-
tablished the Eight Domains (of spiritual realisation) was promulgated by the 
Buddha gShen rab, the foremost one of the doctrine, at the palace of Mt Meru, 
at the [divine peak, < rtse mo>] of lHa ri spo ’thon, in the Island of Heaped-up 
Precious things; in the Island of Clear Light; at Ba ra ti ta, on the shores of Lake 
Mu le khyung; on the Island of the Great Ocean, and in the Seven Unchanging 
Families (this is the name of seven mountains around Mt Meru):16 Yid kyi 
khye’u chung; rTo bu ’bum sangs; gSal ba ’od ldan; Med khams stong pa rje; 
Tshangs pa gtsug phud; Klu mo Ma ma ti; gTsug gshen rgyal ba; gSang ba ngang 
rings. [The teachings were the following eight realms:] Srid pa gleng bzhi; Rin 
chen rgyun; sNang ba; sTong pa rje; So sor thar pa; Rig pa; (lacuna) [dByings 
khams; mNyam pa nyid kyi khams]. These were the Eight Realms that he

The eminent treasure-revealer (996–1035) who is credited with the discovery of the 
Khams chen, among many other works.

The translation assumes that the syllables rgyan and gyi{s} have been reversed.

taught. Thus the teaching forms 16 volumes, in 81 chapters [subdivided into] 
360 sections and 1,001 subsections. To express it in terms of words, that would 
come to many thousands of millions, and because there are 100,000 quatrains 
it is known as “The Myriad”. For the benefit of the body, there is the teaching 
of the Rin chen spyi spungs; for the benefit of speech, there is the So sor rtogs 
pa (Individual realisation) collection; and for the sake of the mind there is the 
Yang dag don (Pure state) collection.
As for the Bye ma nyi khri chu ’bum, it is known as the “‘gShen rab’ Quatrains”; 
this collection of the Eight Realms [of realisation] is known as the “Hundred 
Thousand Million-Word Summary”. The collection that was compiled for the 
pure klu derives its name from the number of [kinds of] klu that there are.17 As 
for the White Collection of the Srid pa, the gSas of the Phenomenal World, [it 
is so called because …] (lacuna).
Teachings the omniscient gShen rab gave are classified into four great col-
lections; the group of disciples he trained were individuals of good karma; 
and the reason he gave his teachings was to guide the living beings of the six 
realms. If you hear these teachings and act in accordance with them, it will 
be as if someone who was desirous of precious things went to such an island: 
whatever anyone at all may be in search of, he or she will find it. (lacuna)
Homage to the sovereign who was ever mindful of the happiness of sentient 
beings; … (lacuna) descended from the gods, the immaculate descendant of 
gNya’ gri tsan po, who protected the realm as if it were his own son, and es-
chewed partiality. Hey! Homage to the descendant(s?) of … (lacuna) the emi-
nent lords from the clan of the great Zhang Tsan tog, judging right and wrong 
like a (or the) dharmarāja, excellently [dispensing] (lacuna) the laws of this 
life and the hereafter!
How marvellous! Homage to the drung chen A ghon bzang po and his sib-
lings; to that great lord from a mighty and awesome line, who has caused the 
Buddha’s doctrine to prosper and flourish, who is victorious over all enemies 
from the outside and zealous in examining the lawless and bringing them 
to justice.
(lacuna) … in the Southern Continent, to the east of that most exceptional 
holy place, Mt Tise, to the south of the eastward-flowing Pagshu River,18 in 
the centre of this land of Lo where the nourishing grain grows, here, in this 
great place of Bonkhor (Bon ’khor) that rises aloft; descended from the great 
lDong clan, one of the [six original] Tribes of the “Little Men”, a noble clan … 
17		
Certainly a reference to the Klu ’bum; see chapter 3.
18		
The name of a river that flows through the mythical land of ’Ol mo lung ring, and is often 
applied to the Tsangpo (Brahmaputra) that flows north of Lo.

Chapter 10
(lacuna) … a mighty benefactor of those who hold the doctrine, the patrons 
Kun thub rgyal po, Blo gros rgyal mtshan, dKar mo dar ro, rGyal mtshan g.yung 
drung, Grags pa rgyal mtshan, Kun bzang ’bum and Kyi khu rgyal mtshan, Kyi 
khu dar re and other siblings. (lacuna) … having fully provided the financial 
wherewithal without (lacuna), with a view to repaying the kindness of their 
parents and to accomplish in full the dual accumulations (of merit and wis-
dom) for this life and the next, [they commissioned] this set of volumes of the 
Eight Realms, that are [part of] the excellent teachings [of gShen rab]. [The 
work] consists of sixteen volumes with a gold-lettered first page, with a carved 
outer cover and gods and other figures in the chapters inside. (lacuna) The 
foremost one of the doctrine [gShen rab himself] and the entourage of [eight] 
gshen who requested teachings from him; the five gods that make water offer-
ings, and the thirty-two stupas; the offerings of the seven royal attributes,19 the 
myriad names of the buddhas and the three collections of dhāraṇis: all these 
have been created in precious gold, and are a delight to the heart. The lady 
with a [beautiful?] form, (lacuna), kind hearted and belonging to the ḍākiṇī 
class; the patroness rTod mo srid skyong, from a good family, beautiful and 
wise, a noble lady with all the accomplishments and with prosperity and glory 
in abundance; a well-mannered and magnanimous lady who brings harmony. 
[Next,] our patronesses (lacuna) … mtsho mo, ’Brug skyes, ’Phan phyug ’dren 
and others who revered us by preparing food with nectar, unwavering in their 
devotion and ever cheerful. Your sons, Khyung mo bkra shis, bKra shis ’bar, Yi 
dam skyab, Gye god ’bum and their other brothers and sisters; may they be free 
from obstacles (bar [chad]) and (lacuna), and may they prosper greatly. [The 
Khams chen] contains passages (kha gang) in Zhangzhung language at the be-
ginning, the end and in the middle; the beginning of the mTshan ’bum and 
the extended collection of dhāraṇis: these were expertly done by the highly 
learned dBang chen tshe rtan and by skilful artisans (dpon chen mkhas) of Lo 
and Dolpo who were assembled, after long training. The ordinary text was writ-
ten by the dextrous (lacuna) with great care. The outer cover, with its golden 
shine and its radiance, was carved by Chos skyong skyabs and the one known 
as mKhas pa (Scholar). The proofreading that ensures that there were no mis-
takes of insertion or omission was meticulously carried out by the teacher and 
our spiritual friends. Our dearly beloved patrons and their near ones (lacuna) 
and all those who gave advice and extended their support—we were delighted 
by them. May all the virtue that this will have generated in the past, the present 
and the future, having thoroughly cleared the darkness of all living beings and

4.2	
Translation
May Nyi ma dpal bzang, the lineage-holder of the teachers of the Ya ngal clan, 
flourish like an eternally brandished victory banner of the doctrine.
Om swa sti siddham. May good fortune and happiness flourish and pervade 
all the four directions. In this mandala of the pure realms, the mandala of the 
five cosmic buddhas, unmodified and free of elaboration in the state of thor-
oughly immaculate great fulfilment that is Pure Nature, I pay homage with 
reverence to the spontaneously-arisen perfected dimension. This perfected 
dimension, with rays of light shining brightly from its form, comes as a succes-
sion of teachers who teach according to the capacities of those who are taught, 
and effect the maturation and liberation of living beings through compassion. 
Reveal the state of absolute truth and distinguish the example and its signifi-
cance. Seeing absolute meaning, explain the meaning through the example. 
I prostrate myself with reverence to the host of the ocean of scriptures that 
are light emitted from the heart. Raise the victory-banner of the teacher that 
claims the place of the doctrine; lamp that dispels the darkness of ignorance 
and lights the path to liberation; be the lord of the doctrine who understands 
the teaching, the instruction and the meaning.

I prostrate myself with reverence to the body of Yangtön Palzang. I offer 
homage to the bodies of the lamas of the precious vinaya,20 the teachers of the 
Gru family who clarify the doctrine, who hold the 250 rules of the monastic 
code and guide living beings through his realisation of the empty nature of 
the Great Vehicle. Be a mighty guide on the path (?) and show living beings 
the way. I pay full and open homage to the bodies of the lamas of the monastic 
seats who preserve the doctrine so carefully, who are mighty guides on the 
path and who show living beings the way, who spread the Yangtön doctrine 
and take care of the monks who comprise the petals of the circle.
How marvellous! This realm of gShen rab, the unbearable hundred billion 
worlds, is under the clear blue sky that is like azure, hundreds of millions of 
celestial systems that constitute the (lacuna) … this place of treasures and 
gold where the teacher’s foot trod, this centre of the scapula-shaped continent 
where the doctrine spread, Olmo Lingchen, the palace where the doctrine 
dwells; the wondrous and beautiful Karnag Trasal,21 [and?] Shampo Lhatse, 
made from precious things; Yungdrung Gutseg where one might achieve ac-
complishments; Senge Gyabnöl (a rock cave at Mt dGu brtsegs, “Two Adossed 
Lions”), the basis of all power-places. Here I have presented a little about what 
there is in the land of Tazig.
How marvellous! To the east of the snow-mountain Tise and the lake 
Mapang, to the right of the slowly-flowing river Pagshu,22 in the centre of Ngari 
Tsang, this central land of accumulated/perfected virtues, there in Mönthang, 
that is like a divine garden. Through the power generated by a hundred excel-
lent merits, a place trodden by millions of lords of men, glorious one who holds 
the victory banner of the two laws that shall never decline, great dharmarāja 
bKra shis mgon, his siblings, his queen and his sons, with immeasurable merit 
the peak of which brushes the sky, and the sweet fragrance (gsung < bsung) of 
whose fame fills the earth and its surroundings—may this wish-fulfilling tree 
who protects the land be victorious! Here, not far to the south of auspicious 
Monthang, the palace of the ruler, in the upper part of the prosperous place 
named Gering (Gad rings), the peak of Lhanyen Düri (lHa gnyan bdud ri) is 
lofty and splendid; the peak to the right is glorious and the peak to the left is 
graceful (sgyegs < sgeg); the irrigation sections (mu ma) of the dice-like fields 
where the nutrition of the grain ripens are like fields of gold; the river water to

How marvellous! Here in this prosperous land of Sha ru, standing proud 
of the earth, the people are of illustrious descent, the lineage of the great 
father(s) who were accomplished masters, endowed with the three studies like 
a vase filled with water, pure in his adherence to the monastic law, and guard-

and of meditation: praise to Shes rab ’od zer. The one who strove earnestly 
after the secret tantric teachings, holding firmly onto the tenets: high praise 
to Drang srong brTson tshul (brTson grus tshul khrims?). The one who enjoys

beautifully ornamenting the crowns of our heads: high praise to Slob dpon 
rGyal tshul.
How marvellous! Our devout sponsor, our patron ’Dzom brtan, beautifully 
ornamented with his glorious wealth; intelligent, striving after virtue in his 
splendour, with the heroic strength to defeat his strongest enemies, and pro-
tecting even his lowest subjects with his intellect; having defeated his powerful 
enemies one after another, causing our lord ruler great gladness. Homage to

and wealth, you have the power to compete with Vaiśrāvana. I rejoice in your

radiant appearance and the graceful countenance, your intelligent behaviour 
seems to be in conflict (’drad for ’gras) with that of the goddesses. Like the 
goddess Nor rgyun, whatever is left with you (gang bzhag) increases. You are 
devoted to virtue, and strive to write out the scriptures. You are as radiant as a 
manifestation of [the ḍākiṇī] Co za bon mo; you protect your circle of depen-
dents with skilful means, and strive after virtue with devotion. You transform 
food into the taste of nectar, delighting the objects of your reverence [i.e. the 
monkish scribes]. Praise to you, our patroness, Hor lady! Manifestation of ’Ong 
co, devoutly striving after virtue: praise to you, most excellent Shes rab bzang 
mo! Like a daughter of the gods who has come to be among humans, even the 
flowers in the garden feel ashamed and run away; you are outstandingly beau-
tiful in this grove! You lovely daughters, dPal ’dzom and your sister. And may 
you, southern girl rGyal po dar, who are like a servant of the gods, preserve and 
adhere to your ancestral tradition. The one who holds the three teachings, and 
has visited all sacred places: homage to you, dge shes Rin chen rgyal mtshan. 
The one who knows in detail the vehicles of the Phya gshen and of the sNang 
gshen, manifestly exalted among the lha bon: homage to you, A pa Dod mo!

How wondrous! Our patron ’Dzom brtan and our patroness Hor lcam, who 
are thus, in order to draw the essence from the illusion that is material wealth 
they lavishly sponsored a transcription of the mDo’i rgyud chen, the most pro-
found of the scriptures, in two volumes, and also the gSer ’od.23 The editor in 
charge of the proof-reading was Yang ston dPal bzang. The craftsman who 
made the strap and the wooden covers24 was excellent. And moreover, as re-
gards the provisions of meat and beer, the young women who provisioned us 
with meat and beer were astonishing in their rapidity, which was like lightning 
flashing in the sky.
How wondrous! I prostrate humbly to the hosts of bygone scholars, such as 
the craftsman of old Khyung po g.yu khri,25 and the scholars of a later era sBal

dang sum pa and Shes rab ’bum.26 The passages of praise in the language of 
Zhangzhung and so forth were inscribed in an ordinary way (rgyus < dkyus) 
without cloth. According to the usage of craftsmen (mkhas pa; or scribes?),

ocean, continue forever to flow like an inexhaustible stream of nectar. Through 
the pure virtue of transcribing this work may I see the faces of the buddhas of 
the three times, and may I receive the auspicious blessings of the triple gem. 
May this abundant wealth, the treasure of Vaisravana, adorn his children and 
his household as if they were Indra in all his finery. Blessings and virtue.

May all be auspicious and well in all directions and times and situations! 
Homage to the unborn body of the ultimate nature of phenomena that is like 
space; to the body of perfection that is adorned with the major and minor signs 
like the sun and the moon; and to the bodies of the teachers of past, present 
and future, the manifestation bodies that appear individually like the mass of 
stars. I pay homage to the body of the gracious Yang ston dPal ldan bzang po, 
who is indistinct from the ultimate truth of the true teaching, keeping at a dis-
tance those who hold wrong views concerning the extremes of subject and ob-
ject, who through the power of his recitation of mantras liberates the enemies 
of the doctrine. Praise to the monks of the sangha, that vase full of the water 
of completely pure moral discipline, and who are thoroughly knowledgeable 
in the meaning of all the scriptures. Praise to the great ruler bKra shis mgon, 
seated majestically in the middle of all the myriarchies here, in this pleasure-
garden that is the great capital Monthang, upon a wheel of mighty wind that 
supports everything, in Zal mo sgang,27 the land of Tibet in which the doctrine 
flourishes, beneath the deep blue sky like azure.
27		
Apparently a pious assimilation to the well-known area in Khams, or else to a sacred site 
of the same name in the vicinity of Mt Kailash.

Chapter 10
Hey! Here in this community of Tsharka, full of virtue and rising proudly out 
of the earth, this famous and celebrated man of the rTod clan, a fine family and 
lineage, on excellent terms with all his fellow citizens thanks to his diplomat-
ic skills: praise to our patron bTsan grub ’phan ne! Praise to our patron Tshul 
khrims legs pa, the young man of clear intellect, who gets on well with both 
the exalted and the humble and who strives earnestly after virtue through his 
magnanimity! Praise to our lady patron, rDo rje smal, the wise woman of good 
descent and lofty family who, through her deep faith delights the foremost of 
those who are to be honoured! praise to our lady patron bSod nams sgron, 
ong co’i r—, lady of distinguished lineage, endowed with wisdom and on good 
terms with all! Beautiful as flowers, and named Nor bu rgyan and ’Dren ne; in 
this way our patron bTsan grub ’phan ne, commissioned the production of this 
Na rag pang kong in order to repay the kindness of her mother. The scribe was 
gYung drung ’od zer. May all our patrons enjoy auspicious good fortune!

Fol. 3r
1.	 ma shor ba: gsal thag rnam thug du ma shor ba: tshod can dang nog po 
la nor po yo[1]: spyang po dang mgo stong la nor po yod: pho rab khog 
yangs dang rdzu khog nas zab tshol: pho ’bring nor sog dang ’jam
A person of moderation and an insignificant individual may be mistaken for 
each other; a clever person and an empty-headed one may be mistaken; care 
should be taken to distinguish a fine man with an open heart from a false man, 
and to distinguish an average man who has accumulated wealth from one with 
a gentle nature;
2.	 khog nas zab tshol / tha ma stag shar dang phrug thabs nas zab tshol/

snying dang ’dra ba lce rtsar bkung (?)/ dgra rang dang ’bras pa rjed du 
ma ’jug/ ’phrad ma thag du
care should be taken when fighting with an inferior young man. Food that 
tastes good in your mouth should be given to others. Attractive goods should 
be kept for oneself, and information that is [as important as] one’s own heart 
should be kept at the root of the tongue. Never forget an enemy who is in con-
flict with you.

Do not speak openly to someone you have only just met; do no let your words 
come freely to someone with you have only just become familiar (’dris < ’dres). 
Do not break into a smile as soon as you’re pleased; do not let your face fall as 
soon as you’re angry. Do not curl your lip at something that displeases you; do 
not become too accustomed to anything that tastes good; do not go hungry or 
turn up your nose at something that does not taste good.

A rich man should know how to enjoy his wealth; a hungry man should know 
how to be thrifty; when there is plenty you should know how to be happy; 
when there is shortage, you should withstand misery. Food that gives satisfac-

concealed within. Since consultation causes good fortune (dbyang < g.yang) to

If you seek to be lofty, you should protect the lowly; if you wish to be great, 
protect the small; if you wish to be righteous, you should observe the law (or 
be disciplined);
2.	 ’dod na khyi gsos / spungs ’dod na ’khor rkyong / phyug ’dod na zhib thig

if you wish to sleep, take good care of your dog; if you want an armed force, 
look after those around you; if you wish to be rich, be meticulous in your ac-
counting; if you want to be scholarly, study; if you wish to be of value, dispense 
with pride; if you wish to be happy, abide with the lowly (keep a low profile); 
if you wish to be content in this life and happy in the next, pursue the divine 
dharma;
3.	 na sos brtol / ’khros na mdangs skyed / sro thung na ngang sring / khams

if you are overly busy, take some rest; if you are angry, generate a composed de-
meanour; if you are quick to anger, cultivate your temper; if you’re withdrawn, 
develop expansiveness; if you’re afraid, develop courage; if you lack wealth, 
develop thriftiness; if you lack clothing and footwear, develop caution (? ca 
ngab); cultivate attention to detail and modesty: [not to do so] would be a vio-
lation of sound advice.
4.	 kyi sdig / nyal khar sings po ma ’thung dros kyi sdig / chang sar bzhug du

When you are old, do not take a young wife—this would be unadvisable; do

last to leave a wedding feast—this is unadvisable; being cautious towards an 
enemy is not a sign of cowardice; giving food to your relatives is not a sign of 
recklessness; embracing wrongdoing should not be your fare; it is no trifle to be 
an adherent of the divine religion;

There are three policies a man should observe: if there is a strong enemy

(Translation begins from line 4)
Concerning reconciliation (da gos? but see da dgos in previous line) in strife 
and conflict: generally, the merit of living beings is presently in decline, and 
the compassion of the Buddhas has been obscured, while bodhisattvic altru-
ism is forgotten. Living beings act out of desire and self-interest, and strife and 
conflict have come about (rga < gda’? brda’?). Examples may be cited. It is said 
that, once upon a time, the gods and demigods fought because they did not 
reach an agreement over the fruit of the Yongs su sa rdol [tree]; the serpent-
spirits fought because they did not reach an agreement over a jewel; the five 
srin po siblings and the nine ma sangs siblings did not reach an agreement over 
the [outcome of?] a contest; the six families of rgyal shar ba (unknown) fought 
because they did not reach an agreement over their respective ranks (lit. ar-
rogance, haughtiness);

’od ’bar fought over the dra ma rtam (?); the khyeng po fought over property; 
the khyeng ngal fought over food. Generally, then, there is conflict even in the 
realm of the gods, while resolution may be achieved (dgyed?) [even] in the 
realm of the demons. Generally, if there is a fight and no reconciliation, that 
is because of karmic enmity: the snake and the weasel are karmic enemies, as 
are the crow and the owl, the sparrowhawk and the sparrow, the horse and the 
yak, the sheep and the wolf. That these cannot reconcile is said to be because 
they are karmic enemies. The fault is due to the feebleness of their reasoning. 
But we humans are endowed with reason, and, even if there is conflict, it will 
not do not to reconcile. Such things should be said as appropriate to the occa-
sion. Here ends chapter 28, concerning the general framework (? da dgos) for 
strife and conflict.
As for the key to binding the ’then shags, [say as follows]: Hey, friends! Don’t 
seek to harm your opponent; don’t try to take advantage. Don’t try to smell 
something that has no smell;

Now you should make this speech to extol and honour them. Listen, friends! 
In the place where the white lion has shown his splendour, the graveyard fox, 
seeking food, may emit an ugly yelp; when the striped tiger leaps, the weasel, 
the companion of the fox, taking exercise, will tear the flesh inside its thighs 
and die. In the place where the wild yak struts haughtily, the old black bull, 
threatening it with its horns, may be tossed on the horns of that yak. In the 
place where the steed gallops, the long-eared donkey, trying to compete with 
it, without even realising that it has lost its hoof, dies. Friends, don’t count on 
those in high places (or, don’t count on those in high places to be your friends?). 
Both the things that you long for and your own downfall may come at the same 
time. Bear it firmly in mind that trusting [just] your own side will lead to your 
downfall. Without force/ without taking sides …?

Since you are proud and powerful, and I, the mediator, am weak, the two of you 
should not compete in might. Reduce your pride; agree to give ground; aban-
don the precipitous place that is the mountain of the dead. Whose fault was 
it first? Then, who removed the animals? And finally, who took the … ? (ngas). 
Saying “this was my fault”, “that was his fault”, speak gently and concisely. We 
know that what we generally refer to as “speech” can gore without horns; it 
can strike without being thrown; it can pierce without a point; [something] 
flung at a wild argali might strike your domestic goat; if you loose an arrow in 
the dark, it might hit a god in the eye—so it is said, and therefore please speak 
gently; [your opponent] too will desist from speaking roughly. Say these things 
as appropriate to the circumstances. Here ends chapter 35, which reveals the 
key to the advice given by the mediator.
This [next chapter] is known as “the passing (gcong = gcod?) of judgment 
and the purification”. There are nine forms of passing judgment: For killing 
there is the sentence of blood-price; for wounding there is the sentence of 
blood; for humiliation … (text ends).
