Chapter 11
A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong 
Collection: Translation and Preliminary Remarks
Charles Ramble and Naljor Tsering
1	
Introduction1
The justification for dedicating a whole chapter to this fragmentary manuscript 
is the rarity of its subject-matter: this text may be the only known Tibetan work 
on the topic of toxicology that actually deals with the preparation and use of 
poison. Inasmuch as it is concerned with the little-explored subject of poi-
son and poisoning in Tibet, the text touches on a much larger field of enquiry 
comprising several disciplines, notably the anthropology of Tibetan societies, 
Indian and Tibetan medical literature and praxis, and a number of natural 
sciences including pharmacology, botany and mineralogy. Given the limited 
scope of the present chapter, it will clearly be impossible to do justice to the 
numerous avenues of exploration suggested by the text, and we will confine 
ourselves to brief discussions of some of these domains in the hope that they 
might form the basis for more detailed investigations at a later date.
2	
Poisoning in Tibet
When we encounter poison in Tibetan works it is usually in a religious context, 
where poison is a metaphor for the three or five toxic emotions—ignorance, 
hatred and lust, to which pride and envy are sometimes added. Poison also fea-
tures in different ritual contexts. Many Bonpo rituals begin with a procedure 
1	 We are grateful to a number of people for their helpful suggestions, especially with regard 
to Indian sources that may have influenced the ideas contained in the text considered 
here. These include Camillo Formigatti, Axel Michaels, Patricia Sauthoff, Michael Slouber, 
Herman Tieken and Christophe Vielle. Special thanks are due to Jonathan Silk for kindly fa-
cilitating our quest for this information. Owing to limitations of space we have not been able 
to incorporate all the findings resulting from the valuable leads received, and all mistakes 
are of course our own. We are also indebted to Khenpo Tenpa Yungdrung for his suggestions 
concerning the interpretation of certain obscure passages in the text. Fernand Meyer’s con-
tribution to this chapter will become evident in the following pages.
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 285 of 332 ---
251
A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong Collection
called dug ’phyung, the “expulsion of poison,” in which a mixture of ingredi-
ents considered to be the poisonous aspects of other substances are burned 
prior to the performance of the main ritual. The ritual has its foundation in 
a myth concerning the emergence of poison from the bile of a demon during 
the origin of the world.2 Exorcism rituals also make use of poison. The instruc-
tions for the preparation of a paper effigy, a ling ga, for use in destructive rites 
specifies that the paper should be “poisonous paper, one span in height,” and 
the stylus should be “made from an arrow that has been used to kill a yeti.” The 
ink in which the magical syllables were written should be a mixture of “blood 
from a bear that has been killed with a sword, the blood of a mule that has died 
from poisoning, and the blood of a mad dog.”3 Although many plants from the 
Thymeleaceae family have toxic properties, according to Lama Sherab Tenzin 
of Samling (Dolpo), the term “poisonous paper” (dug shog) refers particularly 
to paper that has been made from the re lcag pa plant (Stellera chamaejasme).
Physical poison also features in Tibetan literary and oral narratives, as 
well as in travellers’ accounts and, to a lesser extent, in ethnographic works. 
According to some of the earliest Tibetan sources, the death of the eighth king, 
Drigum Tsenpo, was avenged by means of an insidious gift to his assassin of 
two dogs, whose fur was tainted with a contact poison that penetrated his skin 
when he stroked them.4 The Dunhuang literature contains numerous narra-
tives of poisoning;5 Milarepa died from ingesting the poisoned curd that a jeal-
ous monk had given him, with symptoms that would have been intolerable to 
a lesser person: when the saint transferred the pain to a door the wood split, 
and when he transferred just half of it to his assailant, at the latter’s insistence, 
the agony the monk felt caused him to repent.6 One of the recipes given in 
Mardzong A describes a paste of toxic substances that should be worked into 
the shape of a cake of dung and, when dried, left in the house of the victim. The 
smoke from this preparation is said to produce a leprosy-like skin disease when 
it is inadvertently used as fuel in the stove. A reference to the use of smoke 
poison appears in an episode from the Gesar epic, where Khro thung confesses 
to the hero an attempt he has made on his life:
With four iron stakes I stretched [you] wide,
And into the Dharma palace of your heart
I struck the sharpest stake.
2	 Karmay 1998, 142.
3	 Ramble 2015, 562.
4	 Haarh 1969, 146.
5	 Lalou 1959.
6	 Evans-Wentz 2000 [1969], 266.

Chapter 11
And into the poisonous blazing fire of dung
[I put] the soles of hundred female lepers,
The soles of hundred male lepers,
The skirts of a hundred widows,
The pubic hairs of a hundred harlots,
The pubic hairs of a hundred adulterers
And bones of humans, dogs and horses and so on …7
Historical accounts include the death of the Fifth Reting Regent in 1947 in a 
Lhasa prison. The poison that he ingested, said to have been delivered in the 
form of three pills that he had requested as a treatment for a malaise, result-
ed in his dying in audible pain, giving support to another explanation for his 
death advanced by certain reliable sources: that he had been killed by having 
his testicles crushed.8 Various accounts refer to successful or failed attempts 
on the lives of the Dalai and Panchen Lamas. The Eighth Panchen Lama, for 
example, was rumoured to have been poisoned by his own mother, while the 
father of the Ninth incarnation died after eating poisoned food intended for 
his wife by his lover.9 As recently as 2012, the present Dalai Lama is reported 
to have suspected an attempt on his life by Chinese agents masquerading as 
female Tibetan devotees, who planned to assassinate him through contact 
with their poisoned bodies, hair, or clothing, a plot that is curiously redolent of 
the theme of the viṣakanyā—the “venomous virgin”—that appears in certain 
Indian works on statecraft and Āyurveda (see below).
Apart from the use of poison as a means of assassination for personal or 
political interests, there are also widespread beliefs in Tibet about the exis-
tence of poison cults. Such cults are held to be concentrated in certain parts 
of the country, particularly Kongpo, a region that is associated with poisoning 
in works as early as the gZer mig (11th or 12th century). Beliefs concerning the 
practice are recorded by one of the authors of this chapter, who carried out 
research in Kongpo in the 1980s and gives the following account:
The poisoners, I was told, are generally old people. The aim of the ex-
ercise is to obtain the g.yang (the propensity to good fortune) of one’s 
victim. Poison should not act immediately but after a suitable interval,

when the victim is far enough away not to compromise the killer by dying 
conspicuously after his hospitality. A good poison might take effect only 
several years after being ingested.
People were divided about the nature of this substance: some claimed 
that it was a plant that the poisoners secretly cultivate in their houses, 
while others favoured a subtle preparation of numerous fiendish ingredi-
ents. One area of consistency in the reports is that the poison could only 
be infused into moist foods: one might safely accept tsampa, for instance, 
but milk, beer and fresh meat were dangerous. One should be especially 
cautious about eggs, into which the venom can be introduced through 
the shells with sharp thorns.10
A more detailed investigation of similar beliefs in Bachen, in eastern Tibet, 
has been published by Giovanni Da Col (2012). The author’s interest in poison-
related beliefs is primarily to do with their deleterious effect on the normal 
Tibetan patterns of hospitality, in which fear of being poisoned is understood 
as “the reversal of the Tibetan economies of fortune.”11 For now, however, it is 
the mechanism of poisoning that particularly concerns us. Here, as in Kongpo, 
the purpose of poisoning is to divert the g.yang of the victim to the person and 
household of the perpetrator. The poison is believed to operate through the 
agency of a “poison god” (dug lha), who infiltrates his offspring, referred to as a 
worm (mbu, Tib. ’bu), into the victim’s body, where it grows and slowly saps his 
or her vitality. According to a local doctor who was considered to be an expert 
on poison,
poisoning symptoms begin with stomach pain followed by vomiting and 
black-coloured urine. The symptoms vary according to whether it is snake 
poison (stomach ache only), frog poison (swollen throat, difficulty in 
swallowing), or centipede poison, the worst kind (stomach pain but with 
the feeling of eaten from the inside) … Lucky victims would have visions 
of what is happening in their bodies, and even visualize the snake, the 
frog, or the centipede; the unlucky would not see anything. The patient 
capable of visualizing a fully grown animal in his or her body is doomed 
to die. The doctor argued that the son of the poison-god would behave as 
a mbu, an intestinal worm. The most peculiar part of the therapy is the 
diet, the way one should feed one’s internal ‘alien’. One should only eat 
the food the alien likes, but in small doses. Tibetan sugar (purmam), and

corpse through one of the orifices. A monk from Gyamda village swore

who had allegedly been poisoned … A poisoned victim is not murdered 
but is slowly parasitized by the embryo of the poison-god until the new-
born abomination is forced to find a new host.12
The beliefs expressed in these acounts do not seem to find any resonance in 
our text, Mardzong A, leading us to think that this work derives from a different 
tradition of toxicology. There is no mention of the idea that the purpose of poi-
soning might be to appropriate the victim’s prosperity; but we should be cau-
tious about drawing any such conclusions: as we will see presently, the surviving 
material may be no more than a quarter of the text, and we do not know what 
might be contained in the missing portions apart from the sections announced 
in first folio.13 It is true that some of the poisons prescribed take months and 
even years to act, but the purpose of the delay is not to allow time for as much as 
possible of the victim’s vitality to be diverted, but apparently rather to avoid de-
tection. One of the poisons does mention “[a mass of] worms the size of a fist”

3	
Indian Traditions of Toxicology
As we shall see, Mardzong A is a remarkable work inasmuch as it deals with all 
aspects of poison: the classification of poisons, their production and use, and 
the preparation and application of therapeutic antagonists. While our inves-
tigations have not led us to any Indian sources that treat the topic of poison 
quite so comprehensively, there are certain works that deal particularly with 
the use of poison, and others that are concerned with the treatment of its ef-
fects. Detailed advice on the use of poison is given in Kautilya’s Arthaśāstra, 
the Indian classic of statecraft. Book 14, “On Esoteric Practices,” opens with two 
chapters that are of particular relevance in this regard. Chapter 1 of this book 
is entitled “Secret means of killing enemy troops,” and chapter 2 “Deception.” 
Both of these chapters, and particularly the former, contain elaborate recipes 
for poison. The poisons are to be infiltrated into the desired locations by agents 
who are unlikely to be suspected, such as “Well-liked women from barbarian 
groups, masquerading as hunchbacks,” and people “with credible disguises in 
terms of region, attire, craft, language and birth.”14 The poisons are to be admin-
istered by being smeared on the clothing or armour that is to be worn, mixed 
with food or drink, or, in a large number of cases, burned to produce smoke 
that causes blindness, disease, insanity or death. To cite just one example:
Powder made from Pūtikīṭa-stinkbug, fish, bitter gourd, Śatakardama-
plant, Idhma, and Indragopa-insect, or powder made from Pūtikīṭa-
stinkbug, Kṣudrā, Arālā, Hema, and Vidarī, mixed with powder made from 
the horns and hooves of a goat—smoke from that causes blindness.15
In just a few cases the treatment for the symptoms induced is specified. Thus 
after three recipes for formulae that cause leprosy, such as “applying the bile 
and the fluid in the egg of a parrot to the entire body”, we are told that “a decoc-
tion made with the pulp of Priyāla is a remedy for leprosy.”16

Olivelle 2013, 421.

Chapter 11
The topic of poisoning is extensively covered in the Suśrutasaṃhitā, which, 
together with the Carakasaṃhitā, constitute the main corpus of literature on 
Āyurveda.17 The work comprises six books, and the fifth, entitled Kalpasthāna, 
is dedicated to poison. The first chapter is concerned mainly with precautions 
that should be taken against poisoning, with advice on such things as ensur-
ing that one has a trustworthy cook, how to identify poisoners, and the differ-
ent media that an enemy might use for delivering the poison to its target.18 In 
addition to some of the more predictable methods (food, drink, massage oil, 
combs, cosmetics, clothing and so forth) the chapter is at special pains to warn 
kings against the dangers of the “venomous virgin” (viṣakanyā, mentioned 
above), a young woman who has been immunised to poison from infancy but 
who can transmit it to her victim through contact, breath or even her gaze. The 
viṣakanyā is “a stock character from Sanskrit drama and narrative, a solitary 
figure of seduction and danger, a lethal secret weapon of assassination wielded 
by unscrupulous kings and ministers.”19
As a medical treatise, the Suśrutasaṃhitā understandably does not de-
scribe procedures for the preparation and administration of poisons, but is 
concerned rather with their classification, identification of the symptoms of 
poisoning, methods of prevention and recipes for antidotes. The classification 
of toxins is the subject of the second chapter: “It is said that there are two kinds 
of poisons, stationary and mobile. The former dwells in ten places, the latter 
has 16 locations. Traditionally, the ten are: root, leaf, fruit, flower, bark, milky 
sap, pith, resin, the elements, and the tuber.”20 Regarding the immobile poi-
sons, the work identifies eight root poisons, five leaf poisons, twelve fruit poi-
sons, five flower poisons, seven poisons from bark pith and resin, three milky 
sap poisons, two element-poisons and thirteen tuber poisons, giving a total 
of fifty-five.21 The general features of mobile poisons are given in Chapter 3. 
Chapters 4–5 deal with snakebite, chapter 6 poisoning by rats, chapter 7 the 
treatment of poisoning by means of the sound of a medicated drum, and 
chapter 8 poisoning by insects, other arthropods (centipedes, spiders and scor-
pions) and frogs.22 Treatments for poisoning, particularly from snakebite and 
especially—though by no means only—with the use of mantras are discussed

A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong Collection
in considerable detail in Michael Slouber’s fine study of the Gāruḍa Tantras, 
but a systematic comparison of the understanding of poison in these works 
and in Mardzong A would be well beyond the scope of the present chapter.
The different taxonomies given in the Suśrutasaṃhitā do not correspond 
exactly to those that appear in Mardzong A, but the general principles suggest 
that there is a shared base of medical and toxicological schemes. In fact, it is in 
the Man ngag rgyud, the third of the Four Treatises (rGyud bzhi) of the Tibetan 
medical tradition that we find the closest correspondences to the content of 
Mardzong A. The subject of toxicology is one of the eight branches (aṣṭāṅga) 
of Tibetan medicine and of the Indian tradition from which this classification 
derives.23 By way of a prelude to an examination of the sections that are per-
haps most relevant to the present chapter, we can do no better than to pres-
ent an overview of the topic as it has been succinctly summarised by Fernand 
Meyer in a personal communication:
The branch concerning toxicology is set out in three chapters of the Man 
ngag rgyud (87–89), which comprise one section (skabs). The text distin-
guishes three types of poison (dug) which are each the topic of one of the 
three chapters.
1. Concocted (sbyar ba) poison, meaning that the toxicity is the result 
of a mixture of ingredients that are not necessarily toxic themselves. 
However, we can see that these are essentially mineral, animal or veg-
etable substances that at least have the reputation of being toxic. […]
2. Substances that have become toxic (gyur ba’i dug): these are gener-
ally foods or medicines that cause intoxication through their combina-
tion of incompatible components, or meat that has become poisonous as 
a result of being rotten or accidentally contaminated by a poison.
3. Poisons in the strict sense (rang bzhin dug), which are poisonous by 
their nature (such as aconite) without having acquired these toxic prop-
erties in a preparation intended to create toxicity, or through inappro-
priate association with one or more substances. These toxic substances 
have their origin in the myth of the churning of the ocean. A distinction 
is made between “fixed” poisons deriving from the inanimate world (mi 
rgyu ba: metals, minerals and plants) and those that come from the ani-
mate world (rgyu ba, animals).
The three chapters of the Man ngag rgyud dedicated to poisons and 
venoms constitute a very sophisticated development, as much with 
23		
Meyer 1988, 35. In the Carakasaṃhitā, one of the main sources for Āyurveda, it forms the 
fourth branch, while in the rGyud bzhi it is the sixth (ibid., 35).

respect to the classification and description of the different types of 
poisoning, as by a very fine semiology and the variety of the treatments 
offered in relation to these conditions. There is of course a certain influ-
ence of Indian medicine as far as the basic concepts are concerned (the 
three humours, the body tissues, the distinction between “mobile” and 
“fixed” poisons, the idea of “concocted poison” and so forth), but essen-
tially the rGyud bzhi is structured in a far more sophisticated and sci-
entific way than the classic Āyurvedic treatises. The section of the Man 
ngag rgyud dedicated to poison is also very different from the chapter on 
poisons that is in Vagbhata’s Aṣṭāṅgahṛdayasaṃhitā, as well as its auto-
commentary and a commentary by Candranandana that appears in the 
bsTan ’gyur.24
The opening passage of the rGyud bzhi’s section on toxicology is worth closer 
examination at this point. The introduction sets out a number of the basic 
principles concerning the constitution, transmission and effects of poison of 
which Mardzong A contains a number of echos. After some preliminary re-
marks, the 87th chapter of the third volume of the rGyud bzhi, the Man ngag 
rgyud, introduces the topic of poison as follows.

At the end of this age of 500 years, a wide range of wicked acts are per-
formed due to the degeneracy of the times. This era will see the prolif-
eration of evils performed by malicious heretics, and the scissors of 
poison will be applied to the life-force of humans. There are six aspects 
to poison: the categories of poison; the way in which they enter the sys-
tem; the types of vectors; the methods of treating poisoning; counteract-
ing the harmful effects of the treatment; stopping the symptoms. First, 
there are five different kinds of vectors whereby the types of poison are

There are various lands, materials, types of intoxications, and a time 
factor. The lands in question are as follows: India, China, Dolpo and the 
southern borderlands (Mon), which [respectively] use weapons, stakes, 
hos and juices, and each of these comprises 1004 types of preparations. 
The six groups of substances are the precious [metals], minerals, animal 
products, juices, substances that are inherently poisonous and plants.
Although this is not explicitly stated in the main text, mKhan chen Khro ru’s 
commentary specifies that each of these devices corresponds to one of the 
regions that are named.27 The meaning of the term hos is not clear. In a Bonpo 
context the term hos ru denotes a staff, which would be consistent with the 
two objects—weapons and stakes—that precede it. Interestingly, although 
Mardzong A cites India, China and Mon as lands where poisons are made, it 
makes no mention of Dolpo. While the reason for this omission must remain 
a matter of speculation, we may surmise that if the text itself was composed 
in or near Dolpo (in Mustang, for example), advertising one’s own country 
or one’s closest neigbour as a land of poisoners may have seemed unwise or 
undiplomatic.

The precious metals are: gold, copper, brass, iron, mercury and lead. Lead 
and mercury29 are especially used in concoctions. The minerals used are: 
haematite, zinc, soapstone, vermilion, sulphur and pyrites. The animal 
products used are: flesh, blood, bile, fur and bone. The sources of these 
animal products include goats, birds, dogs, weasels, blister beetles,30 
frogs, spiders, scorpions and lizards, though it is primarily products from 
goats, dogs, birds and blister beetles that are used. Juices are extractions 
from any poisonous substances. Inherently poisonous substances are 
wolfsbane [and other varieties of aconite]. Plants used in preparations 
are datura and the Himalayan mayapple, hog plum and caltrop, but the 
majority of preparations are made from datura. These have many ingre-
dients, and there are numerous compounds. Since there are a great many 
varieties of these, identifying them is extremely difficult, and administer-
ing the wrong antidote can cause harm.
As we will see, Mardzong A actually contains a recipe for a particularly insidi-
ous poison that produces symptoms resembling a cold, and it is the inappro-
priate treatment applied by the unwitting physician that will bring about his 
patient’s death. Following a brief list of the various types of pathologies that 
can be induced by the poisons, the Man ngag rgyud explains that toxic sub-
stances are also classified according to the length of time they take to have an 
effect, and are accordingly grouped as zhag dug, zla dug and lo dug, poisons

respect the metre.

A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong Collection
that act respectively within a matter of days, months or years. The point of 
entry of each of the five categories of poisons is explained as follows:
’jug tshul zer la bskur ba mdangs la ’jug / rdzi la bskur ba rtsa sgo sna nas 
’jug / rlangs la bskur ba sna sgo ba spu gnyis / snum la bskur ba ba spu’i sgo 
nas ’jug / zas la bskur ba so lam kha nas ’jug /31
The first is transmitted via the [sun’s] rays and enters through the com-
plexion; the second is carried on the breeze, and enters the doors to the 
channels via the nose; the third is borne by vapour and enters through 
the nose and the pores; the fourth is transmitted by oil and enters via the 
pores, while the last is transmitted with food and enters via the mouth.
Our colleague Fernand Meyer concludes that “at first glance, the Mardzong 
manuscript does not have much in common with either the rGyud bzhi or with 
Classical Indian medical sources.”32 While our knowledge of the latter is insuf-
ficient to allow us to comment on this assessment, from our cursory compari-
son of the text with the relevant passages of the rGyud bzhi we would certainly 
agree with the first part of this conclusion. This being said, it seems to be the 
case that the medical and toxicological principles contained in the two works 
belong to a common tradition. While Mardzong A’s discussion of the vectors 
of poison is less detailed than that given in the rGyud bzhi, it does describe 
three of the five contained in the latter, notably transmission through smoke, 
by contact and through ingestion. It may be significant that the two that are 
omitted—transmission by sunbeams and exhalations from the earth—are the 
two least practical methods of administering poison. Mardzong A shares three 
of the four countries identified by the rGyud bzhi as lands of poisoners, and also 
cites many of the same substances—as well as categories of substances—that 
appear in the rGyud bzhi as ingredients in concoctions, while the techniques 
and terminology related to the treatment of intoxication are broadly similar. 
The main difference between the two works is of course that whereas the 
rGyud bzhi deals primarily with the identification of toxic substances and com-
pounds and the treatment of intoxication, the main purpose of Mardzong A is 
to impart instructions in preparing and administering poisons.

4	
Structure and Content of Mardzong A
The text is presented as a discourse by Mahādeva, who may have been cho-
sen as an authority on the subject by association with the well-known Puranic 
story of his drinking the poison that was generated during the churning of the 
ocean. He begins his discourse with the assertion that there are three main cat-
egories of poison: that which is caused by demons, that which arises as a result 
of a disruption of the elements, and that which is manufactured or concocted. 
There are three ways in which poison can be administered. In order of prefer-
ence these are: 1. through smell, 2. by smearing on the skin, and 3. through in-
gestion. The further subdivisions of manufactured poison (sbyar dug) are then 
presented as follows.
1.	
Jewel poison from the southern lands (Mon)
1.1.	
Eight types of poisons from the five precious substances
1.2.	 Twelve types of poisons that are not plants
2.	
Herb and tree poisons from China
2.1.	 Eight types of poisons from trees
2.2.	 Fifty-two poisons from herbs (rtsi)
3.	
Tibetan concocted poison
3.1.	 Eight poisons from living things
3.2.	 Twenty-two poisons without living things
3.3.	 Thirty compounded poisons
This classification is followed by a summary of the general principles for treat-
ing victims: purging, ingesting powders and inducing sweating.
Two things should be emphasised at this point about the title of the work, 
“Liberation from the bonds of the eight precious substances.” First, this title 
strictly refers only to section 1.1., which deals with poisons from the eight pre-
cious substances—all minerals—that are itemised in fol. 2. Secondly, it seems 
to imply that the main purpose is to prescribe cures for anyone who may have 
been poisoned by the substances in question. In fact, this is not so: immedi-
ately after the brief outline about treatment, the text prescribes the first of 
numerous recipes for the manufacture and administering of a particular poi-
son, in this case a compound that will kill its victim slowly by inducing a fatal 
tumour. It also provides instructions for the measures poisoners should adopt 
in order to avoid being harmed during the manufacturing process.
Unfortunately, most of the folios of the text are missing, and the overall 
structure can only be inferred from the extant folios and from the categories 
presented above. The last numbered folio is 10, and there is clearly a signifi-
cant missing portion between that and the next folio which has no number 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 297 of 332 ---
263
A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong Collection
(fol. X). Assuming that the text addresses all the categories in the scheme given 
above, we can obtain an idea of just how much of it might be missing. From 
the available folios, it seems that the work provides instructions for the pro-
duction, use and neutralisation of each of the categories in the order in which 
they are listed in the initial scheme. Fols 3–5 are missing; fol. 6 has sections 
dedicated to lead and brass; fols 7 and 8 are missing, and fols 9 and 10 appear 
to be dealing with non-plant-based mineral poisons other than the eight pre-
cious substances—the topic of section 1.2. Section 2.1., concerning tree poison, 
begins on fol. 10v. The first section to end on fol. X is 3.1., “liberation from the 
bonds of the eight poisons of living things.”
From this, we can conclude that we are missing parts of sections 1.1 and 1.2. 
(fols 3–5, 7–8); most of 2.1. and all of 2.2.; seven-eighths of 3.1 (the section that 
ends on folio Xr concerns the snowcock, which must therefore be the last of 
the eight creatures in this category); all but the first of the twenty-two “poisons 
without living things” subsumed in 3.2., and the entirety of 3.3., the thirty com-
pounded poisons. In short, the folios that are available to us represent prob-
ably no more than a quarter of the entire text, and probably even less.
5	
Features of the Manuscript and Presentation of the Text
The manuscript is written in an early, elegant form of headless script, with 
the number of lines per folio side varying from seven to nine. Fol. 1r is blank 
except for the marginal number (gcig) and a few scribbled writing exercises. 
The absence of a title is unusual, and may perhaps be attributed to the highly 
sensitive nature of the content. The sections of the text are separated by an or-
namental arrangement of the syllables ī thī (in one case written in headed dbu 
can script), usually signifying a “section break,” and probably more common in 
Bonpo than in Buddhist works.
There are a number of archaic codicological features: the “negative” open-
ing ornament (yig mgo) on fol. 1r; the rather frequent use of the superabundant 
’a: btsa’ (fol. 2r, l. 8); gso’ (fol. 9r, ll. 2, 5; fol. 9v, l. 3; fol. 10r, l. 1; fol. 10v, l. 2; fol. Xr, 
l. 6); bse’ (fol. 9v, l. 4); rka’ (fol. 10r, l. 8); ba’ (fol. Xv, l. 3); the frequent use of the 
ra mgo where standard Tibetan would use a prefixed g- or b-, and the use of 
ṇa (the retroflex na, na log) as an abbreviation for med. Contracted forms are 
infrequent, but there are certain other features that make the text difficult to 
read. One of these is the fact that the letter nga is very truncated, making it in-
distinguishable from the intersyllabic tsheg. In certain letters, such as ’a, la, kha 
among others, the first and second descenders are not connected at the top, 
Agnieszka Helman-Ważny and Charles Ramble - 978-90-04-44372-3
Downloaded from Brill.com05/01/2023 09:13:32AM
via Western University

--- Page 298 of 332 ---
264
Chapter 11
with the result that the first descender may easily be mistaken for nga or part 
of an ’a. These difficulties are compounded by errors on the part of the scribe, 
who sometimes adds horizontal connections between descenders where there 
should not be any.
In the transliterated text, irregular spellings have not usually been amended, 
except in cases where it may not be clear how a translation was arrived at. 
Amendations are presented in round brackets (xyz) immediately after the syl-
lable concerned. Square brackets [xyz] indicate material that has been pro-
posed because it is missing or illegible due to damage. Text in braces {xyz} 
indicates material that should be omitted for a better reading, while text in 
strikethrough between braces {xyz} signfies intentional deletion. Contracted 
forms have been written out in full, without amended orthography (for exam-
ple, the contraction kor is presented as kar po, not dkar po).
For the identification of plants and other substances we have used a lim-
ited number of dictionaries and other sources. In keeping with conventions for 
dictionary references, page numbers are not given and short titles are used, as 
follows: Bod sman: Bod sman gyi rnam bshad 1990, 1–18, a list of materia med-
ica. Goldstein: Goldstein 2001; Meyer: Meyer 1988, 173–81, the list of materia 
medica. THL: Tibetan and Himalayan Library Online Translation Tool, which 
subsumes a number of dictionaries. Full references to these works are given in 
the bibiliography.

’phra men no / rin po che’i dug la brgyad / rtsi ma yin pa’i dug la bcu 2 / gsal

In the language of India: Ratna-bhanda-mocana [nā]ma. In Tibetan: The so-
called Tantra of the Liberation from the Bonds of the Precious Items. Homage 
to Mahādeva. Uma, the goddess of Brahma’s Veda, appeared in all her glory. 
Maheśvara was filled with joy. He rolled his mind into a crystal ball, [expelled it 
from the vase of his throat],33 spread it over his tongue and enunciated it from 
between his conch-like teeth. Mahādeva said as follows. There are [illegible] 
varieties of poison: the poison caused by harmful gdon demons; the poison 
caused by disturbed elements, and concocted poison. There are three [of the 
latter]: the most ideal operates through smell; second-best works by smearing, 
and the least ideal involves ingestion. Poison that acts through smelling and 
smearing is called “white plant poison.” The least ideal one, involving inges-
tion, is of three kinds: jewel-poison of the southern borderlands; Chinese plant 
poison, and Tibetan concocted poison, which comprises poison with living 
things and poison without living things. The Chinese plant poison is of two 
sorts, from plants and from trees. The jewel poison of the southern border-
lands consists of poison of the five precious substances and poison without 
plants. There are two types of Tibetan concocted poison: compounds with liv-
ing things, and compounds without living things.34 There are eight types of 
poison from precious items, and twelve of poisons that are not plants. There 
are eight types of poison from trees35 and fifty-two varieties of poison from 
smaller plants.

There are eight types of poison from living things, and twenty-two without 
living things. There are exactly thirty major groups of compounds. According 
to the teaching in eighty chapters,36 generally, which therapeutic antagonists37 
treat which poisons, and which [antagonists] should be administered for 
which [poisons]: for an intensive treatment one should use purges with 
liquid,38 for milder treatment the consumption of powders. For inducing 
sweats, one should use a decoction.39 Conditions should be treated forcefully 
so that there is no relapse. The body’s elements should be allowed to recover 
their proper balance. To liberate [the patient] from obstructions and the ad-
verse effects of treatment, as far as the eight poisons of the precious substances 
are concerned there are: the administration of [a base of] transformed gold; 
the administration of [bases of] copper, iron, silver, lead (zho for zha nye), 
brass, conch, coral or crystal that have been transformed. Magnetite, burned 
goat horn, burned sheep horn, flax and sesame40 kept in a copper container 
and administered with chang. If [the victim] is a woman, a large bloody tumor 
36		
We do not know which work this might refer to.
37		
gnyen po, antagonist or therapeutic antagonist. The common translation of gnyen po as 
“antidote” would be inappropriate here, since many of the ingredients of the poisonous 
compounds are not themselves toxic, and we have therefore followed Meyer’s rendering 
of the term as “antagoniste thérapeutique” (Meyer 1988, 105, 138, 181).
38		
Purging (bshal) is one of five procedures (las lnga) recognised in Tibetan medicine for 
evacuation (sbyang; Meyer 1988, 181–182).
39		
Decoctions (thang) and powders (bye ma) are two procedures used for “pacifying” (zhi) 
illnesses in Tibetan medicine (ibid., 182).
40		
ting for til, sesame. This reading is justified by the mention of sesame in the following sec-
tion, where the list of therapeutic antagonists is given.

with the powder of conch-shell that has been ground in whey.

The purgative should be made as follows: it should be mainly sal ammoniac, 
and to this add a variety of other salts, and boil it in a clay pot. Add it to a 
suspension of earth and containing potsherds and administer it. Perform the 
washing as much as you wish. If you wish to stop [the effects of the purge], you 
can stop it with gravy made from fresh meat. [However], the purification of the 
channels is not necessary, because [the poison] is located in the hollow viscera 
(i.e. the digestive tract). To counteract the effects of the treatment:47 make a 
broth of boiled blood and silver, and if you can find the gall of a horse or a don-
key that is best, and if you cannot, then whatever bile you can find will do. Mix 
it with the brains of a cow and administer it. Later on, you should administer 
it as a liquid mixture with the five principal medicines.48 Here ends the libera-
tion from the bonds of the precious substance that is lead.49
Brass filings, the yolk of a duck egg, and powdered dried horse liver—if 
you mix these and put them in boiled meat, it will produce in the stomach a 
tumour resulting from indigestion. The sense of taste will be lost,50 leaving a 
taste of verdigris. Just when the vision is obscured, this is the moment to apply 
the treatment, but if the patient so much as vomits bile, it cannot be under-
taken. The treatment is as follows: the antagonist to brass is brick tea. The an-
tagonist for the yolk of a duck egg is alcohol.51 The antagonist for horse liver is 
cow liver. Add this to a broth of rock salt and administer it. Regarding powders 
that are to be ingested:
47		
log non for log gnon. This refers to a treatment that is applied to prevent the harmful sec-
ondary effects of a medication: “Les indications et contre-indications sont données pour 
les médicaments évacuateurs ainsi que leurs effets ’pervers’ et la manière de les maitriser 
(log-gnon)” (Meyer 1988, 182).
48		
rtsa ba lnga. These are the five principal medicines: ra mnye (Solomon’s seal, Polygonatum 
spp.); nye shing (asparagus, Asparagus spp.), lca ba (angelica, Angelica spp. or milk pars-
ley, Selinium spp.), gze ma (caltrop, Tribulus terrestris) (Meyer, THL).
49

’bro long med par: ’bro long (for bro longs), lit. “taste blindness,” denotes ageusia, the loss 
of the gustatory sense. The wording of the phrase is contradictory; better readings would 
be either ’bro long yod par (“experiencing ageusia”) or ’bro ba med par (“without the sense 
of taste”).

skyur, literally “sour,” is a common epithet for alcohol.

take borax, lousewort,52 cardamom, the bya bra of liver, then make a base of 
sugar and administer it. As regards the hot broth: ferns, musk, cattle brains, 
and blood of an argali: make a broth of this and administer it. As regards the 
washing, it accords with the washing procedure for the precious substance 
copper.53 The channels should not be cleansed. For counteracting the side-
effects of the treatment: white beg kan;54 trona;55 rock salt; tsi ni mi; vermilion 
powder—make a thick paste56 from these with white butter and administer it. 
Later, make a medicinal butter with bitumen and administer that. Here ends 
the liberation from the bonds of the precious substance brass.

respectively two, one and two species. All belong to the Pedicularis (lousewort) genus 
(Bod sman, 362–366).

65		
sla ba for gla ba. The term appears elsewhere in the text as bla ba. Strictly speaking, gla 
ba denotes the musk deer itself, while the musk that the male secretes is usually called 
gla rtsi.
66		
shu dag, sweet flag or calamus (Acorus calamus).
67		
rgu rus for rgu drus. “Crested lark” in this case denotes the plant Corydalis dasyptera, not 
the bird of the same name. Chiraita (tig ta) is the usual name in Nepal for Swertia chiray-

mdze tsha for mdze tshwa (Goldstein). Mirabilite, also known as Glauber’s salt, is a

The term given here is sba rgod, which might refer to wild reed bamboo. However, a few 
lines later the plant appears as lca rgod, wild angelica or milk parsley. Since angelica is 
quite closely related to the three candidates for its therapeutic antagonist, and this is 
probably a preferable identification to bamboo.

bye’u mgo kar. But note that, for byi’u mgo, “bird’s head,” Meyer gives “rhynchonelle”,

72		
Identified by Meyer as Cnidium dahuricum or Peucedanum sp. The Cnidium genus in-
cludes various species of parsley as well as hemlock. The Peucedanum genus includes 
P. officinale, commonly known as hog’s fennel or sulphurweed. For tang kun—which 
we take to be a variant spelling—Bod sman gives three species: Peucedanum violaceum, 
Angelica sinensis and Pleurospermum franchetianum. Angelica sinensis is female ginseng. 
The genus Pleurospermum, for which there seems to be no common English name, be-
longs to the same family (Apiaceae) as the other two.
73		
bse. The term bse may refer to several different things, but in the present context leather 
is the most likely meaning.

End of section. This is how one should make red zinc.80 Take zinc, the ash of 
dres ma grass,81 the dung of a brown bear and the fur of an antelope. If you add

with a variety of stones. The treatment for this: take sal ammoniac, musk, pig’s

The eight wood poisons of Mahādeva. The following are the eight poi-
sons from plants and trees. The foremost of these are poisons from bse zho, 
ground-cone83 which causes vomiting, and pu ka ra li, which causes shoot-
ing pains. For washing, there is dong kra poison84 and for purification there is 
croton;85 for causing death, there is sru poison; the Chinese lacquer tree is the 
poison of harmful people (or creatures?); gum86 made from burned laburnum 
(or bayur), the fat from a black snake, the flesh of the chick of a blue-winged 
water bird, black sulphur, and the powder of elongated (nur mo) yob mo.87 Let 
it decompose in the hide of a cow or a horse and dry it. Make it in the form of 
autumn cattle dung and leave it in [the victim’s] house; contact with the smoke 
will cause a leprosy-like skin disease.
As regards the treatment [for this kind of poisoning]: homage to Maheśvar! 
Anyone who is affected by the smoke of the eight poisons from varieties of 
shrubs will develop a chronic leprosy-like skin ailment.

genus, including A. capillaris. Among the species identified for yog mo in THL are phur

lated high-altitude thorny shrub.

Treatment with purgatives and moxibustion are inadvisable. All kinds of sleep-
lessness are caused by moth poison. If you add a moth cocoon, prickly ash 
seeds88 and the flesh of [a person or animal] that has been killed by cinnabar, 
the upper part of the [victim’s] body will feel uncomfortable, and he or she 
will experience severe weight-loss and hiccups. The treatment is as follows: 
the antagonist to moth cocoon is partridge [egg]; the antagonist to prickly ash 
is iron; the antagonist to cinnabar is magnetite. Add these to a solution of soda 
and administer it. Mix the blood of a grey dog, burned goat horn and burned 
sheep horn with butter and administer it as a powder to be ingested. For the 
hot broth: [mix] rock salt, li ga nur,89 Nepalese hog plum,90 and administer 
this. To counteract the secondary effects of the medication: mix snowcock egg, 
the excrement91 of a dead puppy and of a dead female yak with any appropri-
ate oil and administer it. Here ends the liberation from the bonds of [poison 
from] moth cocoons. End of section.

ro tsan has been read as ro tshan, a medical term for the excrement of dead humans,

dead blister beetles; the flesh of an old man who has died from an alcohol-
related illness; dead moths; the deposits from the sleeping place of an elderly 
person who is suffering from an epidemic; a dead weasel; horse liver; hare’s 
flesh; marmot flesh; the flesh of a frog; a dead striped bee; blood from a dead 
chough; the fur of an antelope that has been killed with a weapon; the flesh 
and blood of a person who has died from poisoning; the intestines of a cow 
that has died from poisoning; the brains of a dog that has died from rabies, and 
the five types of poison from flesh—there are twenty-two in all.92 First, the 
blister beetle: if you add seven blister beetles to trona and milk, and mix this 
92		
In fact the list adds up to just twenty, unless one counts the “flesh and blood of a person 
who has died from poisoning” as two of the items, in which case there are twenty-one.

A Rare Treatise on Toxicology from the Mardzong Collection
with bear bile, [the victim] will be seized by an illness that will cause his gall 
bladder to rupture. Some people will maintain that this is the [consequence of 
an excess of the] wind element. The treatment for this is as follows: for the an-
tagonist to blister beetle, administer a mixture of human bile and the six won-
derful medicaments. [Take] soapstone,93 bitumen, kutki,94 fumitory,95 saffron, 
hu lu ka,96 li ga dur,97 chiraita, magnolia, sickle senna,98 and make a hot broth 
from these. Administer borax and cinnabar repeatedly. As soon as there are 
indications that the blister beetle has been counteracted, the channels should 
be cleansed [by means of a laxative] and also by inducing vomiting [as much 
as the patient] can stand. Later, prepare a medicinal butter with gentian. Here 
ends the liberation from the bonds of the blister beetle.
93		
cong zi, presumably for cong zhi.
94		
hong len. Picrorhiza scrophulariaefolia (Meyer), widely known by its Nepali name, kutki.
95		
sgra bzang, cited in THL as an alternative spelling of skra bzang, which is identified with 
parpata. Parpata, which is used in Āyurvedic medicine, may refer to a number of plants 
including Fumaria parviflora, Indian fumitory. THL further suggests that skra bzang may 
be Corydalis sp., crested lark, which appears elsewhere in the present text as rgu rus (for 
rgu drus).
96		
THL suggests that hu lu ka may be another name for brag skya ha bo or for pri yang ku. 
The former is identified by Meyer as Corollodiscus kingianus and the latter as dragonhead 
(Dracocephalus tanguticum).
97		
According to THL, li ga dur is an alternative form of ga dur, for which Bod sman has two 
candidates: Rhodiola kirilowii and Bergeria purpurascens.
98		
tha skar rdo rje for thal ka rdo rje, sickle senna (Cassia tora; Meyer).
